The two inference phases stress hardware differently: prompt prefill is parallel and typically compute-bound, whereas autoregressive decode is sequential and often memory-bandwidth-bound. Conventional width or depth scaling increases both costs together because every added layer is evaluated in both phases. We ask whether additional learned computation can instead be allocated to continuation prediction while preserving the prompt-wide primary computation and a single persistent key-value (KV) cache. We introduce the Dual-Flow Transformer. Its primary flow is a complete causal language model that processes the prompt and writes the KV cache. The auxiliary flow is omitted during prompt processing and activated only from the final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state or influencing the primary flow. The two flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices, while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. Sharing weights and the primary cache also creates opportunities to reuse loaded weights and cached keys and values during grouped execution. Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation makes primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. We study two regimes: increasing decode computation at fixed prefill expert computation, and reallocating a fixed decode expert budget between the two flows. These experiments expose a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate the potential of phase-specific expert allocation.

</details>


### [100] [Learning to Adapt Cross-Domain Preferences via Meta-LoRA for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389)
*Xuefei Wang,Jun Han,Zixuan Wang,Qingkai Zeng,Xiao Wang,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA framework for cross-domain personalization that learns a meta-initialization for LoRA adapters and adapts strength to evidence quality, decoupling user and domain priors via interpretable prompts and domain-soft tokens; shows robust improvements across benchmarks and notably strong gains on HiCUPID for both transfer and unseen-user cold-start scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-domain personalization with zero- or few-shot evidence is prone to overfitting when data is sparse and prone to negative transfer when source-domain artifacts are misaligned with the target domain. Existing methods struggle to calibrate adaptation magnitude and to separate user preferences from domain artifacts.

Method: Propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA: use a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both adaptation start and prior center; regulate update strength by support-set size and predictive uncertainty; decompose personalization priors into a human-readable user prompt and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-conditioned hidden representations.

Result: Empirical results show consistent gains against strong baselines across several personalization benchmarks. On HiCUPID, cross-domain win-rate degradation is reduced by 47.9% relative to the best baseline, and unseen-user cold-start win rate improves by 110.2%.

Conclusion: Calibrated, uncertainty-aware adaptation with a priors decomposition improves cross-domain personalization, enabling stronger personalization as evidence grows while limiting overfitting under sparse evidence; the user/domain priors separation aids stability and transfer reliability across tasks.

Abstract: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization aims to generate user-preferred responses in unseen conversational domains from only a handful of target-domain interactions. Existing adaptation methods struggle to calibrate update magnitude under sparse evidence and thus overfit, whereas history-transfer methods often entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, yielding unreliable personalization priors and negative transfer. To calibrate adaptation to evidence quality, we propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, which uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, while adjusting update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty. This limits overfitting under sparse or ambiguous evidence while permitting stronger personalization as evidence grows. Controlled adaptation alone does not determine which preferences should transfer across domains or how they should be expressed. We therefore functionally decompose personalization priors into user and domain components, using a human-readable prompt for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks show consistent gains over strong baselines. On HiCUPID, our method reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start.

</details>


### [101] [Research Assistant: AstraZeneca's Agentic System for R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12395)
*Piotr Grabowski,Mohamed Alameen,Jorge Bretones,Sabina Cardell,Miguel Carmona,Gavin Edwards,Ben Grainger,Sameh Hassan,Erik Jansson,Artur Kuziakhmetov,Albert Maristany,Hebatallah Mohamed,Andriy Nikolov,Sebastian Nilsson,Mark O'Donoghue,James Pacileo,Ashiq Sultan,Alex Voegele,Michael Ughetto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AstraZeneca internal LLM-based Research Assistant integrates diverse biomedical data sources via a chat interface to support fast Q&A and multi-step research, with provenance and source-linking; the note covers architecture, design choices, and deployment lessons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empower scientists and clinicians to explore biomedical questions across heterogeneous data sources with grounded, traceable responses, enhancing efficiency in day-to-day R&D.

Method: Architecture combining retrieval-augmented generation across literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal systems; dual interaction modes (fast QA and multi-step tasks); evidence grounding and source linking; deployment at scale.

Result: The note presents the system architecture, core design choices, and deployment experience, demonstrating how the integrated platform supports R&D workflows; emphasizes evidence grounding and source traceability.

Conclusion: Lessons learned from large-scale deployment, with insights into benefits, trade-offs, and future improvements for enterprise LLM-assisted biomedical research.

Abstract: We describe Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based system developed at AstraZeneca to help scientists and clinicians explore biomedical questions across a broad range of data sources. The system provides a chat-style interface that brings together evidence from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. It supports both a fast mode for direct question answering and a multi-step mode for more complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence and linked back to the original sources, allowing users to review and further explore the underlying data. In this technical note, we outline the system architecture, the main design choices behind the product, and lessons learned from deploying it at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows across AstraZeneca.

</details>


### [102] [Large Language Models Can Follow Instructions, But Not Many at Once: Phase Transitions in Compositional Constraint Satisfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12426)
*Mariya I. Vasileva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE) benchmark systematically probes how accuracy degrades as the number of simultaneous constraints increases, revealing predictable per-constraint decay, non-uniform constraint degradation (structural vs lexical), near-independent failures, and breakdown past about 5-6 constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize compositional constraint satisfaction in large language models, quantify degradation, and identify factors that govern collapse and potential mitigation; prior work treats constraints individually but not jointly.

Method: Procedurally generated benchmark varying k (1-12), 36 constraint types, deterministic rule-based verifiers, zero LLM-judge involvement; 15 models; 369,753 checks.

Result: Per-constraint pass rate decays gradually; probability of passing all k constraints collapses (e.g., 41% per-constraint pass at k=8; all eight only 5.7%). Structural constraints degrade ~2x more per added constraint than lexical; failures nearly independent; residual coupling tracks shared output features; wrong sentence count triggers all constraints that read it; reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 constraints; 50% probe-level success at 7 constraints for strongest model; 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

Conclusion: Demonstrates fragility of multi-constraint instruction following and shows that constraint type and compositional structure matter; suggests a need for strategies to improve robustness under constraint saturation.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in settings that require simultaneous adherence to multiple explicit constraints - reasoning structure, safety boundaries, output schemas. Individual constraints are handled proficiently, but the compositional regime, where many must hold jointly, remains poorly characterized: how rapidly does performance degrade, what governs the degradation, and can the collapse be mitigated? We introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark that systematically varies the number of simultaneous constraints (k), with every constraint scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier and zero LLM-judge involvement: 15 models, 36 constraint types, 369,753 checks at k=1-12. Three findings emerge. First, per-constraint pass rate decays gradually and predictably, while the chance of satisfying all k constraints collapses - a model passing individual constraints at ~41% at k=8 succeeds on all eight just 5.7% of the time. Second, constraints do not degrade equally: structural constraints lose 2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, ordered by a comprehension-maintenance gap that separates constraints requiring sustained tracking from binary decisions immune to composition. Third, failures are nearly independent, which is what makes the accumulation multiplicative; the residual coupling that does exist tracks shared output features rather than pairwise interference - a wrong sentence count fails every constraint that reads it. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints: probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for the strongest model, and at 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

</details>


### [103] [MindMemOS: A Portable and Self-Evolving Memory Operating Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12428)
*Kaichao Liang,Yuqi Cui,Hao Kong,Xinyuan Huang,Guohaotian Hou,Qingcan Kang,Liang Chen,Yiyang Yin,Ke Ye,Jiaquan Guo,Da Chen,Lingan Zeng,Yixing Peng,Rong Yao,Shixiong Kai,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindMemOS is a portable, self-evolving memory layer for AI agents that uses a unified entity property timestructure to enable scenario-adaptive memory modeling, autonomous memory refinement, and progressively refined skills. It introduces MindMemEvolve with validation-driven evolutionary search, dreaming consolidation, and implicit corrective feedback, plus MindSkillEvolve to convert trajectories into reusable skills. Demonstrates high accuracy on LOCOMO (94.03%) and PersonaMem (70.63%), with +9.2 points on SpreadsheetBench over baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI memory systems are largely fixed after development and cannot adapt their memory schemas, organization strategies, or procedural knowledge through continued use; a need for continual adaptation in open-world settings.

Method: Architectural framework MindMemOS with a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemEvolve performs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios. Dreaming consolidates memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. Implicit corrective feedback provides a human-in-the-loop signal for revision. MindSkillEvolve transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable, refined skills.

Result: Demonstrated high task performance: LOCOMO 94.03% accuracy; PersonaMem 70.63% accuracy. MindSkillEvolve boosts SpreadsheetBench by 9.2 percentage points over initial baseline.

Conclusion: MindMemOS enables scenario-adaptive memory modeling, continuous memory refinement, and progressive skill evolution in open-world agents, validated by quantitative gains across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Memory is a core component of AI agents, enabling them to accumulate experience, maintain personalization, and adapt over long-term interactions. However, existing memory systems often remain fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt their memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. We present MindMemOS, a portable and self-evolving memory operating layer that organizes open-world information using a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemOS supports scenario-adaptive memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, autonomous memory refinement, and continuous skill evolution. Its MindMemEvolve algorithm employs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios, whiledreaming consolidates accumulated memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. In addition, implicit corrective feedback serves as a human-in-the-loop signal for identifying and revising potentially inaccurate or misaligned memories. Its MindSkillEvolve algorithm further transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable and progressively refined skills. MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

</details>


### [104] [Governed Persistent Memory: Source-Bound State Semantics and Fail-Closed Release for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12476)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPM introduces a governed, auditable, bitemporal memory with source-bound admission and five executable clauses to ensure integrity, non-revival, and exact closure, validated via extensive benchmarks and end-to-end tests showing deterministic governance and bounded improvements over ungoverned baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term memory must handle contradictory, superseded, retracted, or deleted records and provide auditable, verifiable release semantics. The work aims to prevent leakage of stale or conflicting claims and ensure controlled release of information.

Method: Proposes Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), a bitemporal state-transition model with source-binding, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses enforce ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure. Evaluations include a prespecified 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, a sealed end-to-end service test across eight query families, and a finite-model/state-space analysis (331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states), plus a 100,000-trace three-engine differential.

Result: On ReleaseBench, the strongest simple policy matches 1,800/3,600; unmatched releases occur in 50% of violations. In the publicly disclosed V3 arm, governed results are correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for the ungoverned Qwen2.5-7B; repairs of 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal achieves 2,400/2,400 per arm across Chinese- and English-command arms. Finite-model exploration yields no full-contract counterexample among 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states; a 100,000-trace differential shows zero mismatches.

Conclusion: These results are bounded contract/implementation evaluations, not open-world model accuracy or world truth. Governed answers in the sealed evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B baseline is an ungovnered comparator, not evidence that the underlying language model is perfectly accurate. The work supports that governance can provide reliable, auditable memory releases within bounded contexts and highlights limitations of extrapolating to open-world settings.

Abstract: Long-term agent memory is usually treated as select--store--retrieve, but retrieval does not decide whether contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records may support an outgoing claim. We introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses cover ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view at one verified head.
  On a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest of three intentionally simple complete policies matches 1,800/3,600 and makes unmatched releases on 50% of violation cases. A separate sealed end-to-end service evaluation exercises real ingestion and release across eight query families. In its publicly disclosed V3 arm, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned local Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal over Chinese- and English-command arms, with generation-date pinning and no post-freeze reducer amendment, again obtains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A production-code-independent finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without a full-contract counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.
  These are bounded contract and implementation results, not open-world model accuracy or evidence of world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B result is the ungoverned comparison, not a claim that a language model itself became perfectly accurate.

</details>


### [105] [$\varepsilon$-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522)
*Aofan Liu,Shiyuan Song,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces epsilon-MemEvo, a cross-task knowledge transfer framework for LLM-based program evolution that stores task-agnostic tactic memories as natural-language summaries and uses an adaptive injection gate to control memory reuse, achieving safe, low-overhead improvements across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM program-evolution systems like FunSearch/AlphaEvolve optimize tasks in isolation and lose search experience after completion; there is a need for cross-task transfer of experience while avoiding negative transfer.

Method: Store compact natural-language tactic memories of successful algorithms; retrieve and adaptively inject memories with a gating mechanism that decides when and how strongly to inject; evaluate with a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol on 8 benchmarks using the GPT-5 backbone.

Result: On 8 diverse tasks, AUCC improves by +8.7% relative to AdaEvolve; early-stage convergence improves by +9.4% on average; naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across ablations; data-updated posterior is interpretable (favoring skip during improving search, shifting from skip to hint across plateaus); computational overhead is under 1%.

Conclusion: Cross-task transfer of tactic memories via an adaptive gating mechanism enables safer, interpretable, and low-overhead knowledge reuse in LLM program evolution, yielding consistent gains across tasks and mitigating negative transfer.

Abstract: LLM-based program evolution systems such as FunSearch and AlphaEvolve have shown strong ability to discover novel algorithms, but typically optimize each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. We introduce $\varepsilon$-MemEvo, a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution. $\varepsilon$-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories: compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. To avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo uses an adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected, and at what intensity. We evaluate $\varepsilon$-MemEvo on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks spanning mathematical optimization and systems engineering, using a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol that excludes target-task memory entries. On the primary GPT-5 backbone, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks, with a mean relative gain of +8.7%, and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. Ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The data-updated posterior is interpretable in observed states: it favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across early and late plateaus. These gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

</details>


### [106] [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555)
*Michael Georgiades,Charalambia Varnava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAS provides local-global causal explanations by framing interventions as an interventional coalition game and distributing outcomes via causal Shapley contributions, yielding Local/Signed Local/Global CAS that separate outcome prediction from causal effect heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish predictive explanations from explanations of how heterogeneity in treatment effects arises, and to provide a targetable, intervention-aware causal reporting layer beyond standard predictive SHAP.

Method: Construct an interventional coalition game and allocate joint intervention contrast using causal Shapley contributions. Derive Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two Global CAS summaries from outcome-scale effects, enabling a local-to-global, intervention-targeted causal reporting framework.

Result: In a known-truth benchmark, mean Local CAS MAE=0.107 (coalition-aware CAS) vs 0.173 (one-at-a-time normalisation) and 0.213 (global ATE). The advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation grows from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On two empirical DoubleML datasets (401(k) eligibility/net financial assets; Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings diverge meaningfully from Feature-CAS treatment-effect modifier rankings; e.g., dep1 moves from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and is the leading local Feature-CAS modifier.

Conclusion: Separating what predicts an outcome from what explains causal-effect heterogeneity yields added value; CAS provides an explicit intervention target and local-to-global reporting that complements predictive explanations with causal interpretability.

Abstract: Predictive explanation methods attribute a model output; they do not, by themselves, attribute an intervention effect on the real-world outcome. We introduce the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a compact score architecture for causal explanation. CAS starts from an identified interventional coalition game, allocates the joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts those raw outcome-scale effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two complementary Global CAS summaries. The innovation is not a new Shapley formula, but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target. In the known-truth benchmark, eight repeated primary-interaction simulations (n = 2,200 each, three actions) gave mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 for coalition-aware CAS, compared with 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector. The paired advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation increased from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On both empirical DoubleML datasets, 401(k) eligibility/net financial assets (n = 9,915) and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration (n = 5,099), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers. In Pennsylvania, dep1 (exactly one dependent) moved from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and was the leading local Feature-CAS modifier. These results isolate the added value of separating what predicts the outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect.

</details>


### [107] [Trie Automata for Constrained Decoding over Large Finite Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12574)
*Xingzi Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a trie automaton for constrained decoding over large finite output sets, using Aho-Corasick to precompute per-node masks; yields substantial speedups and enables stateless, high-throughput batch serving with guaranteed validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained decoding from large finite sets currently relies on general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes a bottleneck (cardinality wall) when the valid set grows to thousands or more; a specialized mechanism could mitigate latency and improve batch throughput.

Method: Develop a trie automaton that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) and employ Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. Compare against XGrammar in vLLM/SGLang, assess per-step latency, compilation time, and end-to-end throughput across multiple tokenizer vocabularies.

Result: Per-step valid-token computation reduced to 0.65 μs (from 5.8 μs with XGrammar); 2–6.5× faster compilation at K ≥ 300; end-to-end vLLM throughput 219 req/s vs 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (≈29×); maintains sub-100 ms compilation up to K = 10,000 across 32K–262K vocabularies; per-step cost is flat regardless of set size; guarantees 100% output validity.

Conclusion: The trie automaton delivers major acceleration for constrained decoding with large finite outputs, enabling scalable, high-throughput serving and 100% validity, with broad applicability across tokenizer families and deployment contexts.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly need to generate structured outputs that conform to predefined schemas, with one common constraint being selection from a finite set of valid strings. Current constrained decoding systems handle this through general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes prohibitively slow as the number of valid values grows into the thousands, a cardinality wall. We introduce the trie automaton, a specialized mechanism that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. The trie achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, one of the primary backends in vLLM and SGLang, and 2--6.5X faster compilation at K >= 300. Because precomputed masks enable a stateless serving path that bypasses the guided decoding pipeline, this advantage compounds in batch serving: end-to-end vLLM throughput reaches 219 req/s vs. XGrammar's 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29X). The 29X combines the algorithmic speedup with integration-path savings that only precomputed masks can unlock. Across seven tokenizer families (32K--262K vocabulary), the trie maintains sub-100ms compilation up to K = 10,000 and flat per-step cost regardless of set size, while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

</details>


### [108] [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus for Evaluating Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585)
*Congchao Wang,Diwakar Singh,Qiaozi Gao,Spyros Matsoukas,Yang Liu,Mahdi Namazifar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reasoning Jury replaces a single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderator-driven consensus to better identify defects in long reasoning traces. It outperforms frontier models in defect detection at substantially lower cost, enabling richer failure-mode analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve the quality of reasoning data curation, reinforce training signals through better defect identification, and surface reasoning mistakes for runtime feedback. Single-model judges (even frontier models) struggle with long traces and guardrails limit online use of frontier models.

Method: A jury of open-weight LLMs (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) deliberates under a moderator who critiques judgments, allows jurors to modify votes, and derives consensus via deliberation or consolidation. Compared against frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, gemini-3.1-pro) for identifying reasoning defects. Cost is tracked across initial verdicts, deliberations, and consolidation.

Result: The Reasoning Jury significantly outperforms frontier models in correctly identifying reasoning defects and does so at a fraction (8-15%) of the cost of running frontier-model judges. The approach also enables deeper understanding of model failure modes on benchmarks.

Conclusion: A moderated jury of open-weight LLMs yields higher fidelity judgments for long-ray reasoning defects, reduces cost, and enhances interpretability for data curation, training, and runtime feedback, with promising potential for broader analysis of model performance.

Abstract: Improving reasoning LLMs requires the ability to judge the quality of long reasoning traces for effective reasoning data curation, strong training signals during reinforcement learning, and an in-depth understanding of reasoning behaviors during model performance evaluation. Additionally, surfacing reasoning mistakes that the model makes would enable improving the model's performance at runtime through providing feedback. Due to the difficulty of this complex task on long reasoning traces, single-model judges (even frontier models) do not do well at identifying reasoning defects. Additionally, leveraging frontier models during online training of reasoning LLMs is generally prohibited due to guardrails in terms of use. In this work, we introduce Reasoning Jury, a system that replaces the single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderated consensus mechanism, to improve the fidelity of judgments for identifying reasoning defects. In reasoning jury, defects of a reasoning trace and their severity are surfaced through a deliberation where a moderator conducts a discussion amongst the jury where the jurors critique each other's judgments and get to modify their initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus through deliberation amongst jurors or consolidation of judgements. We show that Reasoning Jury with a jury of open-weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) is able to significantly outperform frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects. Besides accuracy performance improvements, the aggregated cost of the jury (initial verdicts, deliberations, consolidation, etc.) is a fraction (8 to 15%) of the cost of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. We also show how these judgements can be leveraged to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, which allows much deeper understanding of a model's performance.

</details>


### [109] [Auditable agentic AI for evidence-grounded thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12590)
*Haifan Gong,Shiyu Chen,Bodong Wang,Yuqi Wang,Shijie Wang,Guoliang You,Xinyu Xiong,Haowei Wang,Mingzhi Mao,Dexing Kong,Qinghua Liu,Wei Lou,Fei Chen,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThyroidXAgent is an auditable, clinician-interactive AI system that coordinates thyroid ultrasound tasks across multiple centers, achieving high segmentation and classification performance, improving reporting consistency and efficiency, and introducing a lesion-level ThyClinScore.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation in thyroid ultrasound AI by integrating localization, measurement, risk stratification, and reporting into a clinician-correctable, auditable workflow across diverse datasets.

Method: Developed on OpenThyroidDB (≈0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports). An agent coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores outputs as a case-level evidence record. Evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres (NHC-MISD-TUS cohort). Compared against multimodal language-model baselines for report generation. Tasks included nodule segmentation, benign/malignant classification, lymph-node metastasis prediction, and follicular vs papillary carcinoma classification. Introduced ThyClinScore as a lesion-level clinical semantic metric.

Result: Mean Dice for segmentation: 87.21%. AUROC for benign–malignant: 0.9466. AUROC for lymph-node metastasis: 0.864. AUROC for follicular vs papillary thyroid carcinoma: 0.805. Evidence-grounded report assembly outperformed LM baselines. ThyClinScore showed strongest correlation with location-aware language-model judge. Physician classification accuracy improved; report diagnostic consistency rose from 70.3% to 86.2%; segmentation and reporting time reduced by 35.9% and 27.4%, respectively.

Conclusion: Auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI can support thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting across heterogeneous datasets, enabling integrated, workflow-friendly decision support.

Abstract: Thyroid ultrasound diagnosis requires coordinated lesion localization, measurement, risk stratification and reporting, yet most AI systems address these tasks in isolation and provide limited support for clinical review. We present ThyroidXAgent, a clinician-interactive agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores their outputs as an auditable case-level evidence record. The system was developed using OpenThyroidDB, a multicentre, multitask resource integrating approximately 0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports, and was evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the private NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent achieved a mean Dice score of 87.21 percent for nodule segmentation and a mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign-malignant classification. The same workflow supported lymph-node metastasis prediction and follicular versus papillary thyroid carcinoma classification, with AUROCs of 0.864 and 0.805, respectively. For report generation, evidence-grounded assembly outperformed multimodal language-model baselines across three cohorts. ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric introduced here, showed the strongest correlation with a location-aware language-model judge. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy, increased report diagnostic consistency from 70.3 percent to 86.2 percent, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9 percent and 27.4 percent, respectively. These findings support auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting.

</details>


### [110] [DiG-bench: Discovery in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12593)
*Ruairidh M. Battleday,Kai Sandbrink,Jimi Cullen-Drohan,Zihan Yan,Timothy Muller,Clare Maguire,Ales Kubicek,Fraser Greenlee-Scott,Sukrit Sumant,Tri Dao,Jürgen Schmidhuber,Michal Valko,Joshua Tenenbaum,Thomas L. Griffiths,Zeb Kurth-Nelson,James C. R. Whittington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiG-bench is a discovery-focused AI benchmark with 70 independent games encoded as strings. Each game hides transformation rules to be discovered through interaction, across seven difficulty tiers; 21 games are public and 49 remain private for secure evaluation. All games are solvable by at least one human on first attempt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap in AI benchmarks that probe the ability to discover new knowledge through experimentation under unknown objectives. DiG-bench provides controlled environments to test discovery, rule induction, and generalization under unknown win conditions.

Method: 70 independent, string-encoded games with unique, discoverable transformation rules. Agents interact to uncover rules; win conditions are unknown; seven difficulty tiers from easy to agentic-harness challenging; public subset of 21 games; private set for secure evaluation.

Result: All 70 games were solvable by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is publicly released; the remaining 49 are kept private for secure evaluation.

Conclusion: DiG-bench fills a benchmark gap by enabling evaluation of AI discovery capabilities in unknown-rule environments, supports multi-tier challenge, and provides a mix of public and private games to balance reproducibility with secure evaluation.

Abstract: Discovery---formulating novel generalizations---is a central part of the scientific process. Despite its importance, there is a gap in the current AI benchmark landscape, with few benchmarks directly probing the capacity for discovering new knowledge with experimentation in controlled environments where the objective is unknown. To address this gap, we release a new benchmark: DiG-bench (Discovery in Games). DiG-bench consists of a set of 70 independent games. Each game is encoded as a short string and has unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction and experimentation. The levels of the game present a series of challenges to test whether the rules have been discovered, where the win conditions for each level are also unknown. We provide games at seven tiers of difficulty for AI agents. The lowest tier is routinely solvable by multiple models, while the highest tier challenges the best models in agentic harnesses. All 70 games were solved by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, and the remainder is held private for secure evaluation.

</details>


### [111] [Dead text or binding clause? Measuring and restoring constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599)
*Haoyuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An API-only framework, sysname, to measure, predict, and repair behavioral relapse (revocation inertia) in multi-turn dialogues. It uses a contract ledger to pair constraints with executables and tombstones, ahead-of-time compilation of net constraint state, a sequential ablation probe for per-clause adherence, and a repair ladder under budget; it reduces relapse on a benchmark, predicts relapse via AUROC, and isolates the cost of repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Revoking user-imposed constraints often fails to take effect in ongoing dialogues, producing behavioral relapse. There is no existing granularity per clause measurement, pre-delivery prediction, or budget-aware repair.

Method: Core components: (1) contract ledger that ties each constraint to an executable checker and records revocations as tombstones and compiles the net constraint state; (2) a sequential ablation probe to quantify per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effects; (3) a repair ladder operating under token- and attempt-budget constraints. Evaluation uses ahead-of-time compilation and a dataset with NTasks/HumanEval-scale tasks and clauses, measuring relapse as constraint load increases; also trains a relapse predictor (AUROC) and tests a lightweight tombstone note as a repair signal; computes delivery overhead and API compute costs.

Result: Relapse increases with constraint load at an 8B operating point; ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse versus a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (p<...; CI ..., etc.); adaptive ladder interventions add no detectable gain under matched budgets; the relapse predictor achieves AUROC substantially above chance; a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect; overall costs are nontrivial but finite, making relapse measurable and repairable.

Conclusion: The API-only ledger-plus-probe-plus-repair latch demonstrates that relapse can be measured, predicted, and repaired under budgets. Ahead-of-time compilation is the primary driver of relapse reduction; ladder interventions provide little extra benefit; per-clause analysis and tombstones enable targeted repairs. The approach is promising for reliable constraint handling in multi-turn dialogue systems, with caveats about compute costs, dataset-specificity, and real-world deployment trade-offs.

Abstract: Multi-turn dialogues let users revoke constraints as easily as impose them, but revocation does not reliably take effect: models keep enacting withdrawn requirements (occasionally beneath comments asserting their removal), a failure we call \emph{behavioral relapse}, or revocation inertia. No existing instrument measures this influence per clause, predicts it before delivery, or repairs it under matched budgets. \sysname{} closes the three gaps through the model API alone: a contract ledger pairs every constraint with an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification; a sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect; a repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. On \dataname{} (\NTasks{} HumanEval tasks, \NClauses{} verified checkers), relapse at an 8B operating point climbs from \ScaleDelayedMTwo{} to \ScaleDelayedMEight{} as constraint load grows, while stronger models sit at floor. Under matched checkers, model, and budget, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse against a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (\RestoreDiff{}, 95\% CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, $p$ \RestoreDiffP{}); adaptive ladder interventions stacked on top add no detectable gain (95\% confidence excludes gains $\geq$ \LadderExcludedGain{}). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC \AurocPrimary{}); a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives a placebo control. At \CostDeliveryFactor{} delivery overhead and \CostTotalHedged{} of API compute for every result, revocation failure becomes a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of dialogue state rather than an invisible one.

</details>


### [112] [@skills: Attention is all you have](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12610)
*Li Yin,Zhi Li,Zhan Shi,Haoran Zhang,Haebin Seong,Zhangyang,Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes @skills, an install-less, path-addressed protocol to decouple skill content, persistence, and triggering; uses read access to deploy skills via Git-tracked copies and a optional hub for discovery, enabling scalable reuse of a long-tail of agent skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Build scalable reuse of agent skills beyond scarce prompt residency; address the long tail of skills and enable collaborative ownership by decoupling content, persistence, and triggering while preserving simple integration.

Method: Introduce a protocol that permits reading a skill from a path (no installation); separate content, persistence, and automatic triggering; when used, a copy is vendored into a project's Git-tracked tree; add a single .gitignore-style line to enable residency; directories serve as menus to group skills; provide no manifest/lockfile/registration; ship as an installable package; provide open spec; implement in AdaL CLI; pair with a hub for corpus-wide search and ranking at atskills.one; allow gh: and local paths to resolve without the hub.

Result: A novel, install-less skill protocol with path-based addressing, enabling read-access to any skill; includes tooling (AdaL CLI) and an optional hub (atskills.one) for discovery and ranking; promotes modularity and ownership through Git-tracked copies while preserving compatibility with existing paths.

Conclusion: The approach offers a promising route to expand skill reuse and reduce prompt residency, but requires evaluation of governance, security, versioning, discovery quality, and performance trade-offs in real-world usage.

Abstract: There are 56,804 public agent skills today, and teams write many more privately. The dominant delivery model is installation: once installed, a skill's description remains in the system prompt, competing for fewer than 100 reliable trigger slots. This leaves the long tail with no practical path to use and forces teams' own playbooks to compete for the same scarce space. We observe that installation bundles three separable functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Only the last requires prompt residency. We therefore propose @skills, an open protocol that separates them. A path addresses any skill, subtree, or collection, and reading a skill is sufficient to use it, so nothing is installed or made resident. The operation vendors a copy at the same path into a project's Git-tracked tree for adaptation and ownership. The operation adds one .gitignore-style line, the only element that costs prompt residency. A directory is a menu, making bundles ordinary directories rather than all-or-nothing units. The protocol requires no manifest, lockfile, or registration, and SKILL.md remains unchanged. @skills is additive, ships as an installable package, and turns any agent that can read files and run commands into a client through a single instruction file. Its open specification is at https://github.com/SylphAI-Inc/atskills and it is implemented in the AdaL CLI at https://adalagent.ai . Because paths address skills well but cannot find them, the protocol is paired with a free hub at https://atskills.one for corpus-wide search and ranking, repository-free hosting, private and team collections, and one-screen authoring. The hub is optional: gh: and local paths resolve without it, and indexed GitHub skills retain their gh: identities. Install less, use more.

</details>


### [113] [Jagged Judges: Epistemic Stability Under Silence, Pressure, and Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12645)
*Justin Zhao,Himaghna Bhattacharjee,Hannah Korevaar,Bhaktipriya Radharapu,Khalid El-Arini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wiggle Framework reveals substantial instability in LLM judges under re-prompting and adversarial pressure; majority strength helps predict wiggle; most pressure is net-corrupting with respect to ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy alone is insufficient to validate LLM judges; need robustness to prompting variation, challenges, and adversarial manipulation to ensure stable, trustworthy evaluation, grading, and reward modeling.

Method: Define a three-dimensional stress framework (Mechanical Consistency; Single-turn Conviction; Multi-turn Persistence) and apply it to 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks (safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, political-response). Use static pushback and an adversarial LLM persuader to induce verdict changes; measure wiggle rates and assess relation to ground truth; identify predictive signals such as baseline jury majority strength; perform apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison across the three dimensions.

Result: Models exhibit substantial wiggle: verdicts flip 25–71% under static pushback and 62–91% under adversarial persuader. Pressure that changes verdict is almost always net-corrupting relative to ground truth. Baseline jury majority strength is the strongest single-shot predictor of wiggle. First apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in judging contexts.

Conclusion: LLM judges are fragile to prompting and manipulation; the Wiggle Framework provides a unified stress test to assess epistemic stability. Results motivate designing more robust judging systems and using majority-strength signals to anticipate instability; future work should explore mitigation strategies and broader evaluation of judge robustness.

Abstract: LLM judges have become central infrastructure for model evaluations, online grading, and reward modeling. Judges are typically validated by accuracy on golden data, but accuracy says little about whether they are stable under re-prompting, challenge, or sustained pushback. We introduce the \emph{Wiggle Framework}, a unified stress test for epistemic stability in LLM judges. The framework decomposes judge robustness along three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting and reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under a single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained or adaptive pressure). We use the framework to study 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks spanning safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation. Every model exhibits substantial wiggle as a judge --- flipping verdicts 25--71\% of the time under static pushback, and 62--91\% with an adversarial LLM persuader. Critically, we find that pressure that succeeds in changing a judge's verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Beyond the framework itself, we identify baseline jury majority strength as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items wiggle. Taken together, this is the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in a judging context.

</details>


### [114] [SteerBench-Work: A Benchmark for Agent Steering at Action Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12654)
*Oguz Serdar,Cuneyt Mertayak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SteerBench-Work is an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for gating decisions (proceed vs hold) in long-running LLM workplace agents. It uses 106 scenarios with evidence-reversed mirrors and calibration controls to measure boundary crossing accuracy. Results show asymmetric errors (about 28% false holds on authorized work, 1% unsafe approvals), with hardest cases involving risk-resolved commits and mirrors of famous incidents producing far worse performance. Higher model capability increases boundary refusals; reasoning helps repair weak gates but not calibrated ones. Public leaderboard at steerbench.com.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate and calibrate LLM gating decisions at decision-time boundaries to prevent unsafe or unauthorized actions in workplace automation, distinguishing general capability from proper steering calibration.

Method: Incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark (SteerBench-Work) with 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls; models observe a proposed action and evidence, produce a gate decision (proceed/hold), scored on boundary accuracy across 30 model conditions.

Result: Major findings: boundary errors are largely asymmetric—false holds on authorized, evidence-cleared work occur in 28.1% of opportunities; false approvals of unsafe work occur in 1.0%. Hardest cases are risk-resolved commits; evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents yield 63.8% accuracy vs 98.5% on original incidents. Higher capability models tend to over-refuse; more reasoning can repair weak gates but not calibrated gates.

Conclusion: Calibrating steering is distinct from general capability; models may be too conservative at gating, underscoring the need for targeted calibration and evaluation via standardized benchmarks like SteerBench-Work; the public leaderboard enables cross-model comparison.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents act through tools, and a single step can send an email, merge a pull request, or wire a payment. The steering decision is the pre-commit choice at that boundary: proceed, or hold for human or policy review. We introduce SteerBench-Work, an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for that decision in workplace agents across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security.
  Release v2026-05 contains 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls, with labels split nearly evenly between proceed and hold so the two error directions get near-identical numbers of chances. A model sees the proposed action and the available evidence, returns a gate decision, and is scored on whether it crosses or holds the boundary correctly. Across 30 model conditions the failures run almost entirely in one direction: models wrongly hold authorized, evidence-cleared work on 28.1% of opportunities and wrongly allow unsafe work on 1.0%. The hardest cases are risk-resolved commits, where signed or structured evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger, and models score markedly worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8%) than on the incidents themselves (98.5%). General capability is not the same as steering calibration: higher-capability models often over-refuse at the commit boundary, and more reasoning can repair a weak gate while leaving a calibrated one flat. The public leaderboard is at steerbench.com.

</details>


### [115] [General Probabilities of Causation with Causal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657)
*Xin Shu,Zhen Lei,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tightens bounds for multivalued PoCs by incorporating covariates and mediators; extends binary PoCs bounds to multivalued settings; demonstrated with toy examples and simulations showing improvement over nonbinary bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PoCs quantify unobservable causal responses; partial identification is needed; prior binary bounds and subsequent multivalued extensions exist; this work asks whether additional causal information can tighten bounds in multivalued PoCs.

Method: Derives tighter bounds by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators; extends Tian & Pearl, Mueller et al., Li & Pearl, Shu et al. to multivalued PoCs; provides theoretical derivations, toy examples, and simulation studies.

Result: Bounds for multivalued PoCs are tightened; toy examples illustrate the results; simulations show proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

Conclusion: Incorporating covariates and mediators tightens partial identification bounds for multivalued PoCs; results demonstrate improved identifyability and provide a basis for more accurate causal inference in multivalued settings.

Abstract: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) characterize individual causal responses that cannot be directly observed and therefore generally require partial identification. Tian and Pearl first derived theoretically sharp bounds for binary PoCs, including the probability of necessity (PN), the probability of sufficiency (PS), and the probability of necessity and sufficiency (PNS). Mueller et al. subsequently tightened the bounds for binary PNS by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. More recently, Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., extended PoCs to multivalued settings and derived corresponding theoretical bounds. These developments naturally raise the question of whether additional causal knowledge can further tighten the bounds in multivalued settings. This paper addresses this question by deriving tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs through the incorporation of causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. We illustrate the theoretical results with toy examples, while simulation studies further demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

</details>


### [116] [Designing AI Pipelines for Decision-Ready ITSM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12670)
*Archan Dutta,Yash Dharmadhikari,Marat Valiullin,Rahul Guha,Alexander Liss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A sociotechnical AI pipeline converts heterogeneous ITSM exports into a multilevel executive decision-support artifact, combining LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical clustering; validated with positive stakeholder evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ITSM data are large and heterogeneous, making it hard for sales and executives to derive actionable intelligence. A human-centered, transform-and-abstract approach is needed to produce useful decision-support artifacts.

Method: Design Science Research (DSR). Build a pipeline that (1) applies LLM-based schema normalization to ITSM exports, (2) uses HDBSCAN for sub-topic clustering, and (3) applies hierarchical agglomerative clustering to form Main-topics and Sub-topics. Evaluation with six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success, assessing interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use.

Result: All four decision-support metrics exceed 4.0/5.0 on average, with trust being the most consistent signal.

Conclusion: ITSM analytics can be framed as an information systems problem of transformation and abstraction through human-centered design; the sociotechnical AI pipeline yields interpretable, actionable, and trusted executive-facing insights.

Abstract: IT service management (ITSM) systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence. This paper presents a sociotechnical AI pipeline, designed and evaluated following design science research principles, that transforms raw ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact. The pipeline combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics. A stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles shows that all four decision-support metrics, interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use, on average exceed 4.0 out of 5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal. The findings position ITSM analytics as an Information Systems (IS) problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

</details>


### [117] [On the Expressive Power of Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12671)
*Phokion Kolaitis,Rik Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An overview of how transformer expressivity as language recognizers can be analyzed via circuit complexity; surveys how multi-layer transformers map onto circuit models and what resource constraints (like attention types and precision) imply about their capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge practical transformer models and rigorous theory by leveraging circuit complexity to characterize the expressive power of transformers; address questions about what computations transformers can perform under resource constraints.

Method: Literature survey of results; applying circuit complexity concepts (gates, depth, size) to transformer's components (attention, feed-forward layers, precision); compare architectures to circuit classes; identify assumptions and limitations.

Result: Synthesize key findings showing how transformer resource parameters correspond to circuit resources; highlight when transformer architectures yield certain expressive power and where gaps or limitations remain; provide a roadmap for rigorous analysis.

Conclusion: The overview consolidates the theoretical understanding of transformer expressivity via circuit complexity, emphasizing mappings between architectural resources and circuit models and outlining open questions and directions for future work.

Abstract: Multi-layer transformers form the critical component of essentially all large language models (LLMs) in use today. Because of their ubiquity and computational capability, there is a rapidly growing body of work that aims to precisely calibrate the expressive power of transformers as language recognizers by comparing them against standard models of computation studied for decades by the theoretical computer science community. In this endeavor, circuit complexity has by and large emerged as the "correct" branch of computational complexity to analyze the expressive power of transformers; the reason is that parameterizing transformers by the various resources they use, such as attention and precision, leads to direct comparisons with different classes of circuits parameterized by resources such as type of gates, size, and depth. Here, we present an overview of selected results that delineate the expressive power of transformers using concepts and methods from circuit complexity.

</details>


### [118] [Lines and Ladders: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Framework for Large-Scale Retail Price Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12674)
*Ravi Teja Chunduri,Srikaran Reddy Boya,Deep Narayan Mishra,Ajay Kumar B,Karthik Kumaran,Pranay Kona*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A 3-agent, LLM-powered framework for building scalable 'Lines and Ladders' pricing taxonomies to enforce Everyday Low Price strategies across millions of items; achieves high F1 for Lines and strong domain-specific performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual governance cannot scale to catalogs with millions of items; inconsistent pricing harms customer perception and sales. A scalable, automated approach is needed to maintain price coherence and reduce cognitive load on human operators.

Method: A triad of specialized LLM agents constructs coherent pricing structures by (1) identifying key attributes, (2) extracting multi-modal values, and (3) applying hierarchical grouping, forming 'Lines and Ladders' taxonomies. The framework is evaluated on real enterprise data and deployed in production, with a focus on reducing cognitive overload.

Result: 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines. In Food & Consumables, precision >90% and recall >75%; in unstructured General Merchandise catalog, assignment accuracy is 80.2%.

Conclusion: A scalable, context-aware architecture can reliably automate taxonomy construction for pricing governance, improving consistency and operational efficiency in large-scale retail catalogs.

Abstract: Maintaining price consistency and executing an Every Day Low Price strategy is critical for global retailers. However, with catalogs spanning millions of active items, manual governance of price relationships is infeasible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and cannibalizes sales. To address this, we present a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework designed to automate the construction of "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. Our framework employs specialized LLM agents to construct these coherent pricing structures by identifying key attributes, extracting multi-modal values, and applying hierarchical grouping logic. Evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production, our 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines by mitigating cognitive overload. The system achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

</details>


### [119] [Privacy-Preserving RAG by Concealing Sensitive Information from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675)
*Saleh Almohaimeed,Saad Almohaimeed,Mousa Jari,Fahad Alotaibi,Khalid A. Alobaid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAG is a privacy-preserving framework for retrieval-augmented generation that replaces sensitive entities with aliases before sending data to external generators, achieving strong user-response accuracy while hiding sensitive information; evaluation shows ~75–79% entity-hiding accuracy across models and over 80% user-accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate privacy risks in RAG where external generators observe user queries and retrieved documents, addressing gaps where prior work focuses on access control but not on prompt leakage.

Method: Introduce a lightweight SEAG model that identifies sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and builds a replacement table to anonymize queries and retrieved documents. Two datasets are created for fine-tuning and evaluation. The SEAG pipeline is applied before forwarding data to external generators; performance is measured via a User metric and model-level hiding accuracy.

Result: SEAG achieves >80% User metric accuracy (correct responses with sensitive information hidden). For model-level hiding, Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 reach 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91% respectively, demonstrating effective privacy preservation in RAG.

Conclusion: SEAG demonstrates the feasibility of a lightweight, dataset-supported privacy-preserving framework for RAG, enabling the use of external generators without disclosing sensitive information; future work could improve hiding accuracy and broaden evaluation across more models and datasets.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is widely used to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering user queries. Existing privacy research on RAG has focused on preventing unauthorized users from accessing sensitive data. However, another important problem that is often overlooked in RAG privacy research is that external generators have access to the query and the retrieved documents, which may contain confidential information that could potentially be misused or accessed for unintended purposes. In this paper, we introduce the Sensitive Entity Alias Generator (SEAG), a privacy-preserving framework that empowers users to utilize powerful third-party generators without disclosing sensitive information. SEAG introduces a lightweight model that locates sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table. The table is used to replace sensitive words in the user's query and in the retrieved documents before they are forwarded to an external generator. For this purpose, two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG models to generate entity replacement tables, and another for evaluating the entire SEAG framework. The experimental results demonstrate the success of the SEAG framework. As for the User metric, which measures the ability of the model to provide a correct response to the user while hiding sensitive information from the external generator, all SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy. Additional analysis further evaluated the ability of SEAG models Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 to hide all sensitive entities within given documents. The results show good performance with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

</details>


### [120] [The Role of Natural Language Understanding in Multimodal Video-Based Dengue Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12677)
*Danial Sharifrazi,Saadat Behzadi,Julakha Jahan Jui,Mojtaba Mohammadi,Nouman Javed,Roohallah Alizadehsani,Prasad N. Paradkar,Asim Bhatti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal YOLO-CLIP framework classifies infection status of mosquitoes from video frames, achieving high frame- and video-level accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Infection can alter mosquito behavior, but small, fast-moving insects with variable environments make reliable feature extraction from video difficult; a robust, multimodal approach is needed.

Method: Use YOLO to segment mosquitoes, extract visual frame features, and align them with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a CLIP-based shared embedding space. Fine-tune with supervised bidirectional contrastive learning. Perform frame-level image-text similarity-based classification, then temporally aggregate for video-level decisions. Ablation studies assess the impact of fine-tuning, CLIP representations, and the textual branch.

Result: Frame-level accuracy 98.54%, sensitivity 99.91%; complete video-level performance after temporal aggregation; ablations show fine-tuning and CLIP representations are essential; the textual branch provides semantic alignment but does not outperform a vision-only model in accuracy.

Conclusion: Vision-language frameworks (YOLO-CLIP) are effective for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data and can generalize to similar tasks, with the textual prompts aiding semantic alignment rather than outright accuracy gains.

Abstract: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data is challenging because mosquitoes are small, move rapidly and irregularly, and are affected by environmental factors such as background, lighting, and shadows, which can make reliable feature extraction difficult. In this study, a YOLO- and Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP)-based vision-language framework is proposed to classify mosquito flight frames of uninfected and Dengue virus serotype 2 (DENV2)-infected mosquitoes. First, YOLO is used to isolate mosquito regions from the background. Then, visual features extracted from video frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. The multimodal model was fine-tuned using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning and evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. The results show that the proposed method achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at the frame level. After temporal aggregation of frame-level information, the model achieved complete video-level performance. The ablation results showed that fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential for this domain, while the textual branch provided semantic image-text alignment rather than an accuracy advantage over the vision-only model. These findings suggest that vision-language models can provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data.

</details>


### [121] [Beyond the Best Guess: Improving LLM Solution Coverage with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12679)
*Conor F. Hayes,Elliot Meyerson,Kajetan Schweighofer,Roberto Dailey,Babak Hodjat,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evolution Strategies (ES) outperforms Reinforcement Learning (RL) for post-training of LLMs in discovery tasks by increasing pass@k and expanding solution coverage, leading to better performance on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve discovery with LLMs by enhancing test-time solution diversity and avoiding distribution collapse inherent in RL-based fine-tuning.

Method: Apply Evolution Strategies as a gradient-free, population-based optimization in weight space with random perturbations; compare ES to RL in terms of pass@k and output distribution on discovery tasks (e.g., math benchmarks).

Result: ES yields higher pass@k scores than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage, translating into better performance on standard math benchmarks.

Conclusion: ES provides a superior foundation for post-training in discovery-oriented domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in discovery domains such as math and science. The usual approach is to present the problem to the model and use its answer as the proposed solution. However, beyond this best guess, discovery can be enhanced by increasing test-time compute. In a process called pass@k, the model is allowed to explore the solution space and generate diverse candidate solutions. Unfortunately, the standard approach to post-training LLMs through Reinforcement Learning (RL) may limit pass@k: the model's output distribution narrows around high-reward outputs, causing the solution coverage to collapse. The alternative is to use Evolution Strategies (ES), a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations. As this paper shows, ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage. This coverage in turn makes it possible to achieve better results in e.g. standard math benchmarks. Thus, ES provides a better foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

</details>


### [122] [Spatial Memory Agent: Experience-Grounded Procedure Memory for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743)
*Haokai Zhang,Yuhang Ding,Yunshu Zhou,Xinze Du,Shengtao Zhang,Zhiyue Zhao,Yuling Xi,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A parameter-update-free spatial self-evolution framework for frozen VLMs: Spatial Memory Agent (SMA) leverages verifier-guided reflection to distill transferable lessons from spatial experiences, assigns a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS), and uses semantic-filtered memory retrieval during read-only deployment to improve spatial reasoning without updating model parameters or using external tools at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between post-training/agentic tools and purely frozen VLMs by enabling parameter-free, experience-grounded self-improvement of spatial reasoning through reusable memory lessons.

Method: In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM for an answer and reward, applies verifier-guided reflection to distill compact transferable lessons, assigns each lesson a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS), and uses read-only deployment with semantic/TRS-based retrieval to guide inference; no parameter updates or external tools during deployment.

Result: Across five spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro-average across model blocks and the best accuracy in most of the 20 evaluations, demonstrating a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution.

Conclusion: Parameter-update-free self-evolution via experience-grounded memory is effective for improving spatial reasoning in frozen VLMs, scalable across model scales and environments, and offers a viable alternative to fine-tuning or external tool use.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is becoming a foundation for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. To improve the spatial reasoning ability of VLM agents, existing work has mainly followed two lines. One line uses post-training methods, such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Another line adopts an agentic paradigm in which the model calls external spatial tools, such as depth estimation and 3D reconstruction tools, to gather intermediate spatial evidence. We study a complementary and underexplored route: Can a frozen VLM agent improve its spatial reasoning through \textbf{parameter-update-free self-evolution}, without depending on external expert spatial tools at inference time? We present \textbf{Spatial Memory Agent (SMA)}, an \textbf{experience-grounded runtime framework} that converts verified spatial experience into reusable transferable lessons. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM, obtains a predicted answer and reward, and uses \textbf{verifier-guided reflection} to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. SMA further assigns each lesson a \textbf{Transfer Reliability Score (TRS)}, which is initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes as visit evidence of future transfer reliability. During \textbf{read-only deployment}, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking, allowing the retrieved memory to guide frozen model inference. Across five representative spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy among the evaluated methods in most of the 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution across the evaluated frozen model scales and environments.

</details>


### [123] [Correct Is Not Governed: Provenance Integrity in Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12761)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Matrix provides a deterministic causal-state layer for governance of agentic workflows, enabling provenance, validation of completion evidence, and selective invalidation to support auditable and verifiable work; it acts as an institutional integrity layer rather than a general accuracy enhancer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In institutional settings, correct outcomes alone are insufficient; there is a need to ensure decisions and actions are backed by inspectable provenance, authority, and evidence to enable auditability and integrity.

Method: Introduce Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Evaluates governed vs direct workflows and tests role-separated transfer with a completeness contract.

Result: Governed workflows often reach the same outcomes as direct workflows but preserve governing evidence, reject unsupported closures, and confine recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge shows the completeness contract can over-block valid outputs produced outside the authoring context.

Conclusion: Matrix is not presented as a general accuracy booster but as an institutional integrity layer that makes agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

Abstract: Agentic workflows are commonly evaluated by whether they reach the correct outcome. That is insufficient in institutional settings, where a correct action may rely on the wrong authority, an unsupported completion claim, or work made stale by a later change. We define governed execution as work whose decisions, completion, and response to change are supported by inspectable provenance. We present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Across controlled comparisons, governed and direct workflows often reached the same outcomes, but only the governed path consistently preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge then failed: a deterministically enforced completeness contract severely over-blocked synthetic packets produced outside its authoring context.
