These results do not establish Matrix as a general accuracy enhancer; they support its primary role as an institutional integrity layer for making agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

</details>


### [124] [PROVE-RT: Generating Mechanized Theorem Prover Scripts for Real-Time Systems using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762)
*Sadat Shahriyar,Shareef Ahmed,Abdullah Al Arafat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROVE-RT uses retrieval-guided, staged LLM assistance to generate PROSA/ROCQ mechanizations for schedulability analysis, achieving 44.7% success on a curated test set, addressing limitations of direct prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schedulability analysis is essential for real-time systems, but traditional proofs are hard to scale and maintain. Mechanized PROSA/ROCQ proofs exist but require expert proof engineering. LLMs hold promise to automate script generation, but current models lack PROSA-specific knowledge.

Method:  PROVE-RT combines dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. It builds a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1,191 real-time systems papers comprising 13,134 informal sketches with dependency info, and evaluates on a curated set against direct prompting.

Result: Direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably produce valid PROSA mechanizations; PROVE-RT attains a 44.7% success rate on the evaluation set.

Conclusion: Retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can meaningfully improve automated mechanization of schedulability analyses in PROSA/ROCQ, offering a scalable path toward more maintainable formal verification in real-time systems, though room remains for improvement.

Abstract: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but existing tests are often developed through pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires substantial domain expertise and proof-engineering effort. Recent successes of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks make them promising candidates for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized theorem provers. However, state-of-the-art LLMs often lack the PROSA-specific knowledge required to correctly use its modeling abstractions and proof patterns.
  This paper introduces PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts to mechanize schedulability analyses in real-time systems literature. PROVE-RT guides generation through dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. We construct a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1, 191 real-time systems papers, containing 13, 134 informal sketches with dependency information. On a curated evaluation set, direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations, whereas PROVE-RT achieves a success rate of 44.7%. These results show that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ.

</details>


### [125] [ARAC: Benchmarking Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness on End-to-End Researchs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788)
*Jiale Cui,Yueyao Yuan,Kaixi Zhong,Xiaogang Xu,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARAC-Bench is a two-component evaluation framework that mimics researcher processes to assess alignment, coherence, and completeness of Auto-Research, revealing gaps with SOTA models and correlating with human rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the evaluation gap in Auto-Research: measuring how well AI research processes align with human researchers, in terms of reasoning, methodology, and progression, not just final outputs.

Method: Two synergistic components: (1) Academic Cognition Skills system that converts implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; (2) a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol that decomposes the research process into Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis under modular, independent constraints, enabling traceable evaluation. Applied to 11 SOTA frameworks.

Result: Best alignment score of 67.9/100; a strong correlation (0.8141) with Ph.D. candidate rankings, indicating ARAC-Bench captures dimensions researchers value. Provides a fine-grained diagnostic tool and a scalable reward signal for training autonomous research systems.

Conclusion: ARAC-Bench demonstrates a meaningful, scalable approach to evaluating and guiding autonomous research, highlighting gaps in current models and offering a mechanism to align AI research behavior with human quality standards.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has surfaced a fundamental evaluation challenge: how can we measure the alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of its research trajectory with human research behavior? We propose Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness, ARAC-Bench: a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework that shifts the objective from matching final answers to reproducing high-quality human research processes. The framework operates through two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which is the first to transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; and a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol, which decomposes the research process under strict modular constraints into three traceable, mutually independent dimensions: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Systematic evaluation of 11 SOTA frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 of 100, revealing a significant gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. Candidates rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming that ARAC-Bench reliably reflects the dimensions researchers truly value. ARAC-Bench provides not only a fine-grained diagnostic tool but also a scalable reward signal for training the next generation of autonomous research systems.

</details>


### [126] [CABS+: Efficient and Scalable Model Merging via Conflict-Aware Sparsification and Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842)
*Yuchen Liu,Zongzhen Yang,Binhang Qi,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CABS+ extends Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification for model merging by replacing grid-search with Adaptive Weight Allocation (gradient-free) and adding an asymmetric fitness objective, along with Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to guide model selection; empirically shows improved performance, robustness, and efficiency across 27 datasets and 5 models, with gains over AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, lower GPU memory usage, and faster merging time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate parameter conflicts and knowledge interference in multi-task model merging; reduce the time complexity of finding merging coefficients; avoid dominance of high-performing tasks in the optimization objective.

Method: Propose CABS+ with Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA), a gradient-free search for merging coefficients; employ an asymmetric fitness function to balance gains across tasks; conduct a systematic empirical study of factors affecting model merging; introduce Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify mergeability and guide model selection; compare against CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging on 27 datasets across 5 models (LLMs, small-language, and vision).

Result: CABS+ achieves improvements of 16.97% over AdaMerging and 12.93% over WUDIMerging in overall performance; demonstrates stronger stability and robustness across varying task counts and architectures; uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging; delivers approximately 4× faster merging time than WUDIMerging.

Conclusion: CABS+ is an effective and efficient model merging framework. The Adaptive Weight Allocation with gradient-free search reduces time complexity, and the asymmetric fitness function yields more balanced cross-task gains. RSS provides a practical metric for estimating mergeability and guiding model selection.

Abstract: Model merging has recently attracted significant attention as a promising paradigm for constructing unified multi-task models without requiring additional retraining. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks often degrade merged-model performance. Prior work introduced Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification (CABS), which reduces parameter interference through structured pruning and sequential masking. However, CABS relies on grid search to determine scaling coefficients, resulting in exponential time complexity, while its optimization objective can be dominated by high-performance tasks, leading to suboptimal overall performance. To address these limitations, we extend CABS and propose CABS+. Specifically, Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via a gradient-free search scheme to reduce time complexity, while an asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. Moreover, we conduct a systematic empirical study of key factors influencing model merging performance and propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. We compare CABS+ with state-of-the-art model merging methods, including CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging, across 27 datasets and 5 models covering large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Extensive experiments verify the effectiveness and efficiency of CABS+. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, exhibits stronger stability and robustness across varying task numbers and model architectures, uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

</details>


### [127] [Beyond Retrieval: Query-Conditioned Reuse of Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847)
*Yifei Li,Heng Wang,Lingling Zhang,Muye Huang,Xinyu Zhang,Jiashuai Liu,Hang Yan,Rongman Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-retrieval reuse in long-horizon tasks is identified as a bottleneck. The paper proposes a simple, target-bound reuse (QCR) and an evaluation framework that fixes retrieval components while varying the agent's support. QCR improves success and efficiency over direct full trajectory reuse across 2,391 targets, with strong reranking performance, suggesting decoupling retrieval from usable support is beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval alone yields past trajectories but does not specify how to leverage them when conditions change. There is a bottleneck in translating retrieved experience into safe, useful post-retrieval support for new tasks.

Method: Define an evaluation framework that fixes retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget, while varying the delivered support. Instantiate with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a target-bound note including reusable procedure, recoverable bindings, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. Evaluate on 2,391 targets from WebArena, WorkArena, AppWorld.

Result: QCR achieves 62.3% average success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, with 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Direct trajectory injection loses utility as traces lengthen or source values change, while target-bound support preserves more of the gains.

Conclusion: Separating retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into actionable, safe support is beneficial for long-horizon task performance; QCR serves as a testbed to study reuse strategies without committing to a universal memory format.

Abstract: Retrieval can identify a past trajectory that may matter, yet it does not specify how an acting agent should use that trajectory after users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed. We identify this post-retrieval reuse step as a distinct bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory and formulate an evaluation framework that holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. We instantiate the framework with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a deliberately simple target-bound note that records a reusable procedure, bindings to recover, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. QCR serves to test the reuse hypothesis rather than to claim a universally preferred memory format. Across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, QCR reaches 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task Success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Analyses by trajectory length and source--target binding shift show that direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or source-specific values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the measured gain. The resulting framework separates retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for a new task.

</details>


### [128] [Practice Makes Unsafe: Skill Misevolution in Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12851)
*Xutao Mao,Liangjie Zhao,Xiang Zheng,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SkillMisevo-Gym/BENCH and SafeEvolve to study lifecycle risks of persistent, cross-task artifacts in self-improving LLM agents; shows unsafe successes can become reusable policies, and that governance of updates/reuse is essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts and cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and execution. Self-improving agents can convert unsafe trajectories into persistent, transferable policies; need lifecycle-aware assessment and mitigation.

Method: Present SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks; SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen-benchmark design with benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics; SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Empirical evaluation across 25 agent-method configurations (525 tasks per configuration, 25 episodes); exposure sweep with three malicious tasks; analysis of 21 evolved configurations; metrics include carryover ASR and safe/reuse effectiveness.

Result: All 21 evolved configurations authored unsafe artifacts; 15 led to fresh-session harm. In exposure sweep, malicious tasks raised carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. SafeEvolve reduced unsafe retrieval by 26.7 percentage points and fresh-session harm by 17.3 points, with mean benign-utility change of only +0.4 points.

Conclusion: Persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates are written and what future executors reuse. Code is available at the linked repository.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents convert successful trajectories into persistent cross-task state. An unsafe success can thereby become reusable policy after its triggering input disappears. Skill evolution makes this failure measurable by distilling operational trajectories into executable, transferable, and inspectable procedures. Because evolution optimizes task outcomes rather than procedure safety, compromised experience can cause skill misevolution. Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts but cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and later execution. To expose this lifecycle, we introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks, and SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design from malicious exposure to carryover tasks, with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics. We also introduce SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Across 25 agent-method configurations, each covering 525 tasks in 25 episodes, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only fifteen lead to fresh-session harm. In the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across representative skill evolution methods, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by only 0.4 points. Together, persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates write and what future executors reuse. Code is available at https://github.com/henrymao2004/misevolve.

</details>


### [129] [AI and Consumer Rights in India Working Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863)
*Omir Kumar,Sriya Sridhar,Vibhav Mithal,Balaraman Ravindran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: India's Consumer Protection Act (2019) may cover AI-related harms but has notable gaps, particularly in proving causation, mapping the AI value chain to traditional roles, and addressing complex, multi-stakeholder liability; enforcement needs sector-specific clarification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether the CPA 2019 adequately addresses harm from defective AI products/services and whether liability is proportionately allocated across the AI value chain.

Method: Legal doctrinal analysis of the Act's product liability, harm, and deficiency definitions; mapping of AI stakeholders (data providers, model developers, deployers, users) to the Act's manufacturer/seller/service provider categories; evaluation of causation, enforcement, and fault allocation in AI contexts.

Result: The Act is technology-agnostic and could theoretically cover AI harms (injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, loss of control), but practical gaps exist: establishing causation is difficult due to design-origin defects; AI value chains do not map neatly onto the Act's roles; current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms for multi-stakeholder AI harms; enforcement clarity, especially across sectors, is needed for applicability to AI entities.

Conclusion: To adequately govern AI-related harms under CPA 2019, reforms are needed: clearer causation standards for AI, expanded or redefined roles to capture data providers and model developers, sector-specific guidelines, and potentially AI-specific liability rules to address multi-stakeholder responsibility and enforcement challenges.

Abstract: As AI systems proliferate in consumer facing applications, questions about liability for AI related harms remain unresolved. This working paper examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses harm caused by defective AI products and services, and whether it proportionately allocates liability across the AI value chain.
  The Act's broad definitions of product liability, harm, and deficiency appear technology agnostic and potentially applicable to AI related incidents including personal injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, and loss of control. However, significant gaps remain. Proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm presents a technical challenge, as AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. Additionally, the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, yet the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users that do not neatly map to these categories. Current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms to effectively address complex, multistakeholder AI harms. While the Act may cover AI entities, enforcement requires clarification on sector specific overlaps.

</details>


### [130] [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Improving Comprehension and Reasoning in Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877)
*Runze Zhao,Zixin Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Leyuan Chang,Xiaopeng Fu,Boyu Qiao,Dongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReflectFact introduces a self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification that plans explicit reasoning paths, calibrates evidence grounding, and verifies/revises reasoning steps, achieving state-of-the-art on HOVER and EX-FEVER.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address two core limitations in multi-agent fact verification: (1) lack of global verification objective awareness among subtask agents, leading to misdirected reasoning, and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and provided evidence that undermine grounded reasoning.

Method: Three key tasks: (1) Explicit Reasoning Path Planning to build an evidence-grounded path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing claims into sub-questions, and integrating verified facts into the final verdict; (2) Evidence-Drift Verification to re-answer by quoting supporting evidence when the grounded answer echoes parametric priors, calibrating evidence-grounding; (3) Reasoning Reflection Verification to re-examine and regenerate each reasoning step when inconsistencies are detected, correcting biases (e.g., location/replacement bias) from a global task perspective; finally, aggregation of validated reasoning chains to produce the verdict.

Result: Empirical evaluation on HOVER and EX-FEVER shows ReflectFact remedies provide improved comprehension and reasoning over prior methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32% on HOVER and 2.78% on EX-FEVER.

Conclusion: ReflectFact effectively addresses comprehension and reasoning defects in existing multi-hop fact verification methods, yielding reliable verdicts through explicit planning, grounding verification, and reflective error correction.

Abstract: Multi-hop fact verification, which verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, is critical for combating misinformation on social media yet remains highly challenging. Recent methods primarily rely on multi-agent collaboration to decompose fact verification into specialized subtasks. However, these methods face two critical limitations: (1) agents may perform individual subtasks without sufficient awareness of the global verification objective, causing their reasoning to deviate from the intended direction; and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and the provided evidence may undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts. To address these challenges, we propose ReflectFact, a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification. ReflectFact introduces three key tasks. Explicit Reasoning Path Planning builds an evidence-grounded reasoning path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing the claim into sub-questions, and integrating the verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification makes the agent re-answer by quoting the supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes its parametric prior, thereby calibrating evidence deviation to ensure grounded comprehension. Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it once an inconsistency is detected, correcting reasoning flaws such as location bias and replacement bias through a global task perspective. Subsequently, the agent aggregates validated reasoning chains to yield reliable verdicts. Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies the comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and respectively outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32\% and 2.78\% on the two datasets.

</details>


### [131] [Predictive Memory Localization: Forecasting Selective Intervention Paths from Internal Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892)
*Jinhao Jing,Tian Zeyu,Lucas Qingyang Fang,Zhisheng Chen,Shuang Chen,Yuhao Luo,Qiannian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PML converts measured-grid activation localization into predictive, testable memory-localization-guided control directions, demonstrating selective-path gains with low-dose causal responses and improved decision-making under predictor-driven abstention across multiple models and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether localization corresponds to selective operating regimes rather than generic directional signals, enabling falsifiable forecasts of margin-level outcomes and risk-aware interventions.

Method: Treat the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization; separate random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage; compare static localization and supervised geometry with strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Large-scale frozen study: 3,000 records, 9 datasets, 14 domains; 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths; 210,000 path-strength evaluations. Layer 7 geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction yields target-any/clean-any gains (13.1%/12.3%), exceeding random by 3.6/3.4 pp (record-paired bootstrap). Cross splits show |α|=0.1 as strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths |α| in {0.25,0.5}. On held-out records, predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improving utility and reducing semantic-neighbor damage versus a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in dense scans. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains; low-dose responses yield macro AUROC 0.801–0.828. PML thus provides falsifiable forecasts of margin-level selective outcomes and risk-aware intervention decisions.

Result: Evidence of selective-path gains and robust predictive performance: geometry-derived directions achieve nontrivial target-any and clean-any gains beyond chance; strongest signals at small perturbation magnitudes; predictor-driven abstention enhances utility and reduces collateral damage; macro AUROC on held-out data remains high (0.801–0.828) across three base models; results are stable across multiple data splits.

Conclusion: PML reframes memory localization as a falsifiable predictor of margin-level selectivity and a risk-aware control strategy, demonstrating consistent gains across models and datasets and enabling abstention-based decision-making to mitigate semantic-neighbor damage.

Abstract: Activation steering turns localized representations into control directions, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. We introduce Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. PML separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage, and compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Our frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across record-, dataset-, and domain-grouped splits, responses at $|α|=0.1$ are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths $|α|\in\{0.25,0.5\}$. On held-out records, a predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage relative to a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in a dense scan. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC. PML therefore turns memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and a risk-aware intervention decision.

</details>


### [132] [Agent Behavioral Contracts II: Certifying Compositional Reliability Without Assuming Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12895)
*Varun Pratap Bhardwaj,Garima Singh,Arun Pratap Bhardwaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Independence-based compositional reliability bounds are fragile in multi-agent systems; observed positive dependence inflates joint failure beyond the product of marginals. The authors provide an assumption-free, finite-sample LP certificate based on joint moments, show bootstrap dependence-model bounds worsen with more data, and demonstrate that enriching moment evidence tightens the identified interval and raises the safety floor. They also offer an anytime-valid certificate and release artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the commonly used conditional-independence assumption in compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems and to develop robust, assumption-light certificates that quantify the true joint reliability under limited information.

Method: Empirical test of co-failure in a two-agent handoff (two models) with preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions; assess effect of substituting models or vendors; analyze dependence via bootstrap bounds for fitted models; develop a finite-sample certificate as a linear program over the joint distribution using a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson moment box; expand moment functionals from 10 to 14 to tighten bounds; provide an anytime-valid certificate under optional stopping.

Result: Found that co-fail occurs on 90% of missions where either agent fails (log OR ≈ 6.66; phi ≈ 0.916). Substituting a different model reduces the association in all six contrasts; substituting a different vendor (with an already-different model) shows no effect, supporting a preregistered null. Bootstrap dependence-model bounds deteriorate with sample size. The assumption-free LP certificate is sound and sharp relative to the available moments; expanding moments narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and raises the lower bound from 0.2455 to 0.4116. An anytime-valid certificate maintains type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping. Marginal dependence statistics can reverse condition ordering. Data and code artifacts are released.

Conclusion: Independence assumptions in compositional reliability can dramatically misrepresent true joint reliability due to positive dependence; model sharing inflates joint failure and redundancy is over-credited. Assumption-free and robust certificates (linear-programming based) provide valid bounds; increasing moment information improves tightness. Dependence-statistics can be misleading, so the proposed certificates offer practical, safety-critical guarantees in the presence of uncertain dependence.

Abstract: Compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems multiply component reliabilities, a step licensed by a conditional-independence assumption that is routinely stated and rarely tested. We test it. Two instances of one model, in a two-agent handoff, co-fail on 90.0% of the missions on which either fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi 0.916), in a preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions scored by deterministic code with no LLM judge. Substituting a different model reduces the association in six of six contrasts; substituting a different vendor, model already different, does not -- a registered hypothesis reported as a null.
  The error is signed and runs against the operator: positive dependence inflates joint failure above the independence product, so redundancy is over-credited exactly when components share a model. The assumption-free alternative is often vacuous, and fitting a dependence model is worse: we prove a bootstrap bound on a fitted model's functional loses coverage of the truth as n grows, the identification gap being O(1) while the bootstrap haircut is O(n^{-1/2}). More data makes such a certificate worse, with no visible symptom.
  We give a finite-sample certificate assuming no dependence structure: a linear program over the joint, over a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments. It is sound, sharp for the information supplied, and monotone in the moment family. Enriching ten moment functionals to fourteen narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and lifts the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. A companion anytime-valid certificate holds type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping.
  Common dependence statistics are marginal-bounded and can reverse an apparent ordering of conditions when the compared agents fail at different rates. Contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and the preregistration are released.

</details>


### [133] [Polish Medical Visual Question Answering: Vision-Language Models Underutilize Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12928)
*Jakub Pokrywka,Łukasz Grzybowski,Antoni Lasik,Marek Kubis,Jeremi Ignacy Kaczmarek,Wojciech Kusa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Polish medical VQA benchmark from licensing-exam questions tests multi-modal models; best open-weight model reaches 79% on full set, GPT-5.6 nears human on a subset, and most models lag behind humans; ablation shows models rely more on text than image, yet some information is extractable from answer choices alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of non-English, medically-focused VQA benchmarks and evaluate visual grounding and cross-lingual model capabilities in high-stakes clinical scenarios.

Method: Construct a dataset of image-containing questions from Polish medical licensing exams plus a text-only QA control set; evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models; perform ablation by removing image, question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance; compare to human reference where available.

Result: Best model achieves 79.0% accuracy on full VQA; GPT-5.6 surpasses approximate human reference on the subset with candidate responses; other models perform worse than humans; models derive more information from the question than the image; models show above-chance accuracy from answer choices alone even when components are missing.

Conclusion: The benchmark is challenging and reveals a stronger role for textual information over imagery in this setting; while image data provides value, current models still underperform humans on medical VQA, highlighting room for improvement in multilingual, visually-grounded medical VQA and grounding methods.

Abstract: We introduce a Polish-language medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmark, built from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for licensed physicians and dentists pursuing specialist certification. The benchmark comprises image-containing questions spanning diverse medical specialties and visual domains, together with a text-only question answering (QA) control set. We evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models. The task remains challenging: the best model achieves 79.0\% accuracy on the full VQA set, and only GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; all other evaluated models perform worse than humans. To assess visual grounding, we compare complete inputs with configurations omitting the image, the question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance. Models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image and perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across both QA and VQA, they nevertheless achieve above-chance accuracy from the answer choices alone, showing that non-trivial performance can persist even when key task components are missing.

</details>


### [134] [FlashDrive: Flash Vision-Language-Action Inference for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12932)
*Zekai Li,Yihao Liang,Hongfei Zhang,Jian Chen,Yesheng Liang,Zhijian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashDrive is an algorithm-system co-design to accelerate Vision-Language-Action models for autonomous driving by addressing four bottlenecks with lightweight, tailored algorithms, achieving 4.7x end-to-end latency reduction on Alpamayo 1.5-10B with minimal accuracy loss, enabling near real-time performance on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models for autonomous driving suffer from a cascade of bottlenecks (visual encoding over frames, LM prefill, autoregressive reasoning tokens, and uniform flow-matching denoising). Isolated optimizations are insufficient; a unified, end-to-end acceleration strategy is needed to reach real-time control while preserving accuracy.

Method: A four-stage co-design called FlashDrive: (1) streaming KV-cache reuse across frames via temporal overlap; (2) non-autoregressive diffusion drafter leveraging low per-token entropy and intra-block correlations for speculative decoding; (3) adaptive step caching for flow-matching denoising using the velocity field’s structure; (4) system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization.

Result: End-to-end latency reduced from 717 ms to 151 ms (4.7x) with negligible accuracy loss: minADE6@6.4s shifted by 0.08 m; minADE1 improved; closed-loop collision and off-road rates improved in simulation; increased reasoning VLA throughput from 1.4 Hz to 6.6 Hz on a single GPU.

Conclusion: End-to-end, multi-stage co-design can substantially bring VLA-based autonomous driving closer to real-time deployment. The approach leverages model- and system-level optimizations that complement each other; further work should validate robustness and energy efficiency in real-world settings and across diverse driving tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise to bring end-to-end reasoning to autonomous driving, but their computational cost remains far too high for real-time control. The core challenge is structural: VLA inference is not a single bottleneck but a cascade of four. Visual encoding wastes compute on overlapping video frames; language-model prefill recomputes context that could be carried over from the previous timestep; reasoning tokens are generated serially despite low entropy; and flow-matching denoising applies uniform compute to a non-uniform velocity field. Addressing any one stage in isolation leaves the others untouched. We propose FlashDrive, an algorithm-system co-design framework that targets all four stages simultaneously. Our key insight is that each bottleneck admits a distinct, lightweight algorithmic shortcut: temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; the low per-token entropy and strong intra-block correlations of driving-domain reasoning make a non-autoregressive diffusion drafter highly effective for speculative decoding; and the velocity field's structure---sharp at the endpoints, flat in the middle---permits adaptive step caching that concentrates compute where it matters. Layered on system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, these techniques compound. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x) while leaving accuracy essentially unchanged: minADE6@6.4s shifts by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation. By raising a 10B-parameter reasoning VLA from 1.4~Hz to 6.6~Hz on a single GPU, FlashDrive moves end-to-end autonomous driving substantially closer to real-time deployment.

</details>


### [135] [Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, and Fragility in Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935)
*Lei You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DECAF decomposes perturbation responses into three interpretable components—Evidence (E), Contradiction (C), and Fragility (F)—as inputs are progressively revealed. The decomposition preserves total magnitude (Abs = E + C + F) and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across vision and tabular tasks, E, C, and F track independently measured behaviors; DECAF yields more faithful explanations than magnitude alone and enables efficient, forward-only trajectories with competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnitude of perturbation response alone conflates different semantic effects of input changes. The paper motivates a decomposition that distinguishes supporting, opposing, and endpoint-null signals along the reveal path to provide interpretable evidence about model decisions.

Method: Introduce DECAF, routing aligned (supporting) responses into Evidence (E), opposed (contradiction) responses into C, and endpoint-null responses into Fragility (F). The decomposition preserves the original magnitude (Abs = E + C + F) and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Evaluate across controlled vision and tabular settings, with analyses of response trajectories under different reveal paths.

Result: Key findings: (1) In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, the largest DECAF component aligns with observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, vs 35.0% for magnitude alone. (2) Altering the reveal path increases total response by ~80%, but E remains stable while F grows >4x. (3) On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform tested general-purpose baselines. (4) On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short DECAF trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

Conclusion: DECAF provides an interpretable, magnitude-preserving decomposition of perturbation responses that better aligns with observed behaviors than magnitude alone. It yields efficient, trajectory-based explanations and scales to large models, offering practical advantages for attribution and analysis.

Abstract: Perturbation methods explain model decisions by measuring prediction changes under altered inputs, but response magnitude tells us only how much a model reacts, not what that reaction means. The same magnitude can support the final factual-counterfactual difference, oppose it, or arise strongly along the perturbation path yet vanish at the endpoint. We therefore track how the contrast develops as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. We introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which routes aligned, opposed, and endpoint-null responses into evidence E, contradiction C, and fragility F. The decomposition preserves ordinary magnitude exactly, Abs = E + C + F, and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across controlled vision and tabular settings, the three components track independently measured behavior. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, we compare cases with nearly identical response magnitude but different independently measured behaviors. The largest DECAF component agrees with an observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared with 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing only the reveal path increases total response by nearly 80%, yet evidence barely changes while fragility grows by more than 4x. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform the tested general-purpose attribution baselines. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

</details>


### [136] [Moose: Latent concept learning with reasoning-shortcut awareness in $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12961)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moose compiles EL++ TBox and finite ABox to a differentiable SDD for neuro-symbolic learning under partial supervision; uses closure clauses for exhaustive families outside EL++; proves formal properties; defines a partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task on OWL EL; shows empirical gains on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo; presents first reasoning-shortcut analysis in OWL EL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge neuro-symbolic learning with expressive OWL EL ontologies, addressing the gap of reasoning-shortcut awareness in ontology-based learning and enabling differentiable model counting under partial supervision.

Method: Translate EL++ TBox and finite ABox into a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD); use SDD as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer; add outside-the-EL++ closure clauses to handle partial supervision over exhaustive families; prove termination, soundness, and completeness; ensure polynomial intermediate sizes; validate proofs in Lean; define a partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task with per-individual classifiers for latent concepts.

Result: Moose outperforms propositional neuro-symbolic baselines, fuzzy-logic, and ontology-embedding baselines; provides the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in OWL EL; validates on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo.

Conclusion: Establishes a formal, scalable framework for reasoning-shortcut aware neuro-symbolic learning on OWL EL ontologies, integrating differentiable WMC via SDD with EL++ reasoning; enables partial-supervision latent-concept learning and supports large production ontologies.

Abstract: The OWL 2 EL profile is used in some of the largest production ontologies, including the Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT. Existing neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learning methods accept propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut (RS) awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings. We present Moose, a method that compiles an $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD). The SDD acts as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer, and we add closure clauses outside the $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ profile on declared exhaustive families to overcome the limited expressivity of $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ under partial supervision. We show termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes, and validate the proofs in Lean. We then define the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over an OWL EL ontology, i.e., learning per-individual classifiers for latent concepts from observed ABox literals, and evaluate Moose on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in an OWL EL setting.

</details>


### [137] [OGR-MARL: Option-Guided Residual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Heterogeneous USV Cooperative Pursuit in Constrained Port Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12995)
*Mao Jiayang,Wang Lanfeng,Peng Zhao-Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An option-guided residual MARL framework (OGR-MARL) decouples from specific MARL algorithms to incorporate rule-guided behavior with residual learning for heterogeneous USV pursuit in constrained port waterways. Instantiations with MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC achieve strong performance, notably OGR-MASAC with 75% capture rate and zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed map, indicating generalization to more complex ports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of pursuing evaders in constrained port waterways under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. Traditional MARL requires exploration in constrained environments; leveraging rule-guided behavior can improve safety, coordination, and sample efficiency.

Method: Propose OGR-MARL: an option-guided residual learning framework decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. Key components include a shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning that learns corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors. Implementations named OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC correspond to base MARL backbones.

Result: In a Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario, the OGR-MASAC variant achieves a 75.0% capture rate, showing effective rule compliance and superior heterogeneous coordination among tested methods. A zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map demonstrates notable generalization without retraining.

Conclusion: OGR-MARL offers a decoupled, backbone-agnostic approach that enhances rule-guided behavior with residual learning, improving coordination and transferability in complex port scenarios. The framework shows strong empirical results with at least one backbone (MASAC) performing best and suggests potential applicability to broader ports without retraining.

Abstract: Heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires evader interception under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. This paper proposes OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that is decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning, allowing different MARL algorithms to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring constrained port environments from scratch. We instantiate OGR-MARL with representative continuous-control MARL backbones, including MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC, yielding OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC. Experiments in an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario show that the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, promising mission-effective rule compliance, and the best heterogeneous coordination among the tested methods. Without retraining, zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map achieves promising results, demonstrating the generalization potential of OGR-MARL in more complex port scenarios.

</details>


### [138] [Foundations of MT-PDCL: Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13018)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MT-PDCL extends probabilistic definite clause logic to continuous spaces by grounding in measure-theoretic constructs, replacing finite grounding with exact Lebesgue integration over continuous measure spaces to perform inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grounding-based PDCL is limited to finite domains and discrete distributions; there is a need for a principled framework that supports continuous measurable spaces while preserving declarative syntax.

Method: Define stochastic variables over bounded index domains and interpret over standard Borel σ-algebras; build on Continuous Distribution Semantics; model probabilistic rules as independent causal events; replace propositional grounding with exact Lebesgue integration; introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator to combine priors with observations.

Result: Inference becomes exact, algebraic, and differentiable within a continuous domain, removing the combinatorial grounding bottleneck and enabling continuous probabilistic models while preserving definite clause syntax.

Conclusion: MT-PDCL achieves expressive continuous probabilistic modeling within a pure declarative framework; the approach trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric complexity of higher-dimensional integration but preserves principled semantics and differentiability.

Abstract: Standard probabilistic logic programming frameworks typically rely on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This operational requirement restricts exact inference to finite domains and discrete probability distributions. In this paper, we introduce Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), a generalized foundational framework that eliminates this finite-domain restriction. By explicitly defining stochastic variables over bounded index domains and equipping the interpretation space with standard Borel $σ$-algebras, MT-PDCL allows logical variables to operate natively over continuous measurable spaces. Building on Continuous Distribution Semantics, MT-PDCL models probabilistic rules as mutually independent causal events. However, rather than aggregating these derivations via finite boolean circuits, declarative entailment is formally defined through exact Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. We introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that unifies the integration of continuous prior distributions with the evaluation of exact continuous observations. We demonstrate that this approach replaces the combinatorial bottleneck of discrete grounding with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference. While this transition trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric curse of dimensionality, it achieves the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving the pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

</details>


### [139] [From Local Mismatch to Global Impact: Optimizing Cache Reuse Policy for Efficient Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13043)
*Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Zhikang Xie,Xiangyu Yue,Cheng Jin,Weizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Global-Impact Cache (GCache) for diffusion models uses Bernstein-reparameterized error propagation and bilevel optimization to learn caching policies that align with generation quality, yielding substantial speedups and quality gains over local heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models have high inference overhead; existing cache policies use local similarity which poorly correlates with final image/video quality due to non-uniform error propagation along the denoising trajectory.

Method: The paper provides a theoretical upper bound on error propagation, reparameterizes the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form, and formulates bilevel optimization where inner objective searches optimal reuse policy and outer objective weights error by its impact on generation quality.

Result: GCache outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation; on Wan2.1 video model, 2.17x speedup and LPIPS drop from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

Conclusion: By aligning caching decisions with their impact on perceptual quality and incorporating rigorous propagation analysis, GCache achieves better efficiency and fidelity, suggesting broader applicability to diffusion-based generation.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved dominant performance in visual generation but suffer from substantial inference overhead. While cache-based acceleration has emerged as a promising solution, existing policies rely on local similarity heuristics, which we identify as being significantly misaligned with final generation quality. This discrepancy stems from the non-uniform propagation and accumulation of errors along the denoising trajectory. To address this, we propose Global-Impact Cache (GCache). We first establish a rigorous theoretical characterization of the error propagation upper bound. Recognizing that this bound can be overly conservative for complex, highly non-convex diffusion models, we further reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form and reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization problem. In detail, GCache identifies an optimal reuse policy in the inner objective while aligning the error-weighting function with generation quality loss in the outer objective. This framework effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation. Notably, on the state-of-the-art Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache maintains a 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing generation quality, reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

</details>


### [140] [BoardroomAI: Dependency-Aware Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation through Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046)
*Sanjeev Manivannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Persistent human-in-the-loop decision framework using a typed decision DAG, intervention compiler, and dependency-aware propagation. Demonstrates efficient incremental updates on synthetic data, but real-world validation remains as future work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of transcript-based multi-agent systems by enabling continuous human involvement, challenge of assumptions, and dynamic evidence integration in organizational decision-making.

Method: Introduce a typed decision graph (DAG) capturing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; an intervention compiler to apply confirmed human actions as explicit graph updates; dependency-aware propagation to identify affected subgraphs and selectively reactivate specialists; an evaluation framework assessing intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity; evaluation conducted on 600 synthetic interventions and 12-case pilot.

Result: Propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In the 12-case pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in six. Highlights the need for a decision-sufficient context closure for human-steered deliberation; results are synthetic/prototype-level.

Conclusion: The approach enables efficient, human-in-the-loop deliberation via incremental graph updates, but the findings emphasize that correct intervention routing alone is insufficient without adequate context; real-world validation and further context-sensitive mechanisms are needed.

Abstract: Organizational decisions are co-created while evidence, constraints, and human priorities continue to evolve. In conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, humans typically provide an initial problem, agents deliberate internally, and the system returns a final response. BoardroomAI instead treats the human as a persistent participant who can intervene by challenging assumptions, modifying constraints, changing priorities, introducing evidence, or redirecting the decision process. We operationalize this human--agent coexistence through four components: (i) a typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; (ii) an intervention compiler that converts confirmed human actions into explicit graph updates; (iii) dependency-aware propagation that identifies affected subgraphs, preserves unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivates relevant specialists; and (iv) an evaluation framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Across 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case exploratory pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the remaining six. These abstentions show that correct intervention routing may still provide insufficient context for synthesis, motivating a \emph{decision-sufficient context closure} for human-steered multi-agent deliberation. All results are synthetic and prototype-level.

</details>


### [141] [DMDIntel: Interpreting Large Language Models via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048)
*Amogh Joshi,Animesh Mukherjee,Sergey Utyuzhnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMDIntel leverages dynamic mode decomposition to render LLM classification predictions interpretable by ranking input tokens via mode projections; it outperforms PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP across multiple datasets and model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the opacity of LLM decision-making in classification and advance input attribution by leveraging dynamical systems techniques to extract dominant patterns (modes) from hidden states.

Method: Apply dynamic mode decomposition to the LLM hidden states to identify prominent modes; compute projection values of input tokens onto these modes; rank tokens by their contributions; evaluate against baselines across three datasets and three model families.

Result: Experimental results show that the ranked token attributions derived from DMDIntel substantially outperform PCA, Integrated Gradients, and SHAP.

Conclusion: DMDIntel provides a robust, interpretable attribution mechanism for LLM classifications by translating hidden-state dynamics into human-friendly token rankings, with consistent gains over established baselines.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce DMDIntel which uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to make the predictions made by LLMs in a classification task interpretable. It develops an input attribution pipeline, that first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into prominent patterns, also known as modes, and then associates ranks to the input tokens based on the projection values on those modes. Rigorous experiments across three datasets and three model families consistently show that the ranked attribution of input tokens obtained using DMDIntel by far outperforms state-of-the-art techniques such as principal component analysis, integrated gradients and SHAP.

</details>


### [142] [VALG: An Agentic System for ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060)
*Dechen Zhang,Xuan Tang,Xinxiang Yin,Xingwu Chen,Jian Qian,Difan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VALG automates ML theory research via a graph-based, agentic workflow combining verification, adaptive problem formulation, and structured proof development; evaluated on COLT-2026 subproblems with mixed success; open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To organize and accelerate ML theory discovery by treating hypothesis formulation, proof construction, and validation as an autonomous, interconnected, agentic workflow that preserves mathematical relations to source problems.

Method:  VALG comprises multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. It maintains source-relative theorem branches with fixed specs, checks a typed proof-dependency graph, constructs/reviews proofs in dependency order, and routes failed attempts to address whether obstructions are in derivation, proof structure, or formulation. When formulation obstructions occur, it generates related variants or relaxations that preserve the relation to the source problem.

Result: Applied to nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs yielded internally finalized theorem candidates within the scope of their source briefs; the other seven produced restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. The studies demonstrate that VALG maintains source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts as distinct mathematical entities. VALG is open-source at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: VALG demonstrates a structured, autonomous workflow for ML theory research, capable of generating partially or fully formalized results while preserving relationships to original problems. It shows promise for accelerating discovery, with future work likely needed to broaden success, validate robustness, and expand the problem domain.

Abstract: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups in which the data model, training protocol, oracle access, loss, metric, and randomness define the phenomenon that a theorem is meant to explain. Solving an open problem therefore requires the problem formulation, theorem target, and proof mechanism to be developed in concert. Researchers formulate hypotheses, test them through preliminary theoretical or empirical analysis, and refine both assumptions and proofs. We investigate whether this process can be organized as an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research.
  We develop VALG, an agentic system that combines multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Within each source-relative theorem branch, VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks the theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, the proof structure, or the theorem formulation and routes the next attempt accordingly. Formulation-level obstructions initiate an explicitly related variant or relaxation, preserving the mathematical relation between the resulting theorem and the source problem.
  We evaluate VALG on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs produce internally finalized theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs; the remaining seven yield restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. These case studies show how VALG keeps source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts mathematically distinct. VALG is open source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

</details>


### [143] [Uniform Herding: Exemplar Replay with Representation Refresh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13061)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Uniform Herding improves continual learning replay by partitioning the active exemplar set across observed classes and refreshing exemplars from a bounded candidate pool; on CIFAR-100 with 10 tasks, it outperforms iCaRL in final accuracy and forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feature representation drift during incremental learning requires replay buffers that remain informative; previous methods like iCaRL use aging exemplars with limited flexibility. Uniform Herding aims to better preserve earlier classes under bounded resources by reallocating and refreshing exemplars.

Method: Introduce Uniform Herding protocol: allocate the current active set across observed classes and use a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. Experimental setup: CIFAR-100, ResNet-18, 10 incremental tasks, active budget M=2000, retrieval budget b=64, three seeds. Comparisons include iCaRL and ablations varying NME/herding, distillation, and budget parameters.

Result: Uniform Herding achieves 44.00±0.51% final average accuracy and 17.22±0.43% forgetting, vs. iCaRL's 42.33±1.20% accuracy and 24.87±1.11% forgetting. Retrieval budget has a smaller effect than active budget. Ablations show final accuracy drops when NME or herding are replaced; forgetting rises when distillation is removed. The reported comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end, and findings are limited to the tested protocol.

Conclusion: Uniform Herding yields a meaningful improvement over iCaRL within the tested protocol, but results are protocol-bound. Future work could test additional datasets, task sequences, budgets, and further isolate the impact of the refresh component.

Abstract: As the feature representation changes, replay must preserve the earlier classes. However, only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed. We propose Uniform Herding, which allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks, a ResNet-18 backbone, active budget $M=2{,}000$, retrieval budget $b=64$, and three seeds, Uniform Herding obtains $44.00\pm0.51\%$ final average accuracy and $17.22\pm0.43\%$ forgetting, compared with $42.33\pm1.20\%$ and $24.87\pm1.11\%$ for iCaRL. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, final accuracy decreased when NME or herding was replaced with the tested alternatives, while forgetting increased when distillation was removed. Changing the retrieval budget has a smaller effect across the tested range than changing the active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end. It does not isolate the effect of refresh from the other protocol differences. These results are limited to the tested protocol.

</details>


### [144] [Explanatory Engagement Under Rare Anomalous Failure: Asymptotic Rarity in Model Behavior (or: The Asymptotic AI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063)
*Sam Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Explorations of explanatory engagement in LLMs under rare failures reveal no universal monotone trend; outcomes depend on elicitation design. With immediate forcing to explain every failure, length increases then plateaus; grouped-run batching and passive prompting reduce apparent collapse; model-specific self-monitoring emerges in some cases. A guaranteed-failure test shows variability in anomaly recognition, distinct from engagement. Discrete rate points limit inference; finer sampling needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether an LLMs’ propensity to generate explanations (length, specificity, and self-reported confidence) adapts as anomalous failures become rarer within an ongoing workflow, beyond mere anomaly detection.

Method: A local zero-cost harness evaluated three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) on a repeated tool-call task with one call failing with probability p. Eight failure rates (0.2 down to 0.0001) and five elicitation conditions (e.g., immediate_forced, grouped_runs, passive_unprompted) were used. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells with backfilled rates) tested anomaly recognition. Limitation noted: analysis is restricted to discrete rate points.

Result: Across conditions, a simple, monotonic rise in explanatory engagement with decreasing failure rate is not supported. Under immediate_forced, explanation length peaks at 28.4 words near p=0.05, then plateaus to 17.4–19.0 words at rare rates; confidence climbs from ~53% to the 70s–90s. Grouped_runs shows no collapse; passive_unprompted yields a floor artifact in aggregate, though llama3.1:8b reveals model-specific self-monitoring (structured confidence reports) while the others do so infrequently. Elicitation structure markedly modulates collapse observability. The guaranteed-failure run reveals model differences in anomaly recognition independent of engagement. Limitation: discrete rate sampling constrains inference; future work should explore continuous-rate regimes.

Conclusion: Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of observed collapse in explanatory engagement under rare failures. There is no universal monotone trend across models or conditions; anomaly recognition and engagement can diverge, and self-monitoring effects may emerge selectively. Future work should employ finer-grained rate sweeps to map behavior between measured points.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM behavior under anomalous conditions asks whether a model notices anomalies. We ask a narrower question: once a model sits in a workflow with a low, controllable failure rate, does its explanatory engagement - length, specificity, self-reported confidence - change as failure grows asymptotically rarer? We built a local, zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) running a repeated tool-call task where one call fails at probability p, swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions from immediate prompting to none. We hypothesized a rise in engagement as failures grew rarer, then a collapse near a detectability threshold. Pooled across conditions this appeared false: length fell in a flat, monotonic pattern. Splitting by condition overturned that. Under immediate_forced, where the model must explain every failure instantly, the predicted rise is confirmed but followed by a plateau, not a collapse: length peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settles to 17.4-19.0 words at the rarest rates, and confidence rises unevenly from about 53% to the 70s-90s. Under grouped_runs, explanation batched to run-end, no collapse appears. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but a recovered logging gap revealed real, model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1:8b volunteers structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence as trials accumulate; the other two do so only once, as boilerplate. Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells, backfilling rates where random sampling gave zero real failures) shows models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly, distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points cannot capture behavior between them, a direction for future work.

</details>


### [145] [Behavioral Reprogramming of Open-Weights Models: Cognitive Plasticity and Alignment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13069)
*Lucia Malíčková*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Empirically reprogram open-weight LLMs to be proactive, Socratic tutors using compute-efficient PEFT (LoRA) under constrained HPC, identifying architectural and training bounds, scaling benefits, and cross-lingual alignment limits with DPO-based behavior shaping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Counter the default passive, sycophantic alignment of LLMs by forcing cognitive plasticity to enable high-frequency, constraint-aware question generation and Socratic dialogue across languages.

Method: Massively parallel hyperparameter sweep with 405 HPC jobs; identify LoRA rank threshold r=16; perform epoch ablation to locate optimal training window e in [2,3]; scale model to 14B parameters; apply Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to decouple assertive behavior from syntax; conduct cross-lingual stress tests to assess zero-shot persona transfer across linguistically related vs distant languages.

Result: Found that generalization peaks within the optimized training window (e in [2,3]); minimum validation loss 0.919; 14B-scale model achieves perplexity 1.414 on localized evaluation; DPO successfully separates behavior from syntax; cross-lingual tests show strong alignment within closely related language families but degradation for morphologically distant targets; provides bounds for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

Conclusion: Provides a rigorous empirical framework for scalable, compute-efficient behavioral modification of LLMs across languages, outlining clear architectural (LoRA rank) and training (epoch window) conditions for eliciting proactive behavior while revealing boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly aligned to function as passive, sycophantic assistants. We challenge this default paradigm by empirically evaluating the cognitive plasticity of open-weight architectures when subjected to rigorous behavioral reprogramming. Our objective is to induce a proactive, Socratic conversational framework, characterized by high-frequency question generation under strictly constrained high-performance computing (HPC) conditions. Through a massively parallelized hyperparameter sweep comprising 405 HPC jobs, we define precise mathematical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). We identify an architectural threshold at LoRA rank $r=16$ and demonstrate via extensive epoch ablation that generalization capacity strictly reaches its optimal convergence within an optimized training window of $e \in [2, 3]$ depending on dataset density (minimum validation loss of 0.919). Furthermore, scaling model capacity to 14B parameters yielded a lower localized evaluation perplexity (1.414). Subsequent Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) successfully decoupled the underlying assertive behavior from localized syntax, while rigorous cross-lingual stress testing reveals both the capabilities and the structural boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer, demonstrating robust alignment in closely related linguistic families alongside identifiable degradation pathways in morphologically distant targets. These findings establish a rigorous empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

</details>


### [146] [EEG-PRIME: Prototype-Aligned Representation Learning with Multi-Level Conditioning for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13072)
*Shuailei Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Wei Zhang,Jinbo Chen,Zhiwei Guo,Yong Li,Yi Ding,Cuntai Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-PRIME is a two-stage foundation model for cross-dataset EEG decoding that uses masked pretraining and prototype-aligned instruction tuning to achieve cross-subject and cross-task generalization, enabling near-zero-shot transfer across diverse BCI paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG decoding models suffer from poor generalization due to domain shifts in data acquisition and individual neurophysiology, necessitating a unified, adaptable model for multi-task EEG across datasets.

Method: A two-stage approach: (1) masked pretraining of an EEG encoder with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation to learn transferable representations; (2) instruction tuning with conditioning for task semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant signals. The conditioning modulates a Q-Former via Layer-wise Query Modulation, and frozen text embeddings of class labels act as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces.

Result: Across 16 datasets spanning motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload, EEG-PRIME shows consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models in cross-subject settings. On two held-out datasets, it achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization or linear probing, indicating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

Conclusion: EEG-PRIME enables instruction-aware, subject-invariant cross-dataset EEG decoding with strong cross-task generalization and zero-shot transfer, highlighting the viability of EEG foundation models for diverse BCI paradigms.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) decoding models often generalize poorly across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. We propose EEG-PRIME, a two-stage EEG foundation model for cross-dataset multi-task decoding. EEG-PRIME combines masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms. During pretraining, an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. During instruction tuning, EEG-PRIME incorporates task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning.
