The resulting conditioning signal modulates the Q-Former through Layer-wise Query Modulation, while frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces. Experiments on sixteen datasets covering motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets, EEG-PRIME achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing, demonstrating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

</details>


### [147] [SPADE: Speculative Decoding for Precise and Low Cost Distributed Edge Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13076)
*Divya Jyoti Bajpai,Kishan Kumar Upadhyay,Manjesh Kumar Hanawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A distributed inference framework that uses edge-generated speculative tokens with a cloud verifier to dramatically cut cloud calls (76%) while maintaining full-model accuracy, via a plug-and-play edge-cloud SD pipeline without retraining, validated on SpecBench and CNN/Daily Mail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the computational cost and latency of deploying large LLMs by shifting most work to edge devices and using speculative decoding with a cloud-level verifier to preserve accuracy, avoiding retraining.

Method: Deploy a compact draft model on the edge to rapidly generate candidate tokens. A large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained; rejections trigger verifier correction. The system is plug‑and‑play with no retraining, and evaluated on SpecBench and CNN/Daily Mail across multiple NLP tasks.

Result: Cloud model calls are reduced by 76% with zero loss in accuracy versus the full big-model, demonstrating substantial cost and latency savings while preserving accuracy.

Conclusion: SD across edge and cloud enables scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate LLM deployment without retraining. The approach offers practical deployment benefits and generalizes across tasks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural language understanding and generation, but their deployment is constrained by high computational demands. Deploying smaller LLMs directly on the edge can circumvent this, but with degraded accuracy. Deploying smaller cloud-based big LLMs preserves performance, but at the cost of expensive per-token computation. We present a distributed inference framework, \our{}, that integrates speculative decoding (SD) across edge and cloud. A compact draft model deployed on the edge generates candidate tokens rapidly, and a large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, while only rejections trigger verifier correction, substantially reducing the number of cloud queries. Our plug-and-play design shifts the bulk of computation to the edge, significantly lowers inference time and cloud cost, and preserves the accuracy of the big model without any retraining requirement. Our approach demonstrates a practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments. Experimental results across multiple Natural Language Processing tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets demonstrate that \our{} reduces the cloud model calls by $76\%$ with zero loss in accuracy as compared to the full model.

</details>


### [148] [Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging: A Semantic Superposition and Contextual Lamination Framework for Malpractice-Resilient Online Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13100)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,Kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sri Ram,Parani Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes MCCT, a mathematical framework to securely render assessment content via semantic superposition under MSCCM, with an explicit extraction-channel model, six operators, and entropy-based ambiguity guarantees enabling legitimate recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current online assessments rely on browser lockdowns and monitoring but remain vulnerable to content extraction (screenshots, OCR, scraping). There is a need for a principled, decidable framework that protects rendered content while preserving accessibility.

Method: Develops the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT) within the MARS framework, introducing an extraction-channel operator and six coupled constructs (Context Inversion, Contextual Lamination, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, Context Camouflage Tensor); models ambiguity via conditional entropy; guarantees exact recovery through an filtering identity; presents a rendering algorithm with complexity analysis and a pre-registered evaluation protocol.

Result: Establishes theoretical properties: ambiguity bounds, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing; provides a closed-form expression for computational ambiguity; ensures legitimate recovery via exact filtering; delivers a rendering algorithm and evaluation protocol within MCCT.

Conclusion: MCCT delivers a mathematically rigorous foundation for resilient, accessible digital assessment by securing rendered content while maintaining readability for legitimate users, enabling behaviorally adaptive and computation-robust evaluation.

Abstract: Contemporary online assessment systems rely primarily on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics, yet remain vulnerable to attacks that extract the assessment content itself through screenshots, screen sharing, optical character recognition, and automated scraping. This paper extends the Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) within the MARS (Multi-modal Assessment Resilience Suite) by introducing the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT), a mathematical framework that protects rendered assessment content through semantic superposition. Authentic assessment content and synthetically generated camouflage are represented as a unified rendering while remaining recoverable only by legitimate candidates. The framework models the adversarial extraction process through an explicit extraction-channel operator and develops six coupled constructs: the Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Computational ambiguity is formulated using conditional entropy, yielding a closed-form expression that quantifies uncertainty during unauthorized extraction, while legitimate recovery is guaranteed through an exact filtering identity. We further establish theoretical properties governing ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing, and present a rendering algorithm with computational complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol. MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered assessment content while preserving readability for legitimate users.

</details>


### [149] [Robust Dempster-Shafer Evidence Fusion with Chaos-Conflict Measurement and Historical-Experience Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108)
*Huiyu Li,Weibo Liu,Xinru Xu,Dongchen Gao,Meng Zhang,Junhua Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a unified DS-based evidence reasoning framework combining chaos-conflict measurement, long-term reliability profiling, and adaptive fusion rules to improve multi-source decision making; validated on 16 real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory suffers from treating cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty separately and lacks exploitation of long-term reliability across decision contexts; a unified framework is needed for adaptive, robust fusion.

Method: 1) Introduces chaos-conflict measurement that jointly quantifies cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity with five proven properties; 2) Employs a historical experience driven weighting scheme that partitions decision space via spectral clustering and uses regret theory to compute context-specific reliability from past fusion outcomes; 3) Develops a hybrid combination rule that balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by global conflict; 4) Applies a belief-interval decision strategy to maintain epistemic uncertainty; 5) Validates through experiments.

Result: On 16 real-world benchmarks, achieves average F1 of 85.78 and mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods; ablation analysis confirms contribution of each component.

Conclusion: The framework offers an effective and robust approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making, with demonstrated performance gains and reliability across contexts.

Abstract: Multi-source evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory faces two persistent challenges: existing conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, yielding incomplete evaluations, and current fusion methods evaluate evidence sources exclusively through instantaneous comparisns without exploiting their long-term reliability across diverse decision contexts. This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that addresses both limitations. Specifically, a chaos-conflict measurement is introduced to jointly quantify cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, with five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. A historical experience driven weighting scheme partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes. These mechanisms feed into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that enables robust classification without discarding epistemic uncertainty. Experiments on 16 real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and a mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirms the contribution of each component we proposed. The framework offers an effective approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making.

</details>


### [150] [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120)
*Qianxi Yan,Chunrong Chen,Jiuzhou Zhao,Min Zhang,Yongzhou Xu,Xiaochuan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEvo introduces a feedback-driven, multi-turn evaluation loop and governance layer to sustain skill evolution in AI services, outperforming single-turn QA and end-to-end evaluation baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current skill evolution relies on single-turn feedback, causing degradation to go unaddressed; there is a need for gradient-rich, layer-by-layer feedback and an independent governance mechanism to repair degradation and constrain gradient drift.

Method: Two-component approach: (1) a feedback generator that converts multi-turn user simulation into layered defect exposure, so each revision generates new feedback; (2) an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing gradient drift. Evaluation across six cloud-service categories, nine production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files.

Result: SkillEvo surpasses baselines by 23.0 points over self-reflection-based evolution and by 15.4 points over single-turn QA-driven evolution.

Conclusion: Trustworthy, layer-wise feedback combined with proactive governance yields sustained skill evolution, yielding more robust and less degraded skills across iterations.

Abstract: Agent Skills are today either hand-authored or produced in a single LLM generation pass, and consequently possess no closed loop through which they might improve from the interaction failures they actually cause. Recent work does close this loop, but derives its feedback from single-turn question-answering evaluation. The consequence is a sharp asymmetry: once the first round has patched the gaps that a single exchange can reveal, the evolution gradient decays, the defects that surface only across multiple turns remain invisible, and evolution stalls. Governance in these systems is likewise driven by an end-to-end verification score, a scalar gate that can reject a degraded candidate but can neither localize nor repair its structural cause. We argue that the binding constraint on sustained skill evolution is neither editing capability nor the number of iterations, but whether the evaluation feedback keeps supplying trustworthy evolution gradients. We introduce SkillEvo, in which trustworthy feedback generates the gradient and controllable governance constrains its direction. The first component recasts multi-turn user simulation from an evaluation endpoint into a feedback generator: follow-up questions expose defects layer by layer, so that every round of revision both consumes feedback and produces new feedback. The second replaces the passive rejection of a scalar gate with an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing the gradient from drifting as degradation accumulates. Across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single- turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

</details>


### [151] [Numeracy in Large Language Models: Fundamental Limitations and Paths to Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13129)
*Aoxin Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NGF decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (mapping numeral forms to value) and Procedural Grounding (performing arithmetic), to diagnose and mitigate LLM numerical failures. It organizes benchmarks, analyzes failure modes and mitigations, and evaluates three model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, proposing interventions (digit-aware tokenization, Abacus Embeddings) and deployment guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are strong on high-level math but unreliable on elementary numeracy (magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, scientific notation). Numeracy is a distinct capability requiring grounding in both representations and procedures, not just symbolic manipulation or reasoning. A systematic framework is needed to diagnose, compare, and improve numeracy across models and benchmarks.

Method: Define RG and PG within the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF). RG maps numeral forms to value, magnitude, and alternative representations; PG executes arithmetic per formal definitions. Use NGF to organize diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigations. Review tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. Apply NGF to three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Propose architectural interventions (digit-aware tokenization, Abacus Embeddings) that help models trained from scratch; for pretrained models, leverage supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools. Conclude with deployment recommendations and future research directions.

Result: Provides a coherent framework to diagnose and categorize numeracy issues, links architectural choices and data properties to failures, and demonstrates cross-benchmark/model insights. It shows certain interventions can improve numeracy for scratch-trained models, while pretrained systems rely more on fine-tuning, reasoning strategies, and external tools. The work offers practical deployment guidance and a roadmap for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models across benchmarks.

Conclusion: NGF offers a structured lens to analyze numeracy in LLMs and actionable directions for model design, data, and tooling. It emphasizes the separation of representational and procedural grounding, benchmarks-driven diagnostics, and targeted interventions, with caveats about limited transferability of some scratch-induced gains to pretrained models and the need for external tools and scaffolds in real-world settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks yet remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks, including magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, and scientific notation. This survey examines basic numerical understanding as a capability distinct from high-level mathematical reasoning. We propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (RG), mapping numeral forms to value, magnitude, and equivalent representations, and Procedural Grounding (PG), executing arithmetic operations in accordance with their mathematical definitions. Using NGF, we organize recent diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigation strategies. We review evidence concerning tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. We also apply NGF in a coordinated evaluation of three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can improve models trained from scratch but are generally unavailable to users of pretrained systems, for whom supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical. We conclude with deployment recommendations and research directions for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models.

</details>


### [152] [Rethinking Normalization Placement for LLMs: Post-Norm under Curriculum Depth Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156)
*Sheng Ren,Yadong Wang,Naiqiang Tan,Jiangang Kong,Jun Fang,Rui Liu,Jun Wang,Kai Chen,Lipeng Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Normalization placement interacts with curriculum depth growth in Transformers; post-norm gains appear under block-adjacent curriculum, while joint training shows negligible difference, implying boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks is crucial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether standard pre-norm is still preferable once depth is introduced via a training curriculum and whether normalization placement and curriculum interact.

Method: Distill Qwen3-8B into a 9-layer student. Compare pre-norm vs post-norm under two regimes: joint training and curriculum depth growth. Include controls: post-joint control with active-layer tokens, single-block and freeze controls. Diagnose boundary-scale conditioning via residual scales, structural-token scale drift, and identity-like final pre-grow block.

Result: Under joint training, pre-norm and post-norm are nearly indistinguishable (CE difference ≈ 0.0004). Under curriculum growth, post-norm outperforms pre-norm by ≈0.0328 CE. A post-joint control with active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, arguing against compute as sole cause. The ranking crosses over as blocks are appended; this effect localizes to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics tie post-norm to stable residual scales and pre-norm to scale drift; final pre-grow block is nearly identity-mapped. Phase-wise crossover supports boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended.

Conclusion: Treat normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in curriculum-based distillation; accounting for their interaction is essential for optimal forward conditioning and efficient learning.

Abstract: Pre-norm is the standard normalization placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models. We ask whether this preference persists when depth is introduced through a curriculum. In curriculum depth growth, each appended block receives the boundary representation produced by a trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. We therefore test whether placement and training curriculum interact. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable under joint training, differing by $0.0004$ validation CE, while post-norm improves over pre-norm by $0.0328$ under curriculum growth, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, which rules out compute as the sole explanation. The ranking crosses over during the curriculum: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the ranking change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift; on a fixed batch, the final pre-grow block is also nearly identity-mapped. Together with the phase-wise crossover, these observations are consistent with boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended. The results motivate treating normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in this distillation setting.

</details>


### [153] [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley Valuation for Skill Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173)
*Chang Liu,Yuqi Zhang,Yiman Zhong,Boyi Liu,Hengjun Wang,Shuyue Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillShapley applies Shapley-value attribution to quantify the contribution of individual steps within agent skills, using a two-phase approach to identify informative coalitions and adaptively sample marginals; validated on SkillsBench to highlight high-/low-value steps for better skill design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of quantitative, step-level understanding of how each action in an agent skill affects overall task performance, hindering principled skill development.

Method: Model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value problem. A two-phase framework first locates informative coalitional regions (discretized rewards with sharp cliffs) and then adaptively samples coalitions to gather reusable marginal evidence, leveraging largely additive step interactions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on SkillsBench shows SkillShapley effectively identifies high- or low-value steps, enabling guidance for skill creation improvements.

Conclusion: SkillShapley offers a principled, efficient method for step-level attribution in agent skills, providing actionable insights to improve skill design and performance.

Abstract: Agent skills are crucial external instructions that enable language agents to execute long procedural tasks such as coding or document processing. Existing agent skills are primarily created through human manual crafting or agent execution traces, with limited understanding of how each step contributes to overall skill performance on specific tasks; i.e., there remains an open problem in quantifying the contribution of individual steps within an agent skill. To address this issue, we first model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, and then propose SkillShapley, a step-level attribution framework for agent skills. Notably, SkillShapley operates in two phases, motivated by key empirical insights, i.e., discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs, and step interactions that are largely additive rather than synergistic. Specifically, it first identifies informative coalitional regions, and then adaptively samples new coalitions that can yield reusable marginal evidence. Experiments on skills from the widely adopted SkillsBench demonstrate that our SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing several key takeaways for agent skill creation.

</details>


### [154] [Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Bounded Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)
*Zechuan Wang,Siyuan Lu,Hongxuan Zhang,Linjian Mo,Chenyi Zhuang,Leilei Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrEST is a hierarchical credit assignment framework for RL with verifiable rewards that uses turn-segmented advantages and entropy-gated self-teacher modulation to provide dense token-level supervision while preserving verifier-bounded performance, outperforming RL and distillation baselines on long trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the mismatch between trajectory-level credit signals and heterogeneous per-turn outcomes, enabling dense token-level guidance without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

Method: Two-level credit mechanism: (1) turn-segmented verified advantages to address inter-turn dilution; (2) entropy-gated self-teacher modulation to refine intra-turn token contributions; employs a privileged self-teacher to modulate update magnitudes instead of dictating update directions.

Result: CrEST shows consistent improvements over RL and distillation baselines on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench across two model scales, with largest gains on long trajectories and strict session-level metrics.

Conclusion: Dense token-level credit assignment via a self-teacher can be integrated with RL using verifiable rewards without violating the verifier bound; the teacher serves as a magnitude modulator rather than an update-orientation oracle.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) offers a verifier-bounded performance ceiling for training multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

</details>


### [155] [TsuGO: Probing Search Efficiency in LLM Reasoning via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221)
*Shunwen Bai,Ziping Ma,Chaoyang Zhang,Yarong Wang,Jiale Liu,Zhen Qin,Qingpei Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TsuGO is a process-level reasoning benchmark for LLMs that evaluates Search Efficiency in Go life-and-death problems, revealing that current models struggle with search organization and that longer chain-of-thought does not guarantee better search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond final-answer accuracy and quantify how LLMs plan and allocate reasoning resources, disentangling domain knowledge from search strategy, which existing CoT-focused benchmarks fail to capture.

Method: Create a constrained, verifiable Go life-and-death problem space with an explicit search-tree structure. Parse CoT into this search tree, measure Search Efficiency plus Token Efficiency, and provide diagnostics, visualizations to compare candidate generation, response checking, branch exploration, and backtracking across models.

Result: Stronger LLMs locate correct candidates earlier and maintain productive branches, yet most models resemble unguided search rather than neural-guided strategies like KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not guarantee better search performance.

Conclusion: Search organization and reasoning-resource allocation are missing dimensions in LLM evaluation. TsuGO enables disentangling knowledge from search and provides a process-level assessment of search efficiency.

Abstract: The evaluation of LLM reasoning is moving from final-answer accuracy to process-level assessment, yet existing methods still fail to capture how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources--that is, how they organize search. Prior process-level methods focus on the coherence and redundancy of chain-of-thought (CoT), and most benchmark tasks have a single objective solvable by static capabilities such as derivation and tool use, leaving search organization unmeasured. We introduce TsuGO, a process-level reasoning benchmark for evaluating Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning through Go life-and-death problems. These problems provide closed and verifiable solution spaces with an inherent adversarial structure, making candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking necessary parts of reasoning rather than incidental trace patterns. By constraining the solution space, TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search organization, parses CoT into a structured search tree, and reports Search Efficiency together with Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics and visualizations. Experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding the correct candidate earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models still behave much closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not necessarily imply better search. Our results identify search organization and reasoning-resource allocation as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation.

</details>


### [156] [Capability Sheaves for Compositional Agent-Harness Repair: Controlled Quotients and a Real-Repository Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13228)
*Saveliy Batruin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A capability-sheaf framework integrates retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification across agents; exact CSPs and relative cohomology diagnose/invariantize shared state; experiments show invariance to stale representations but no clear real-world cohomological advantage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address disagreement among locally successful components in agent ensembles by enforcing a principled notion of shared state through a capability sheaf and cohomological diagnostics, aiming for invariance and identifiable corrections.

Method: Model state coordination with a capability sheaf: stalks store typed behavior signatures, restriction maps preserve shared fields; acceptance defined by an exact finite CSP; a linearized relative cohomology class provides diagnostics and a search feature; controlled experiments with hidden mediators; quotienting coboundaries; evaluation on PatchFuseBench SWE-bench discovery/abstention gates; comparison against noncohomological selectors.

Result: Exact CSP aligns with the quotient, indicating invariance to stale representatives; discovery gate fails in real-world settings; abstention gate achieves 127/160, tying strong anchor and exceeding matched gate by one issue (p=1.0); overall patch-fix results show limited cohomological advantage and reliance on identifiability corrections.

Conclusion: Controlled invariance and identifiability corrections are supported, but there is no clear, practical cohomological advantage in real-world settings; usefulness may lie in diagnostics and invariant reasoning rather than superior performance.

Abstract: Agent harnesses combine retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification, but locally successful components may disagree on shared state. We model this failure with a finite \emph{capability sheaf}: stalks encode typed behavior signatures, restriction maps retain shared fields, and accepted runs are useful global sections. An exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP) defines acceptance, while a linearized relative cohomology class provides a diagnostic and search feature.
  A controlled experiment over 20 task clusters introduces hidden interior mediators whose raw states are nuisance variables. Quotienting their coboundaries reduces the candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster; aligning the hidden state removes the gap. Exact CSP matches the quotient, so the result demonstrates invariance to stale representatives, not superiority over exact reasoning.
  We then test the method on a discovery split from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool of PatchFuseBench: 160 issues from 20 repositories, 875 real candidate patches, 2,579 source-aware edit atoms, and 153 newly executed patches. A first pool-level construction is constant because $[b-Dx]=[b]$ in $\operatorname{coker}D$ and therefore cannot rank configurations. A candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on 848/875 candidates and varies within 120/160 issues. It resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not supported across repositories (exact sign-flip $p=0.75$). A leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160, tying the strong anchor and exceeding its matched gate by one issue ($p=1.0$). The discovery gate therefore fails and the confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the controlled invariance mechanism and an identifiability correction, but not a real-world cohomological advantage.

</details>


### [157] [vToken: Token-Level Virtualization for Reclaimable KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13263)
*Yuanhang Gao,Xiangrui Yang,Yuanfeng Chen,Hongjia Chen,Qianru Lv,Wenfei Wu,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: vToken introduces a token-level virtualization layer to decouple logical token liveness from physical KV blocks, reducing fragmentation and enabling efficient reclaiming. It preserves PagedAttention/CUDA Graph compatibility and yields notable throughput and concurrency gains with simpler integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In LLM serving, the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size, and block-level memory management causes intra-block fragmentation. Token-level eviction is finer-grained but clashes with fixed-size blocks, leading to unreclaimable memory.

Method: Design and implement vToken as a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that uses a token-table indirection to maintain a stable logical token view while physically reclaiming by repacking live tokens asynchronously. Keeps PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. Implemented in vLLM and evaluated on H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models.

Result: Compared to a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2%–72.3% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37×. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2×, while reducing per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

Conclusion: Token-level virtualization via vToken effectively mitigates fragmentation, improves memory efficiency and throughput, and simplifies integration without sacrificing kernel compatibility.

Abstract: Large language model serving faces a critical memory bottleneck: the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks to reduce allocator-level fragmentation, but recent KV eviction algorithms operate at a token granularity finer than block-level management. This mismatch causes intra-block fragmentation, leaving a large fraction of allocated KV memory unreclaimable. We present vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. vToken maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by repacking live tokens asynchronously. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. We implement vToken in vLLM and evaluate it with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2\%--72.3\% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37$\times$. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2$\times$, while reducing the per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

</details>


### [158] [Sovereign by necessity? Frontier AI export controls, cyber security, and the limits of national AI capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13272)
*Alan Woodward,Andrew Rogoyski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Access to frontier AI is becoming a national security matter, with licensing and export controls used by a subset of states; sovereignty strategies include conditional access, inference-level controls, hedges with open weights, regional pooling, and cyber resilience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain how political economy, governance, and security considerations shape frontier AI development across states of different sizes; assess feasibility of sovereign capability.

Method: Theoretical and qualitative synthesis drawing on training cost data, compute concentration, national AI programs, and market dynamics; proposes a layered governance framework with practical recommendations.

Result: Frontier AI access is increasingly linked to national cyber defense, and sovereignty is variably feasible; open-weight hedges offer leverage but carry political exposure; risk is more about deployment/containment than raw performance.

Conclusion: Advocate a layered strategy: negotiated access, sovereignty at inference, hedging with open models, regional pooling, talent development, and cyber resilience; emphasize careful deployment and containment over matting performance.

Abstract: A small number of firms based in two states produce the most capable frontier AI models. The governments of those states have shown both the legal power and the political will to decide which other countries may use these systems. In June 2026 the United States required a leading developer to obtain licences before releasing its most advanced models to any foreign person, including foreign nationals resident in the United States. The affected models were withdrawn worldwide at short notice, partly because the restriction proved impractical to administer. This followed within months of the first documented case of a largely autonomous, AI-run cyber espionage campaign, and coincided with mounting evidence that frontier models alter the economics of both cyber attack and cyber defence. This article examines how these two developments interact, and situates them within the unusual market dynamics now driving large-scale AI development. It argues that access to frontier AI is becoming part of national cyber defence, that such access can be revoked, and that the obvious remedy of sovereign capability remains only partly feasible for all but a handful of states. Drawing on evidence about training costs, the concentration of computing power and the support offered by national AI programmes, it asks what sovereignty can realistically mean for small and middle powers, and for large powers as well. The article proposes a layered strategy: negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at the level of inference, hedging with open-weight models, pooled regional capability, sustained talent development and continued investment in basic cyber resilience. The open-weight hedge proves at once more capable and more politically exposed than is commonly assumed. Much of the near-term risk lies in how capable models are deployed and contained rather than in their apparent performance.

</details>


### [159] [Towards Context-Aware Clinical Motion Understanding in Daily Living at Home: Freezing of Gait Detection with Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13283)
*Vayalet Stefanova,Diwas Lamsal,Margot Genbrugge,Maxim Yudayev,Christian Schlenstedt,Moran Gilat,Bart Vanrumste,Benjamin Filtjens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMU-based sequence model (TCN) outperforms pretrained ego-video representations for FOG detection in PD during ADLs; ego-video adds contextual information but does not beat IMUs; suggests multimodal context improves clinical motion understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics; FOG in PD is highly contextual and challenging to detect using inertial data alone; egocentric vision could provide task-related context to disambiguate behaviors.

Method: Synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels from 13 PD patients in their homes. Evaluated frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, plus an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation.

Result: IMU-based TCN achieved 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC; V-JEPA2 ego-video features yielded 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC. Ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing but was above chance; qualitative analyses suggest ego-video captures FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs.

Conclusion: Pretrained ego-video representations can augment wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living, indicating value in multimodal approaches that combine contextual vision with inertial sensing.

Abstract: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics, because similar inertial patterns during activities of daily living (ADLs) can reflect intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological movement impairment. Egocentric vision provides task-related context that may help disambiguate these cases. We investigate this challenge through freezing of gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease (PD), a symptom strongly influenced by contextual factors during ADLs. Using synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes, we evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation. The IMU-based TCN achieved the strongest event-detection performance, reaching 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC, compared with 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC for V-JEPA2 ego-video features. Although ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing, it showed above-chance discrimination, and qualitative analyses suggest that egocentric vision may capture FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs. Together, these results support the use of pretrained ego-video representations to add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living.

</details>


### [160] [NAS-Driven Hardware Accelerator Exploration for Edge AI and Quantization Effects on the Pareto Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13293)
*Eleftherios Mylonas,Angelos Kouprizas,Michael Birbas,Alexios Birbas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-search INT4 PTQ effects on NAS-Bench-201 Pareto frontier analyzed; a three-stage hardware-aware NAS+hardware-mapping framework is proposed, with FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforming INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address lack of analysis on PTQ impact within NAS Pareto space and absence of a framework combining quantized architecture mapping with reconfigurable accelerators and automated hardware exploration.

Method: Three-stage pipeline: (1) hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate on NAS-Bench-201; (2) quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control; (3) evolutionary Domain Space Exploration backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Empirical study quantizes all 15,625 architectures to INT4, evaluates stability metrics on ground-truth data, and compares FP32 zero-shot surrogate to an INT4-trained surrogate across two standard search strategies.

Result: INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space; FP32 zero-shot surrogate yields better Pareto-space coverage than an INT4-trained surrogate across both search strategies.

Conclusion: The proposed three-stage, hardware-aware NAS framework supports post-search quantization; results show FP32 surrogates are more robust for Pareto coverage and provide guidance for integrating PTQ into NAS pipelines.

Abstract: Edge AI deployment demands neural architectures that are simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable - a challenge addressed by hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search (NAS). While recent works incorporate quantization directly into the NAS loop, these approaches expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture and quantization design. The simpler post-search quantization strategy has received little analytical attention: the effects of Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) on the NAS-discovered Pareto structure remain uncharacterised, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping onto reconfigurable accelerators with automated hardware exploration. This paper addresses both gaps. First, a three-stage pipeline is proposed: a hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, and an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Second, an empirical study characterises how INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on ground-truth data for all 15,625 architectures, and demonstrates that an FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

</details>


### [161] [StateBridge: Training-free Hidden-state Alignment for Latent Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Training-free latent communication via a closed-form orthogonal transform (StateBridge) aligns sender final-layer hidden states with the receiver's input space, enabling a continuous prefix instead of discrete tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete token bottlenecks in LLM-based multi-agent systems lose information beyond token identity. Latent communication aims to transmit continuous representations, but prior approaches require layer-by-layer memory injections or trained projectors, hurting portability.

Method: StateBridge performs a closed-form orthogonal transformation to align the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space without training. It uses lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring to align with the pretrained input distribution and prepends the aligned states as a continuous prefix to the receiver's input.

Result: Evaluated on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: StateBridge demonstrates a portable, training-free latent communication approach that effectively bridges sender and receiver representations, offering strong empirical gains across diverse tasks without requiring model-specific adapters.

Abstract: Large language model based multi-agent systems usually communicate in text, i.e., using discrete tokens. However, text introduces a discrete bottleneck. Converting the sender's continuous hidden states into discrete tokens discards information that token identities alone cannot capture. Recent work proposes latent communication as an alternative, where agents transmit hidden representations directly without converting them to text. However, existing latent methods either inject working memory layer by layer across the transformers, or require trained projectors that limit portability. We propose StateBridge, a training-free latent communication approach that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation. Lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended to the input of the receiver agent as a continuous prefix. We evaluate StateBridge on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [162] [LLM-Guided Graph Generation for Structure-Based Local Improvement Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13333)
*Hai Xia,Vaidyanathan Peruvemba Ramaswamy,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-driven automatic graph-based pipeline for problem-agnostic variable selection in constraint optimization, improving performance on MiniZinc instances and outperforming a one-shot Gurobi baseline and single configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Create a universal, structure-aware variable-selection mechanism that generalizes across diverse constraint problems by using LLM-specified graph representations to extract features for configuration and selection.

Method: Prompt an LLM with semantic guidelines to generate a graph generator that maps any problem instance to a uniform weighted graph (nodes = decision variables, edges = constraint relations). Use these problem-agnostic graphs within a structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) for variable selection. Extract graph features for configuration selection, enabling shared representations across instances. Evaluate on 20 MiniZinc competition problems, comparing algorithm selection against a one-shot Gurobi baseline and against best single configurations; perform ablation studies on configuration and features.

Result: Algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate over a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration at 19.3%. Configuration and feature ablation boost performance to 44.0%.

Conclusion: LLM-guided semantic generation enables effective automated extraction of structure and features for constraint optimization, supporting a problem-agnostic pipeline that improves performance across diverse MiniZinc instances.

Abstract: Large neighborhood search normally selects a random subset of decision variables for iterative optimization. For efficiently solving different problems, researchers tend to design variable selection strategies by taking into account structural features from different domains. In this paper, we build an automatic pipeline that is problem-agnostic to all problems in the MiniZinc format. By prompting an LLM with our semantic guidelines, we guide the LLM to produce a graph generator that maps any instance of a problem type to a uniform weighted graph, where nodes represent decision variables and edges represent constraint relationships. These problem-agnostic graphs guide our structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) in variable selection. Meanwhile, the weighted graph enables all problem instances to share the same generic graph representation, from which the same graph features can be extracted and used for configuration selection. We evaluated our pipeline on instances across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, finding that algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation boost the performance further to 44.0%, demonstrating that LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization.

</details>


### [163] [LongEarth-R1: Benchmarking and Aligning Vision-Language Models for Long-Horizon Earth Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13344)
*Yupan Ding,Jing Xiao,Zhenyuan Zhang,Chaofeng Chen,Liang Liao,Gui-Song Xia,Mi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces LongEarth-Bench, a long-horizon Earth observation benchmark with ~120k QA samples from 117k images, featuring multi-frame sequences and 12 tasks; includes a 30k-sample structured-trace subset; LongEarth and LongEarth-R1 achieve strong performance on long-sequence tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap where remote-sensing vision-language models mainly handle isolated images or short sequences, hindering reliable grounding across frames and regions in extended EO sequences.

Method: Construct LongEarth-Bench from 117k unique images, with average sequence length ~15 frames (up to 30). Cover 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. Provide a 30k-sample subset with structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. Train via supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Extend to LongEarth-R1 by applying group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards.

Result: LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks and remains competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured reasoning, combined with policy-optimization for long-horizon rewards, yields strong performance on long-sequence EO tasks and that the LongEarth framework offers a scalable benchmark for grounding across extended image sequences.

Abstract: Long-horizon Earth observation reasoning requires models to organize multi-stage geographic evolution, localize spatial changes, detect temporal anomalies, and infer future from extended image sequences. However, existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting reliable grounding in the relevant frames and regions. We introduce LongEarth-Bench, a benchmark containing approximately 120k question-answering samples derived from 117k unique images. Its sequences average 15.14 frames and extend to 30 frames, covering 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset further provides structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. We develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Building on LongEarth, LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards. LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

</details>


### [164] [Rules or Character? Scaling Laws for AI Safety Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13345)
*Satoshi Takahashi,Nobuji Kouno,Masaaki Komatsu,Ryuji Hamamoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A stylized model analyzes how to allocate AI safety resources between shaping behavior during training and enforcing rules at inference, showing a weak shift toward shaping with scale and a dominant role for initial character fragility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide formal guidance on how to balance training-time character shaping versus inference-time rule enforcement as AI deployment scales increase, accounting for distributional shift and fragility.

Method: Comparative-statics model with a resource-allocation parameter alpha in [0,1], incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility. Uses a multiplicative Pareto damage model to derive closed-form expected harm and Monte Carlo CVaR for tail risk, analyzed across three deployment scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic).

Result: Optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary; as deployment scale T grows, alpha* shifts weakly toward character shaping (Delta alpha* in [0.01,0.21] depending on scenario). The dominant driver is the baseline character fragility p^(0)_frag, which can move alpha* by ~0.50 across its range, far surpassing effects of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failures. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T.

Conclusion: Safety-architecture decisions depend more on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift than on deployment scale per se; reducing fragility improves optimal balance more than scaling alone.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) safety systems combine character shaping (e.g., Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback [RLHF], Constitutional AI), which modifies behavioral distributions at training time, with rule enforcement (e.g., output filters, safety classifiers), which blocks harmful outputs at inference time, yet little formal analysis exists on how their optimal balance should change as deployment scales increase. We introduce a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as a resource allocation alpha in [0,1] between these two approaches, incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility -- the risk that shaped behavior degrades or collapses under novel conditions. Under a multiplicative Pareto damage model, we derive closed-form expected harm and supplement it with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation. Across three scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic), the optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary and shifts weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale T grows, from negligible (Delta alpha* = +0.01) to pronounced (Delta alpha* = +0.21) depending on scenario. The dominant parameter is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by 0.50 across its range -- far exceeding the effect of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T. These results suggest that safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale per se than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift.

</details>


### [165] [TopoIntent: Compiling Security Intent into Executable, Compliance-Checked Network Topologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13389)
*Xiaokang Qu,Jianliang Ma,Zao Fan,Tianshu Chu,Tianlong Fan,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoIntent automates translating security intent into executable, compliant network topologies, via schema contracts, template retrieval, staged fusion, and Mininet export.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No public benchmark exists for requirement-to-topology generation; existing NetOps tools lack support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements.

Method: Uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion, checks topology against CIS Controls v8.1.2, marks unresolved cases for manual review, performs additive schema-preserving repairs, and exports to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs.

Result: Additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in under 1.5 rounds on average; one feedback round raises post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

Conclusion: TopoIntent enables executable, compliance-checked security topologies from underspecified intents and provides a dataset for evaluation, with demonstrated improvements in CIS satisfaction and ACL policy outcomes on held-out finance/government scenarios.

Abstract: Enterprise security topology design requires translating business intent, regulatory requirements, and risk assumptions into zones, boundary devices, inter-zone paths, and access-control policies. Existing NetOps automation tools mainly operate after this design is fixed, providing limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements. We present TopoIntent, a system that compiles security intent into executable, compliance-checked network topologies. It uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, and applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. The generated topology is checked against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards visible at the topology layer, while unresolved cases are marked for manual review. Structural gaps are repaired through additive schema-preserving edits. The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [166] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Transformer-based model (TRACE) jointly predicts a student’s upcoming semester course set and grades, capturing course concurrency to improve accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing predictive models simplify student history as a single sequence and ignore concurrent courses in a semester, leading to underestimation of performance for heavy/challenging course loads. Modeling course concurrency can improve prediction quality and support early detection systems in higher education.

Method: TRACE encodes courses per semester to capture concurrency, and uses a joint loss that combines course-set prediction with grade prediction. Trains a Transformer-based architecture on ten years of institutional data, comparing against grade-only baselines, LSTM, and graph neural networks. The model allows easy incorporation of student attributes and is adaptable to new institutions via retraining/calibration.

Result: Joint prediction of courses and grades yields ~50% reduction in mean absolute error compared to an identical architecture predicting grades alone. Outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models and graph neural networks.

Conclusion: Modern neural architectures like TRACE can create interpretable, adaptable models for predicting semester performance when course concurrency is modeled. The approach supports integration into early warning systems and can be retrained/calibrated for new institutions.

Abstract: Existing predictive models in learning analytics often treat student academic history as a simple sequence, overlooking the concurrent nature of courses taken within a semester. This simplification can lead to inaccurate performance predictions, particularly for students with heavy or challenging course loads. This paper introduces a TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation (TRACE) that addresses this limitation by jointly predicting both the set of courses a student will take and their corresponding grades for an upcoming semester. Our approach encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture the effects of course concurrency and utilizes a novel loss function combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. We demonstrate that predicting courses taken in addition to the grades in those courses leads to significant improvements in prediction quality. Trained on ten years of institutional data, our joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to an identical architecture that predicts grades alone. The model also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models, as well as graph neural network-based approaches, and offers natural ways to incorporate student attribute data. This work demonstrates the utility of modern neural architectures for creating interpretable models that can be adapted to new institutions via retraining and recalibration, as well as the importance of key techniques, such as predicting courses taken during training. We discuss how this model could be incorporated into early detection systems at institutions of higher education.

</details>


### [167] [Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)
*Mirko Tritella,Riccardo Pozzi,Matteo Palmonari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAG system ParliamentRAG for the Italian Chamber of Deputies mitigates three risks in parliament-scale retrieval: speaker dominance, topical-expertise weighting, and citation misattribution. It uses a topic-dependent authority model to weigh speakers by query context, retrieves relevant chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across groups, and generates summaries with supporting quotations. Evaluated vs Google NotebookLM on 15 topics; shows higher coverage and faithful quotations, with expert-preference signals; NotebookLM excels in prose quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parliamentary proceedings are voluminous and fragmented, hindering multi-perspective access for citizens, journalists, and researchers. When applying RAG to transcripts, risks include dominance by frequent speakers, inability to weight expertise by topic, and potential citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. A system that jointly addresses these risks can provide balanced, expert-informed summaries of parliamentary debates.

Method: Introduce a topic-dependent authority model combining interpretable speaker features (profession, education, prior interventions) to estimate speaker authority conditioned on the user query. The system retrieves relevant speech chunks, ranks topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesis with supporting quotations from identified experts.

Result: ParliamentRAG was evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics using a two-level protocol: automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. ParliamentRAG achieved higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95) and perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and showed stronger expert-preference signals on source-related dimensions. NotebookLM outperformed ParliamentRAG on prose-oriented dimensions.

Conclusion: Incorporating a query-conditioned authority model improves multi-perspective coverage and quotation fidelity in parliamentary RAG systems, enabling more balanced, expert-informed summaries across groups. ParliamentRAG demonstrates clear advantages over a general-purpose LLM for source-aware retrieval, though prose quality may still lag behind specialized systems for narrative generation.

Abstract: Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

</details>


### [168] [Beyond Final Scores: A Systematic Evaluation of Agents for Long-Horizon AI Research and Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13417)
*Yiwei Li,Wanli Yang,Hexiang Tan,Xiangzhou Huang,Zhengyu Chen,Ziran Li,Borun Chen,Shanglin Lei,Huaisheng Zhu,Hao Tian,Fei Sun,Xunliang Cai,Jingang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks using a rule-based framework (Solution Framing, Execution, Feedback Control) and controlled experience-reuse comparisons reveals that current models function more as engineering optimizers than autonomous researchers; progress is variable across runs and genuine methodological novelty is rare.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the current limits and sources of progress in long-horizon autonomous agents beyond final scores, by diagnosing where experience, design, and decision-making contribute to performance.

Method: 7 frontier models evaluated on 36 long-horizon tasks using a new framework with rule-based metrics for within-run behavior (Solution Framing, Execution, Feedback Control) and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks.

Result: Agents can formulate and implement practical solutions but exhibit substantial run-to-run variability; strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques; genuine methodological novelty is rare. Performance is shaped by process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect stability.

Conclusion: Concrete directions for improvement include better model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design to stabilize performance and enable more genuine methodological advances.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of improving models, systems, and other technical artifacts through long-horizon experimentation. To understand the current state of this capability, however, evaluation must go beyond final scores, which neither reveal where progress is gained or lost nor indicate whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. We therefore present a systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks based on a new framework that uses rule-based metrics to characterize within-run behavior through Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. The results show that current agents operate more like engineering optimizers than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but their performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Detailed analysis reveals that observed performance is shaped by multiple factors, including distinct process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect performance stability. These findings suggest concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design.

</details>


### [169] [Enhancing Virtual Agents through SLMs and Edge-Computing: An Exploratory Evaluation of Think and Memory Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13420)
*Aimilios Hadjiliasi,Louis Nisiotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge-based Small Language Models are used to support selected CEAA components (Think and Memory) in an edge gateway for embodied agents in virtual worlds; evaluated on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX with Qwen2.5; demonstrated a partial CEAA implementation with routing, memory-read, and latency metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building cognitively capable, persistent agents in complex virtual/Metaverse environments is challenging; leveraging CEAA and edge-capable Small Language Models can enable efficient on-device cognition, enabling perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and action at the edge.

Method: Developed an edge-based virtual agent gateway system; deployed Qwen2.5 models of varying sizes on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX; evaluated service-request processing and memory-driven conversations; focused on Think and Memory components; conducted simulation experiments measuring routing accuracy, memory-read latency, and overall latency.

Result: SLM-driven prototype partially implements selected CEAA processes, demonstrating feasibility of edge-based cognitive orchestration for embodied agents; empirical results show measurable routing, memory-read performance, and latency characteristics supporting on-edge operation.

Conclusion: Edge-optimized SLMs can support selected CEAA components, enabling context-aware, persistent agents in immersive virtual worlds; future work should extend to remaining CEAA processes, optimize latency/energy, and validate scalability across more complex scenarios.

Abstract: Embodied intelligent virtual agents are expected to operate as persistent, adaptive, and context-aware entities within complex virtual and Metaverse worlds. However, implementing cognitively capable agents in such environments is conceptually and technologically challenging. Among a range of blueprints and development approaches, the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) has been developed as an implementation-oriented framework for architecting components of perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and embodied action. Considering the recent advances in edge computing and generative AI language models, this paper explores the use of Small Language Models (SLMs) to support edge-based operation of selected CEAA components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" as processes central to cognitive orchestration and persistence of virtual agents in interactive virtual worlds. An edge-based virtual agent gateway system was developed and evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 models of different sizes, exploring the system's capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations. A series of simulation experiments evaluated routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, demonstrating an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements selected CEAA processes to support the development of embodied agents whose cognitive "brain" can operate efficiently and contextually for interactive experiences in immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [170] [RAIL: An Automatic Classifier of the Artificial Intelligence Readiness Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428)
*Juan Irving Vasquez,Juan Terven,Laura-Ivoone Garay-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Unifies AI readiness assessment into a nine-level AIRL scale with evidence ladder and dimensional caps, plus RAIL, a panel-of-LLMs classifier, to produce decidable readiness judgments; tested on several works for consistency and avoiding monolithic overestimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current readiness frameworks for AI are heterogeneous and not easily automatable; gaps in AI-specific gating, access to artifacts, and comparability hinder decision making.

Method: Introduce AIRL: a nine-level ordinal scale with environmental evidence ladder and caps on specs, data viability, legality, expert knowledge, and algorithm maturity; generality-anchoring rule and assignment disciplines. Introduce RAIL: independent LLM agents (one evidence agent + six dimension agents) with a deterministic aggregation rule and chief expert oversight; refusal to exceed caps.

Result: Applied to multiple research works; demonstrated consistency and no overestimation relative to monolithic LLM classifiers.

Conclusion: AIRL + RAIL provide a decidable, comparable readiness framework for AI projects, enabling better investment, management, and policy oversight; the approach supports automatic or semi-automatic assessment from natural-language descriptions.

Abstract: Assessing the maturity of artificial intelligence technologies is essential for investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring, yet the available readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: the adaptation of Technology Readiness Levels to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, the Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels presuppose access to internal process artifacts, and AI/data readiness dimension models employ scales that resist direct comparison. This paper makes two contributions. First, we unify these three frameworks into the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale built on an environmental evidence ladder and complemented by dimensional caps (covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity) together with a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines, so that a readiness level becomes decidable from a natural-language description of the work alone. Second, we propose RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), a panel-of-experts classifier that operationalizes the scale: one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, each a large language model with a narrowly scoped mandate, deliver verdicts that a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews under asymmetric authority, confirming or lowering the panel's recommendation but never raising it above the caps. The method was tested in the analysis of several research works showing consistency and avoiding overestimation from monolithic LLM classifiers.

</details>
