### [171] [Academic League of Artificial Intelligence - An Integrative Perspective of Teaching, Research, and Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13447)
*Alison R. Panisson,Maria Eduarda W. M. Vianna,Italo Firmino da Silva,Heitor Henrique da Silva,Rafaela Fernandes Savaris,Bernardo Pandolfi Costa,Martin Augusto Gagliotti Vigil,Jim Lau,Agenor Hentz,Andréa Sabedra Bordin,Alexandre Leopoldo Gonçalves,Roberto Rodrigues-Filho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A replicable, student-centered framework for organizing academic leagues that integrates teaching, research, and extension at UFSC's LIA, demonstrated through diverse initiatives and intended to guide similar organizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To promote extracurricular education and strengthen university-society integration by uniting teaching, research, and extension under a democratic, project-based, student-led framework.

Method: Describe a dynamic organizational framework combining democratic governance, collaborative learning, and project-driven activity organization; illustrate with representative initiatives (competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, AI-powered socially impactful applications).

Result: The framework provides a flexible, replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, with practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations; demonstrated through diverse projects that foster leadership, scientific output, community engagement, and knowledge preservation.

Conclusion: The proposed framework is a viable blueprint for integrating teaching, research, and extension in engineering and computing education, adaptable to other universities and similar student groups.

Abstract: Academic leagues have become important mechanisms for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening the integration between universities and society. This paper presents the organizational framework adopted by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence (LIA) at the Federal University of Santa Catarina (UFSC), designed to integrate teaching, research, and university extension through a student-centered, project-based approach. The framework combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization to foster both technical and transversal competencies. The framework is illustrated through representative initiatives, including competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications with social impact. These projects demonstrate how diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities can be developed within a common organizational structure while promoting leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The reported experience indicates that the proposed framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

</details>


### [172] [A Unifying Perspective on Causal World Models: From Observations to Representations to Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13456)
*Avinash Kori,Fabrizio Russo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Causal World Models (CWMs), a multi-level, causality-aware framework for world modeling that integrates entity properties, interactions, and environment dynamics, connecting WM with causal representation/discovery and clarifying identifiability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models must do more than generate data; they should capture causal structure to explain dynamics and support planning under distribution shifts.

Method: Formal definition of CWMs anchored in tasks; situates CWMs among causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, SCMs, and model-based decision-making; discusses abstraction levels from perceptual observations to causal structure; analyzes identifiability and equivalence.

Result: Provides a theoretical framework and connections to existing literature; clarifies when WM components can be recovered from data up to identifiable equivalence; grounds WM in causal representations to support reasoning and decision-making.

Conclusion: CWMs offer representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making; identify identifiability limits; guides future WM design integrating causality.

Abstract: World Models (WM) are increasingly seen as a foundation for intelligent agents that can predict, plan, and act beyond their training distribution. In this paper, we study WMs from a causal perspective across multiple levels of abstraction, ranging from perceptual observations to building a conceptual representation of the structure governing the environment dynamics. We argue that useful WMs must go beyond generative capabilities alone: they should also capture entity properties, entity-to-entity interactions, and entity-to-environment interactions that determine and explain the dynamics of a system. We provide a formal definition of Causal WMs (CWMs) grounded in the tasks they are intended to support, connecting world modelling with existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making. Finally, we relate CWMs to the literature on identifiability, clarifying when the components of a WM can be recovered from data and up to which equivalence. With this, we ground WMs in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

</details>


### [173] [MARC v1: An Open-Source Multi-Agent Framework for Clinical AI Reasoning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13476)
*Saisha Shetty,Satvik Tripathi,Austin Lin,Colin Zhao,Theodore Kim,Don Enwerem,Jacinta Arnold,Shahriar Faghani,Tessa S Cook*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARC is an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic, multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning. It features role-specialized agents (extraction, reasoning, answer generation, evaluation), explicit context passing, and traceable intermediate outputs for stage-wise failure attribution. It introduces a Decomposer module to auto-generate task-specific prompts from plain language, and is configurable via YAML for API or local CPU deployments. The framework aims to be model-agnostic, interpretable, and accessible to clinicians without programming.



<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the limitations of single-prompt, monolithic LLM reasoning in clinical settings, where interpretability, traceability, and reliable failure attribution are critical. It also aims to reduce manual prompt engineering and support accessible deployment options for practitioners outside of programming, while maintaining model-agnostic compatibility.

Method: Architect a multi-agent system with role-specialized components: extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation. Implement explicit context passing and maintain traceable intermediate outputs to enable stage-wise failure attribution. Add a Decomposer module that converts plain-language task descriptions into task-specific, agent prompts. Provide YAML-based configurability and support both API-based and local CPU deployments, ensuring no code changes are required to use the framework.

Result: Proposes a modular, deterministic framework (MARC) that orchestrates multiple agents for clinical reasoning, with a Decomposer for automatic prompt generation and YAML-based configuration to enable deployability across environments. Emphasizes traceability and interpretable outputs.

Conclusion: MARC presents a model-agnostic, interpretable, and clinician-friendly framework for distributed LLM reasoning, with open-source availability and no requirement for programming expertise or code modifications. It promises improved failure attribution and configurability, potentially enhancing reliability in clinical NLP tasks.

Abstract: We present Multi-Agent Reasoning and Coordination (MARC), an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning. MARC coordinates role-specialized agents for extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation, with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs, enabling stage-wise failure attribution. We additionally introduce a Decomposer module that generates task-specific agent prompts from a plain-language description, eliminating manual prompt engineering. The framework supports both API-based and local CPU-compatible deployments and is entirely configurable via YAML, without code modifications. MARC is designed to be model-agnostic, interpretable, and accessible to clinical domain experts without programming expertise. The full framework is available at https://github.com/Penn-RAIL/MARC-v1.

</details>


### [174] [AlayaWorld: Interactive Long-Horizon World Modeling - Full Technical Report (v1.1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13492)
*AlayaWorld Team,Kaipeng Zhang,Chuanhao Li,Yifan Zhan,Yongtao Ge,Yuanyang Yin,Jiaming Tan,Kang He,Liaoyuan Fan,Mingliang Zhai,Ruicong Liu,Xiaojie Xu,Xuangeng Chu,Zhen Li,Zhengyuan Lin,Zhixiang Wang,Zian Meng,Zihui Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An improved AlayaWorld focusing on conditioning alignment via six changes: motion-aware latent conditioning, causal encoding of memory, pixel-space temporal alignment, hard memory dropout, unified VAE protocol, and removal of camera AdaLN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce mismatch between conditioning signals and generated content by redesigning conditioning representation and integration, ensuring latent and temporal structures are aligned with the produced video while preserving the backbone and training data.

Method: Introduce six modifications: (1) replace static-frame conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encode re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) align the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) adopt hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens instead of zeroing them; (5) unify VAE encoding/decoding protocol across training and inference; (6) remove the camera AdaLN branch so viewpoint control comes from the re-rendered spatial condition. Additionally, keep the backbone architecture, chunk-wise autoregressive generation, and training data unchanged, and replace depth-warping-based spatial memory with a streaming 3D point-cache renderer.

Result: The abstract describes methodological changes and design goals but does not report empirical results; it outlines expected improvements in conditioning fidelity and temporal coherence.

Conclusion: By aligning conditioning signals with generated content in both latent and temporal dimensions through these six changes, AlayaWorld aims to achieve more faithful, temporally coherent video generation while preserving the existing backbone and data.

Abstract: This report presents an improved version of AlayaWorld. While the backbone architecture, chunk-wise autoregressive generation scheme, and training data remain unchanged from the previous release, we substantially revise how conditioning signals are represented and integrated into the model. The new design is guided by a simple principle: conditioning signals should match the generated content as closely as possible in both latent representation and temporal structure. To this end, we make two major changes. First, we replace the previous depth-warping-based spatial memory with a streaming 3D point-cache renderer. Second, we redesign the conditioning pipeline so that visual conditions are encoded in the same causal-VAE latent space, with temporal statistics consistent with those of the generated video. Concretely, the new version introduces six modifications: (1) replacing static-frame image conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encoding re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) aligning the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) adopting hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens rather than zeroing them; (5) unifying the VAE encoding and decoding protocol across training and inference; and (6) removing the camera AdaLN branch, such that viewpoint control is provided entirely through the re-rendered spatial condition.

</details>


### [175] [QuoteBench: How Matched Scores Can Hide Command-Path Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13547)
*Shangao Li,Yao Zhang,Volker Tresp,Yuanyuan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuoteBench exposes a boundary between model-generated commands and the execution/parsing layer by exact final-state validation across 56 tasks from 14 families, revealing that matching generation scores can severely misrepresent reliability when an added parser or encoding changes outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish failures arising from model generation from those introduced by down-stream execution/serialization in LLM-based command agents, and to remedy the overreliance on matched scores.

Method: A boundary-aware benchmark (QuoteBench) using exact final-state validation on 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families. It introduces a deliberately unescaped added parser around command execution, then shows that escaping at the interpolation point reproduces raw-path outcomes. Replaying the same reply through the added parser across eight configurations assesses boundary adaptation by comparing raw generation vs. results under modified execution paths.

Result: Replaying with the added parser reduces success by 55.4–73.2 percentage points; disclosures recover 30.4–60.7 points for six configurations, with zero or slight negative recovery for two. Raw generation is nearly saturated; boundary adaptation remains the differentiator. GPT-5.6-sol’s gap appears small (-3.6) but hides large net damage (-64.3) offset by compensation (+60.7). Deployment configuration can reorder model performance, with 1 clear reversal among 26 pairs and four more near margins.

Conclusion: Evaluation of command-issuing agents should report model configuration, generation contract, execution path, operating point, and final-state validation rather than rely on matched scores as intrinsic model properties; boundary-aware reporting is essential for fair comparison and understanding of model behavior in real deployment.

Abstract: LLM coding agents issue Bash commands through interfaces that may serialize, wrap, and reparse model output. Matched execution scores alone cannot distinguish command-generation errors from failures introduced after generation. QuoteBench measures this boundary with exact final-state validation on 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families, crossing the generation contract with the execution transport around one deliberately unescaped added parser. Escaping at the interpolation point reproduces each replayed reply's raw-path outcome, so any recovery under a disclosed boundary must come from the model changing its generation. Across eight same-window configurations, replaying the same reply through the added parser lowers success by 55.4 to 73.2 percentage points; disclosure recovers 30.4 to 60.7 points for six configurations, and zero or slightly negative for the other two. Raw generation is nearly saturated at the frontier; boundary adaptation is what still separates models. GPT-5.6-sol's matched gap of -3.6 points hides -64.3 points of damage and +60.7 points of compensation. The deployment configuration reorders models: one reversal among 26 comparable pairs is unambiguous and four more sit on single-task margins. Evaluations of command-issuing agents should report the model configuration, generation contract, execution path, operating point, and final-state validator rather than treat a matched score as an intrinsic model property.

</details>


### [176] [OmniScientist: An Omni-Modal Omni-Discipline AI Scientist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13558)
*Bobo Li,Hao Fei,Tianjie Ju,Mong-Li Lee,Wynne Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniScientist is an end-to-end omni-modal AI scientist that uses a perception layer and three autonomous agents (ideation, experiment, writeup) to reason over heterogeneous raw evidence across modalities, enabling lifecycle-wide, evidence-grounded scientific discovery; evaluated on 36 real-data cases with strong performance gains over a precomputed-scalar baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI research workflows rely on partial representations (text, code, labels, precomputed summaries) that miss crucial spatial, temporal, cross-modal, and procedural relationships; there is a need for lifecycle-wide perception to connect observations to research questions, experiments, and claims.

Method: Proposes an end-to-end pipeline with a perception layer and three autonomous agents operating in a deterministic pipeline. It supports multiple disciplines, modalities, and evidence types; implements idea, rigour, and claim checks in code to enforce novelty screening, statistical validity, provenance, and traceability; evaluates on 36 real-data cases across 5 discipline families and 4 evidence families, including images, signals, audio, video, 3D structures, trajectories, tables, formulae, graphs; completes from raw data to manuscript.

Result: Mean overall paper score of 6.3 with the reference reasoning backbone; paired blind variant with precomputed scalar features shows direct perception improves all 7 evaluation dimensions and wins 85% of head-to-head judgments; demonstrates lifecycle-wide perception is essential and feasible.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-wide perception is essential for evidence-grounded scientific discovery and provides a practical path toward broadly capable AI scientists.

Abstract: Recent advances in foundation models have enabled AI scientists to automate increasingly complete research workflows, from hypothesis generation and code execution to manuscript preparation. Yet workflow coverage alone does not provide access to the full evidence on which scientific discovery depends. Existing systems typically reason over text, code, labels, or precomputed summaries, leaving scientifically decisive spatial, temporal, cross-channel, and procedural relations unavailable to the agent. We introduce OmniScientist, an end-to-end, omni-modal AI scientist that conducts multidisciplinary research directly from heterogeneous raw evidence. A perception layer and 3 autonomous agents for ideation, experiment, and writeup operate within a deterministic pipeline, allowing observations to shape research questions, experimental decisions, and final claims throughout the research lifecycle. By running idea, rigour, and claim checks in code, the system enforces novelty screening, statistical validity, execution provenance, and numerical traceability. We evaluate OmniScientist on 36 real-data cases spanning 5 discipline families, 4 families of scientific evidence, and modalities including images, signals, audio, video, 3-D structures, trajectories, tables, formulae, and graphs. The system completes the full path from raw data to a compiled manuscript in all 36 cases and achieves a mean overall paper score of 6.3 with the reference reasoning backbone. In paired comparisons against a blind variant that receives only precomputed scalar features, direct perception improves all 7 evaluation dimensions and wins 85% of head-to-head judgments. These results show that lifecycle-wide perception is essential for evidence-grounded scientific discovery and provides a practical path toward broadly capable AI scientists.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [177] [LoKiFormer: Locality-aware Attention with Decoupled Knowledge Memory for Efficient Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12419)
*Qiuwu Chen,Zimo Liu,Yuchen Li,Ying Sun,Yifan Zhang,Zhijie Qiu,Zeng You,Ryan Dong,Simeng Ma,Yaofo Chen,Mingkui Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoKiFormer augments the decoder with Local Fusion Attention (LFA) and Knowledge Memory Module (KMM) to inject locality and decouple global knowledge storage from computation, achieving faster pretraining convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard decoders suffer from (i) lack of explicit locality inductive bias in self-attention, leading to redundant local modeling; (ii) mixture-of-experts coupling storage with computation, hindering flexible access to global knowledge. The work aims to incorporate local patterns and addressable global knowledge to improve efficiency and knowledge integration during pretraining.

Method: Propose LoKiFormer: a decoder-based architecture enhanced with (1) Local Fusion Attention (LFA) that uses convolutional fusion within attention to capture local patterns; (2) Knowledge Memory Module (KMM) featuring a parametric key–value memory for global knowledge stored in addressable slots, decoupling storage from computation and enabling direct retrieval.

Result: Empirical evaluation reports 1.33x faster convergence in pretraining compared with baseline models, indicating improved training efficiency.

Conclusion: LoKiFormer demonstrates more efficient and effective integration of information at local and global levels, suggesting superiority over conventional LLM architectures in pretraining efficiency and knowledge access.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable breakthroughs across various applications. However, their architectures remain inefficient in pretraining due to two main limitations: (i) self-attention lacks an explicit inductive bias for locality, leading to redundant modeling of sequence-internal local information; (ii) mixture-of-experts (MoE) implicitly couples knowledge storage with computational pathways, hindering flexible access to sequence-external global knowledge. To overcome these limitations, we propose LoKiFormer, a novel LLM architecture that augments the standard decoder with two dedicated modules: 1) Local Fusion Attention (LFA), which incorporates a convolutional fusion to attention, explicitly capturing local patterns and allowing the attention to operate on more informative representations; 2) Knowledge Memory Module (KMM), which introduces a parametric key-value memory that explicitly stores global knowledge in addressable slots, decoupling storage from computation and enabling direct knowledge retrieval. Together, these modules enable LoKiFormer to achieve more efficient and effective integration of information at both levels. Experimental results show that LoKiFormer converges 1.33x faster in pre-training than baseline models, underscoring its superiority over existing LLM architectures.

</details>


### [178] [Which Site, and When: A Free-Satellite-Data Test of Himalayan Glacial Lake Bursts, Landslides, and Ice Floods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12422)
*Matthew Kahn,Milan Arjel,Nirmala Adhikari,Mingmar Sherpa,James Pope*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Free-data evaluation of hazard prediction for glacial-lake outbursts across the Nepal Himalaya; three hazards tested; simple baselines often match or beat complex models; antecedent weather signals improve timing; results are region-sensitive; the output is a Nepal-focused watchlist rather than a universal predictor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap identified by prior work: while deformation and weather signals indicate risk, there is no robust predictive model using freely available data. The study aims to develop and compare models that predict which site is susceptible and when a trigger occurs, across multiple glacial-lake hazards, using public data.

Method: Three hazards (morainic/ice-dammed bursts, rainfall-triggered landslides, pond-origin floods) are evaluated with two questions per hazard. The dataset comprises 589 dated outbursts from HMAGLOFDB and several thousand landslides. For each event, the authors match to similar but unfailed sites. They employ map-tile cross-validation that withholds entire tiles to avoid spatial leakage. They compare five deep-learning models against a gradient-boosted baseline, and also test a simple three-rule decision tree for lake hazards. Performance is assessed with ROC statistics. A Nepal-specific signal is analyzed, and a ranked watchlist is produced.

Result: Antecedent weather times the trigger with ROC around 0.73 for large bursts, 0.83 for landslides, and 0.82 for small floods. Terrain-based susceptibility is overstated by naive scoring (near 0.9) due to clustering of failures in wetter zones; when matched against comparable nearby sites, honest performance drops to 0.76 (bursts), 0.71 (landslides), and 0.54 (small floods). The burst signal remains strong within single regions (ROC 0.89 in Nepal). Five deep-learning models do not decisively outperform a simple gradient-boosted baseline; marginal gains for landslides are not robust. For lake hazards, the baseline (three-rule decision tree using ruggedness and monsoon rainfall) wins outright. The study concludes with a Nepal watchlist for prioritization rather than universal prediction and notes the limits of free data for predictive modeling.

Conclusion: The work provides a practical prioritization tool (Nepal watchlist) rather than a generalizable predictive model. It underscores the limits of free satellite data for multi-hazard prediction and shows that, in this setting, simple baselines can outperform complex models. It also highlights region-specific performance and the need for careful evaluation to avoid inflated metrics due to spatial and data clustering.

Abstract: Two free satellite signals carry real information about glacial-lake outburst risk in the Nepal Himalaya: radar interferometry sees a moraine dam slowly sagging, and satellite weather marks the weeks when a primed lake is under stress. A companion feasibility study found that deformation indicates which lake is destabilizing and weather indicates when it is at risk, but proposed no predictive model. To address this gap, we propose and evaluate models that predict which site is susceptible and when a trigger arrives. We test three related hazards on free data alone: large moraine- and ice-dammed bursts, rainfall-triggered landslides, and smaller floods from ponds on and around a glacier. Each hazard gets two questions, never blended. Using 589 dated outbursts from HMAGLOFDB and several thousand catalogued landslides, we match each event against similar but unfailed sites, and hold every model to a strong simple baseline under spatial cross-validation that withholds whole map tiles, so no model succeeds by recognising a trained-on neighbourhood. Antecedent weather times the trigger at ROC 0.73 for big bursts, 0.83 for landslides, and 0.82 for small floods. Terrain ranks susceptibility only in part: scored naively it appears near 0.9, largely because catalogued failures cluster in wetter ranges; matched against comparable nearby sites the honest figures are 0.76, 0.71, and 0.54 (no better than chance). The burst signal holds within single regions, reaching 0.89 in Nepal alone. Five deep-learning models do not decisively beat a simple gradient-boosted baseline. Three score marginally higher on landslides, a hint too small to confirm. For the lake hazards the baseline wins outright, reproduced by a three-rule decision tree on ruggedness and monsoon rainfall. We close with a ranked Nepal watchlist, a prioritisation aid, not a prediction, and note where free data reaches its limits.

</details>


### [179] [MARCH: Scaling Recurrent Memory with Content-Routed State Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12435)
*Ming Zhang,Kaisen Yang,Shu Yu,Ermo Hua,Ning Ding,Xia Hu,Bowen Zhou,Chaochao Lu,Youbang Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MARCH introduces memory-anchored routing to scale state-space models beyond fixed dimensions by caching recurrent-state anchors with content-conditioned keys, enabling attention over historical anchors for long-context processing while maintaining efficiency; pretrained, it outperforms linear attention baselines on several long-range tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context models struggle to retain early history efficiently. Pure transformers have quadratic training costs and linear caching at inference, while recurrent models risk overwriting earlier information. A scalable, memory-rich architecture is needed to improve recall without sacrificing efficiency.

Method: MARCH caches cumulative recurrent-state checkpoints as state anchors. Each anchor has a compact, content-conditioned key. A memory bank grows with context length. At each token, an anchor query attends all available anchors, and the output is an attention-like aggregation over historical anchors along the current state.

Result: After standard pretraining, MARCH consistently outperforms multiple linear attention variants on commonsense reasoning, LongBench, and in-context retrieval benchmarks.

Conclusion: Content-routed state caching via memory anchors strengthens recurrent long-range memory while preserving the native computation path and allowing a controllable trade-off between historical resolution and memory cost.

Abstract: Transformers owe much of their strong long-context retrieval capability to a token-level memory that grows with context length. This flexibility, however, incurs a quadratic computation complexity during training and a key--value cache that grows linearly during autoregressive inference. Recurrent alternatives offer efficient decoding by compressing the entire history into a fixed-size state, but often underperform on recall-intensive tasks since earlier associations usually get overwritten by subsequent updates, and only the most recent contextual information is retained. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Anchor Routing across Context History (MARCH), a network architecture that effectively scales state-space models beyond a fixed-size dimension, while maintaining computational efficiency over long-sequences. MARCH periodically caches cumulative recurrent-state checkpoints as state anchors and associates each anchor with a compact, content-conditioned anchor key. This lets MARCH maintain a memory bank, which can grow as context length increases, providing a controllable trade-off between historical resolution and memory cost. At each token, MARCH produces an anchor query to attend all causally available state anchors, and the output is calculated as an attention-style aggregation over all historical anchors along the current state. We show that after standard pretraining, MARCH consistently outperforms multiple linear attention variants across commonsense reasoning, LongBench, and in-context retrieval. These results demonstrate that content-routed state caching substantially strengthens recurrent long-range memory while preserving its native computation path.

</details>


### [180] [Multi-AUV Ad-hoc network-based Target Tracking: A Value Gradient Guidance Multi-Agent Diffusion Reinforcement Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12436)
*Jiaao Ma,Chuan Lin,Guangjie Han,Shengchao Zhu,Qian Zhu,Ying Liu,Zhenyu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion RL approach with a three-tier control architecture for cooperative multi-AUV tracking; shows improved convergence, accuracy, and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater ad-hoc networks face constrained acoustic comms, dynamic topology, and disturbances. MARL suffers from high-dimensional joint state-action spaces and noisy, unstable policy learning; a scalable, stable framework is needed for reliable cooperative tracking.

Method: Propose MDCA, a three-tier closed-loop control architecture (global intelligent layer, local online training layer, physical action execution). The local layer handles policy learning; VGG-MADiffRL is a diffusion-policy-based MARL algorithm guided by value gradients, using twin value networks with soft target updates to reduce overestimation and oscillations. Actions are generated via a reverse denoising process within diffusion policy, informed by value gradients. The framework models sonar detection and ocean currents within an MDP formulation for cooperative tracking.

Result: Experimental results show faster convergence, higher tracking accuracy, and smoother training dynamics in cooperative multi-AUV tracking scenarios, validating effectiveness and practical value in dynamic underwater environments.

Conclusion: MDCA and VGG-MADiffRL offer a stable, scalable solution for cooperative target tracking in underwater networks, combining diffusion-based action generation with value-gradient guidance and a hierarchical control structure for improved performance and robustness.

Abstract: Multi-AUV ad-hoc network-based target tracking requires networked autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) to cooperatively track maneuvering targets under constrained acoustic communication, dynamic topology, and uncertain ocean disturbances. Although multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) enables decentralized coordination through centralized training, existing methods suffer from high-dimensional joint state-action modeling, noise-sensitive policy generation, leading to unstable training and degraded tracking. To address these issues, we propose VGG-MADiffRL, a value-gradient-guided multi-agent diffusion RL algorithm, and MDCA, a diffusion?based hierarchical control architecture. Leveraging underwater mission characteristics, we model sonar detection mechanisms and ocean current disturbances, formulating cooperative tracking for multi-AUV ad-hoc networks as an MDP. The proposed MDCA constitutes a three-tier closed-loop control framework: a global intelligent control layer, a local online training layer, and a physical action execution layer. This structure enables synergistic optimization across task allocation, local decision processes, and execution feedback. Within MDCA, the local online training layer is the policy learning framework; VGG-MADiffRL builds on diffusion policies and incorporates value gradients to guide action generation in the reverse denoising process, steering the generated actions towards higher expected returns. It employs twin value networks with joint optimization and soft target updates to mitigate overestimation and training oscillations, promoting more stable convergence. Experimental results show that VGG-MADiffRL consistently achieves faster convergence, higher tracking accuracy, and smoother training dynamics in cooperative tracking scenarios, validating its effectiveness and practical engineering value in dynamic underwater settings.

</details>


### [181] [Unifying Generative Models with Path Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12438)
*Ramon Winterhalder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified path-integral framework for generative modeling that subsumes flow-, diffusion-, variational-, and adversarial methods via a single master action; uses MSRJD to separate probability flows and yields one-loop corrections improving sampler accuracy without additional sampling cost; introduces a response-weighted score-matching objective and EFT-style operator expansions for symmetry-equivariant drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify disparate generative modeling paradigms under a single formalism and enable systematic perturbative improvements to samplers by leveraging diagrammatic techniques.

Method: Formulate generative modeling as a path integral; derive MSRJD action; perform diagrammatic perturbation theory; compute a one-loop correction to deterministic samplers; model imperfect scores as insertions yielding a response-weighted score-matching objective; develop operator expansion for symmetry-equivariant drift with EFT power counting; validate on solvable and nonlinear drifts.

Result: One-loop correction achieves substantial accuracy gains (reduces an initial 53% tree-level error to 1.6% in tested cases) at no extra stochastic sampling cost; imperfect scores enter as insertions; symmetry-equivariant drift design aligns with operator expansion.

Conclusion: The master-action path-integral framework unifies generative modeling strategies and enables perturbative improvements, offering practical gains and a foundation for further EFT-inspired analyses in ML.

Abstract: We formulate generative modeling as a path integral in which flow-based, diffusion-based, variational, and adversarial models arise as different evaluation principles for a single master action. Its Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-de~Dominicis (MSRJD) form separates free from interacting probability flows and opens them to diagrammatic perturbation theory. The expansion yields a one-loop correction to deterministic samplers at no stochastic-sampling cost, which we validate on solvable and nonlinear drifts, where it reduces a 53 % tree-level error to 1.6 %. Imperfect learned scores enter as insertions and yield a response-weighted score-matching objective, and symmetry-equivariant drift design becomes an operator expansion with EFT power counting.

</details>


### [182] [Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution: Architecture-Aligned Post-Hoc Explainability for Recurrent Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12441)
*Iyad Assaad Nekka,Hamida Seba,Khaled Walid Hidouci,Karima Amrouche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-hoc explainability for AddGraph via Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution (DSTA): spatial, short-term temporal, and long-term temporal attributions are added without altering detection performance; achieves identical AUC on UCI Message benchmark while providing explanations; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need auditable, trustworthy anomaly detection in regulated, cooperative systems. Deep detectors for dynamic graphs are accurate but opaque, hindering governance and compliance; prior work lacked explainability for edge-level anomalies in AddGraph.

Method: Three-component DSTA: (1) gradient-based relevance over current adjacency (spatial); (2) reuse of inference-time attention weights for short-term temporal attribution (zero cost); (3) gradient rollback through recurrent hidden states for long-term temporal attribution. Detector is frozen to preserve performance (Delta AUC = 0). Training baseline AddGraph achieves 0.8705 AUC on UCI Message; X-AddGraph reproduces scores exactly and adds explanations.

Result: Baseline AddGraph: average per-snapshot AUC 0.8705 (improves upon original). X-AddGraph reproduces all scores identically while adding explanations. Across four edge populations, long-term attribution reveals historical snapshots with higher counterfactual signal (0.127) than random (0.074). Spatially-blind explainers cannot provide this. Code released for reproducibility.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of strictly post-hoc explainability for AddGraph without harming detection performance. The multi-faceted attribution (spatial, short-term, long-term) yields richer interpretability, especially the long-term signal. Provides a reproducible framework and potential path to auditable, trustworthy dynamic-graph anomaly detectors.

Abstract: Deep learning detectors for anomalies in dynamic graphs have reached strong accuracy, yet they remain opaque: when an edge is flagged, the analyst receives a score but no reason. This opacity is untenable in the cooperative, regulated information systems where such detectors are deployed, where automated decisions must be auditable and trustworthy. We address this gap for AddGraph, the foundational GCN+GRU framework for edge-level anomaly detection in dynamic graphs, which to our knowledge has never been equipped with any form of explainability. We present a strictly post-hoc explainability framework, X-AddGraph, built on a Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution (DSTA) mechanism whose three components are each aligned with one of AddGraph's architectural modules: a gradient-based relevance attribution over the current adjacency structure (spatial), a direct reading of the contextual attention weights already computed during inference (short-term temporal, at zero additional cost), and a gradient rollback through the recurrent hidden states (long-term temporal). Because the detector is frozen, detection performance is preserved exactly (Delta AUC = 0, verified empirically to ten decimal places). On the UCI Message benchmark, our trained AddGraph baseline reaches an average per-snapshot AUC of 0.8705, exceeding the originally published result; X-AddGraph reproduces every score identically while adding explanations where none existed. Evaluated across four edge populations - confident true positives, low-confidence true positives, false positives, and random samples - the long-term attribution identifies historical snapshots carrying significantly more counterfactual signal than random selection (0.127 vs. 0.074), a capability that no spatially-blind explainer can provide. We release our implementation for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [183] [Personalized Scorer Modeling: A Learning-Based Framework for Deriving Robust Sleep Stage Labels from Multiple Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12446)
*Seyyed Ali Hoseini,Javad Baseri,Hamid Saadatfar,Edris Hoseini Gol,AmirHossein Eshghi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning-based hypnogram (LBH) uses per-scorer confusion matrices to model each expert's stage-specific labeling behavior and aggregates them to derive a consensus hypnogram, improving sleep-stage classification performance on DOD-H/O multi-scorer datasets compared with single-reference approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inter-scorer variability in manual sleep staging undermines the reliability of a single reference hypnogram; leveraging multi-scorer annotations may yield more robust reference labels.

Method: For each scorer, LBH models the probability of a true sleep stage given the scorer's label via confusion matrices derived from machine-learning models, then column-normalizes to estimate P(true stage | scorer label). Probabilities are aggregated across scorers to assign the final epoch label. The approach is evaluated with random forest, SVM, and multilayer perceptron classifiers in EEG-only and EEG+EMG settings, using 30 EEG features and 30 EMG features (60 total). Datasets: DOD-H and DOD-O; compared to dataset hypnogram (DH) and best-scorer hypnogram (BSH).

Result: LBH consistently improved performance over DH and BSH. Best results were achieved with random forest and EEG+EMG, yielding about 86.0% accuracy, ~85.2–85.5% precision, and ~84.7–85.3% F1 on both datasets (DOD-H: 86.07% acc, 85.46% precision, 85.29% F1; DOD-O: 86.04% acc, 85.21% precision, 84.70% F1).

Conclusion: Modeling the stage-specific behavior of individual scorers and aggregating these probabilistic assessments can enhance the construction of reference hypnograms, preserving information from multiple experts and improving sleep-stage classification benchmarks.

Abstract: Sleep stage classification is important for the diagnosis and management of sleep disorders, yet most automatic staging studies evaluate models against a single reference hypnogram despite known inter-scorer variability. This study investigates whether multi-scored datasets can be used to construct more reliable reference labels from the collective behavior of multiple experts. We use the publicly available DOD-H and DOD-O datasets. EEG (C3-M2) and chin EMG signals were segmented into 30-s epochs, and 30 features were extracted from each modality, yielding 60 features for EEG+EMG. We propose a learning-based hypnogram (LBH) that models the stage-specific behavior of each scorer using confusion matrices derived from machine-learning models. After column normalization, these matrices estimate the probability of each true sleep stage given each scorer's label; probabilities are aggregated across scorers to assign the final label for each epoch. LBH was evaluated with random forest, support vector machine, and multilayer perceptron classifiers under EEG-only and EEG+EMG settings, and compared with the dataset hypnogram (DH) and best-scorer hypnogram (BSH). LBH consistently improved overall performance. The best results were obtained with random forest and EEG+EMG, reaching 86.07% accuracy, 85.46% precision, and 85.29% F1-score on DOD-H, and 86.04% accuracy, 85.21% precision, and 84.70% F1-score on DOD-O. These findings suggest that personalized scorer modeling can improve reference hypnogram construction without discarding information from individual experts.

</details>


### [184] [Geometric and Behavioral Stratification in Transformer Residual Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12447)
*Nelson Guda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformers develop a prediction-direction as a content-defined privileged anchor in the residual stream. Residual variance is geometrically and behaviorally stratified by proximity to this anchor; a narrow, scale-invariant readout interface governs prediction, while the distal, larger-scale component expands with model size. Disrupting proximal variance directions yields immediate divergence, whereas perturbing farther directions delays it; the organization is robust across 18 model families and tasks. 


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain how high-dimensional transformer representations support linear readouts by identifying privileged axes and their geometric role in shaping prediction and readout.

Method: Empirical analysis across 18 models (dense and Mixture-of-Experts; sizes 7B–120B; base and instruction-tuned). Identify the unembedding (prediction) direction as a content-defined privileged anchor. Quantify residual-stream variance relative to this anchor, its geometry, and readout alignment. Assess how perturbations to variance directions near and farther from the anchor affect prediction, framing, and task performance.

Result: The prediction direction acts as a privileged anchor. Residual-stream variation is stratified by proximity to the anchor, with prediction-proximal regions forming a highly structured, prompt-clustered zone, and a broad, flatter distal region that expands with model size. A narrow, scale-invariant prediction interface concentrates readout-relevant structure, while the distal complement grows with scale. The prediction direction is nearly orthogonal to principal variance axes; variance-based analyses capture only part of the organization, especially with heterogeneous prompts. Disrupting variance directions closest to the prediction causes immediate divergence and task-frame shifts; disrupting the next level down delays divergence but preserves framing. The distal portion is weakly readout-aligned per direction yet causally load-bearing. Behavior is driven by direction, not magnitude.

Conclusion: The prediction direction is a distinct, privileged axis from previously described coordinate bases, providing a geometric account of how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout and showing the robustness of this organization across model families and prompt variations.

Abstract: Trained transformer models develop privileged bases: coordinate axes whose statistics differ from the rest of the residual stream. But what kind of direction does such a basis select? We investigate the prediction direction, the unembedding direction of the token a model currently predicts, and find that it functions as a content-defined privileged anchor. Measured with respect to this anchor, residual-stream variation is geometrically and behaviorally stratified by proximity to the prediction.
  The stratification holds in all eighteen models tested (dense and mixture-of-experts, 7B-120B, base and instruction-tuned). A narrow, scale-invariant prediction interface concentrates readout-relevant structure, while the vast prediction-distal complement expands with model scale. Because the prediction direction sits nearly orthogonal to the principal variance axes, variance-based analyses recover this organization only partly, and the shortfall grows with prompt heterogeneity.
  Anchoring reveals a steep geometric gradient: prediction-proximal regions are highly structured and cluster related prompts, while the complement is flatter and anti-discriminates among prompt groups. The interface is a narrow slice but functionally decisive. Disrupting the variance directions closest to the prediction causes immediate divergence and frequent task-frame shifts; disrupting the next level down delays divergence and preserves framing. The complement is weakly readout-aligned per direction yet causally and temporally load-bearing, and behavior is driven by direction rather than magnitude.
  These results establish the prediction direction as a privileged anchor distinct from previously described coordinate axes, and give a geometric account of how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout.

</details>


### [185] [Exemplar-based objective classification of gust-induced loads across multiple flight conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12448)
*Paolo Olivucci,Kowshik Srivatsan,David E. Rival*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine-learned exemplar-based framework classifies gust-induced pressures; identifies nine fundamental response types across six flight attitudes from 3480 measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for an objective, interpretable, and scalable classification of gust-load complexity beyond coarse attitude labels.

Method: Encode observations into a learned representation, then summarize to select a minimal set of highly significant exemplars; establish a similarity-based classification of all observations; apply to 3480 gust-load measurements on a flying-wing model at six attitudes.

Result: Nine fundamental response types recur across attitudes; analyzing a type’s transient response yields physical intuition into the fluid-mechanics causing the loads.

Conclusion: Exemplar-based summarization yields an interpretable, objective classification that can guide expert inspection and future experiments; validated on a large gust-load database.

Abstract: Is it possible to find an objective classification criterion that organizes the complexity of gust-induced loads across many flight conditions? And one that remains as interpretable as a labelling based on coarse parameters, such as the flight attitude? Our approach encodes a large number of experimental observations through a machine-learned representation and applies a summarization procedure to select a minimal subset of highly significant exemplars. The exemplars provide a similarity-based objective classification criterion of all the observations, they can be more conveniently inspected by experts and can become subject of more refined experiments. We demonstrate the approach on a database of 3480 pressure-load measurements induced by random gusts on a flying-wing model across six flight attitudes. We find nine fundamental response types that recur across multiple attitudes; analysis of a type's transient response enables physical intuition into the underlying fluid mechanics.

</details>


### [186] [Learning Under Treatment-Induced Label Indeterminacy with Expert Annotations of Counterfactual Outcomes: A Case Study in Neurological Prognostication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12477)
*Xiaobin Shen,Chloe Y. H. Huang,Jonathan Elmer,George H. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework to evaluate clinical prediction models when outcomes are censored by treatment decisions, separating certain and uncertain cases, and a neural-tabuled approach that trades off performance on these two groups; standard metrics can miss key failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outcomes can be permanently unobservable due to treatment decisions, creating censoring that challenges model evaluation and calibration; need methods that explicitly account for uncertain outcomes.

Method: Split evaluation into certain and uncertain cases; develop a neural model that uses labels from both groups and allows trading off performance; compare against tabular baselines across AUROC, Brier score, and probabilistic calibration on uncertain cases.

Result: Similar AUROC on certain cases can mask substantial differences in Brier score and probabilistic estimates for uncertain cases; aligning more with uncertain-case labels often reduces certain-case accuracy, revealing a tradeoff hidden by standard evaluation.

Conclusion: When treatment decisions determine observability of outcomes, conventional metrics understate risks; explicit handling of uncertain cases is essential to identify failure modes in prognostic models and to understand performance tradeoffs.

Abstract: Clinical prediction models are often developed as if the outcome of interest were cleanly observed for every patient. This assumption fails when treatment decisions make the clinically relevant outcome permanently unobservable. As a case study of this problem, we consider post-cardiac-arrest neurological prognostication using a cohort of 2,497 patients, including 1,429 patients whose outcomes were rendered indeterminate by treatment decisions. These patients with indeterminate outcomes were reviewed by independent clinical experts, who provided their guesses of counterfactual outcomes about what would have happened to the patients. We refer to these patients as uncertain cases. We also have patients for whom we observe their clinically relevant outcomes; we refer to these patients as certain cases. We propose a framework for evaluating prediction models that explicitly splits the evaluation between certain and uncertain cases. Here, we cannot easily evaluate both types of cases in a uniform manner as the available target labels differ. We then propose a simple prediction model that uses target labels from both certain and uncertain cases in a manner that allows us to trade off between them. Across the proposed neural model and a collection of tabular baselines, models with similar certain-case AUROC can nevertheless differ substantially in both certain-case Brier score and their probability estimates for uncertain cases. Improving alignment with target labels of uncertain cases for our proposed model generally comes at the cost of worse accuracy on certain cases, highlighting an explicit tradeoff that standard evaluation conceals. These results show that when treatment decisions determine whether clinically meaningful outcomes remain observable, conventional evaluation metrics can miss important failure modes in the very patients for whom prognostic support matters most.

</details>


### [187] [When Can You Trust Offline Evaluation of Equal-Cost Top-k Allocation? A Controlled, Reproducible Benchmark and Practitioner's Guide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12489)
*Binshuang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmarking study of six off-policy estimators for deterministic top-k policies across five datasets, revealing that overlap is driven by logger-target action alignment, that cross-fitting the nuisance does not fix estimator bias (the estimand shift matters), and that propensity-estimation error is the largest degradation, with a synthetic/semi-synthetic benchmark and public release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand practical reliability of off-policy evaluation (OPE) for deterministic top-k policies under real-world logging, identify what drives overlap, bias, and variance, and provide a reproducible benchmark.

Method: Benchmark six estimators on five datasets with two known-effect sweeps, compare against a non-simulated paired reference, analyze overlap drivers (logger-target alignment vs. logging sharpness), test cross-fitting of the outcome nuisance, examine the optimizer's curse with honest policy-level splitting, assess propensity-estimation errors with bounded weights (propensities floored at 0.02), use synthetic/semi-synthetic data, and release the benchmark with public data only.

Result: Key findings: (1) Overlap is governed by the logger-target action alignment; widening logger sharpness has little effect; action-level disagreement collapses overlap. (2) The optimizer’s curse persists; cross-fitting nuisance alone leaves reuse bias and can worsen it; honest policy-level splits alter the estimand to avoid reuse. (3) Propensity-estimation error is the dominant degradation; out-of-fold propensity errors hurt IPS more than other stresses, while doubly robust methods are relatively robust; floor on propensities bounds weights and reduces tuned hybrids to untuned parents, yielding four practically distinct estimators; all exact-value surfaces are synthetic or semi-synthetic. They release a public benchmark dataset only.

Conclusion: The study provides practical guidance for OPE in deterministic top-k settings: rely on logger-target alignment for overlap diagnostics, recognize the limitations of cross-fitting for bias correction, and prioritize accurate propensity estimation; the authors supply a reproducible benchmark (public data only) to compare estimators under realistic logging conditions and to inform logger design and estimator choice.

Abstract: Organizations decide whom to treat under a budget and want to know what a targeting rule would have earned before deploying it. Off-policy evaluation promises this from logged data, but the deployable rule is a deterministic top-k policy: it removes all averaging over actions, so weak overlap hits the estimate directly. We benchmark six estimators across five datasets and two known-effect sweeps, and validate the mechanisms against a non-simulated paired reference. First, weak overlap is governed by logger-target action alignment, not by logging sharpness alone: what governs support is the logger's probability of the target's actions. Sharpening a logger built from the target's own score barely moves overlap over the tested range; action-level disagreement collapses it. Effective sample size ranks this risk across logging environments, but is weak at ranking candidates within the single log a practitioner holds, and its cut point does not transfer. Second, the optimizer's curse is not fixed by cross-fitting the outcome nuisance. When the rule is fit on the data used to evaluate it, cross-fitting the nuisance alone leaves the reuse bias in place and makes it worse. Honest policy-level splitting avoids the reuse by targeting the learning procedure's value -- a change of estimand, not a de-biasing of the full-sample policy. Third, propensity-estimation error is the largest degradation we measure: an out-of-fold estimate hurts IPS more than any other stress we apply, leaves doubly-robust estimation almost unchanged, and can invert the overlap diagnostic itself. Logging is synthesized and propensities floored at 0.02, so every failure occurs with bounded weights; the floor also reduces the two tuned hybrids to their untuned parents, leaving four practically distinct estimators, and all exact-value surfaces are synthetic or semi-synthetic. We release the benchmark; public data only.

</details>


### [188] [Exploring Oversmoothing with Householder Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12514)
*Bhaskar Karol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HouseGNN uses Householder reflections driven by neighborhood messages to form piecewise orthogonal layers that preserve node-wise Euclidean norms, aiming to mitigate oversmoothing in deep GNNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep GNNs suffer from oversmoothing, where repeated propagation collapses node representations. The approach seeks norm-preserving, depth-stable updates by using a Householder reflector guided by aggregated neighborhood information.

Method: For each node, aggregate neighborhood messages to estimate a reflection direction. Update the node embedding via a Householder reflector followed by GroupSort, producing a piecewise orthogonal layer that preserves Euclidean norm at every node and depth.

Result: The paper proves three core properties: (i) every internal layer preserves node-wise Euclidean norm; (ii) the Householder reflector is scale- and sign-invariant in the message; (iii) pairwise distances between nodes can change due to mismatch between node-wise orthogonal operators.

Conclusion: HouseGNN provides a depth-stable, norm-preserving GNN by employing Householder-based layers, maintaining Euclidean norm at each node/depth and enabling potential mitigation of oversmoothing; the approach yields theoretical guarantees about norm preservation, invariance, and distance dynamics.

Abstract: Deep graph neural networks(GNNs) suffer from oversmoothing- a progressive collapse of node representation towards a low information subspace as network depth increases because the normalized graph propagation operator is repeatedly applied directly to the hidden representations. In this work we study Householder Graph Neural Network (HouseGNN). Rather than updating the hidden state like standard GCN, HouseGNN uses the aggregated neighbourhood message solely to estimate a reflection direction; the node embedding is then updated by a Householder reflector followed by GroupSort, yielding a piecewise orthogonal layer that preserves Euclidean norm at every node and at every depth. We prove three core properties: (i) every internal layer preserves the node-wise Euclidean norm; (ii) the Householder reflector is scale scale and sign-invariant in the message; and (iii) pairwise distance between nodes can change through mismatch between node-wise orthogonal operators.

</details>


### [189] [GENADA: efficient generative time series adversarial attack framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12535)
*Michael Baronov,Denis Vorobev,Margarita Rusanova,Petr Sokerin,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GENADA: a generative, single-pass adversarial attack for time-series models that matches strong baselines in quality but reduces perturbation generation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the computational burden of gradient-based iterative attacks while exploiting the vulnerability of deep time-series models to adversarial perturbations.

Method: Train a generative model to produce deceptive perturbations in a single forward pass (with variants for single-step and iterative schemes).

Result: Empirically achieves comparable attack quality to strong baselines across multiple neural models and time-series datasets in a controlled setting, with faster perturbation generation at inference.

Conclusion: Generative, non-iterative perturbation generation is a viable, efficient alternative for time-series adversarial attacks; implications for robustness evaluation and defense; future work on broader, real-world settings.

Abstract: Deep learning models are widely used for time series analysis in domains such as healthcare, finance, energy systems, and environmental monitoring. However, these models remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks, where small input perturbations cause severe degradation in predictive performance. Commonly used gradient-based attacks, iterative first-order methods, are computationally burdensome, as they repeatedly backpropagate through the victim model to compute input gradients during a number of iterative refinement steps. We propose a GENerative ADversarial Attack (GENADA) that learns a generative model to produce deceptive perturbations directly in a single forward pass and a procedure to train it. Variants include single-step and iterative generative attack schemes. The validation considers attacks on several neural models and datasets in the time-series domain, a controlled, low-dimensional setting. Empirically, GENADA achieves comparable attack quality to strong baselines while requiring less time to generate perturbations during inference.

</details>


### [190] [Scaling Automatic Research Agents via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12564)
*Xiyuan Yang,Sheikh Sarwar,Jingru Cheng,Zhan Shi,Duanshun Li,Huiyuan Chen,Haiyang Zhang,Chenlei Guo,Jingrui He,Zhenyu Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: World Model RL (WMRL) replaces environment execution with a world model to alleviate the RL bottleneck in AutoResearch. It adds Online Debiasing and Inverse-Variance Denoising to handle biased/noisy rewards, achieving 3–4x training speedups and stronger performance with smaller agents, with transfer to embodied policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AutoResearch RL suffers from a fundamental bottleneck: generation (shared compute via batching) scales differently from environment execution (exclusive sandbox/real machine time). As trajectories grow, environment interaction becomes the primary cost, hindering scalability.

Method: Introduce a world model to stand in for environment execution during training. Augment with Online Debiasing to correct reward bias and Inverse-Variance Denoising to suppress reward noise. Provide convergence guarantees showing improvements. Empirically validate on tasks across scales and demonstrate transfer to post-trained embodied VLA policies.

Result: WMRL yields 3–4x faster training across varied tasks and agent scales, outperforming standard RL baselines. 4B and 9B agents exceed the performance of much larger 48B and 120B open-weight agents on held-out benchmarks. The approach generalizes to embodied VLA policies beyond AutoResearch.

Conclusion: Replacing environment execution with a world model, coupled with debiasing and denoising, effectively mitigates the RL training bottleneck in AutoResearch, improves convergence guarantees, and generalizes to related post-training settings.

Abstract: Automating empirical research is a long-standing direction of AI. Recent automatic research (AutoResearch) agents bring this goal within reach, as modern LLMs show the capability to independently implement solutions and learn from the execution outcomes. Behind these gains, post-training (especially RL) plays a central role. In this paper, we identify a fundamental tension when scaling RL for these agents: the two components of every AutoResearch trajectory (agent generation and environment execution) scale in very different manners, since all generation shares compute through batching, while each execution occupies its exclusive sandbox and real machine time. As a result, the environment execution dominates the training cost and becomes the bottleneck as trajectories grow. To resolve this tension, we propose World Model RL (WMRL), which replaces environment execution with a world model to remove this bottleneck. Additionally, the world model can be imperfect, as its rewards are corrupted by bias and noise. Therefore, we further equip WMRL with two mitigations, Online Debiasing and Inverse-Variance Denoising, which offset the bias and suppress the noise respectively. Theoretically, we prove that both mitigations of WMRL strictly improve the convergence guarantee. Empirically, WMRL accelerates training by 3-4x on various tasks at different agent scales, while exceeding the performance of standard RL baselines. Moreover, our post-trained 4B and 9B agents outperform much larger open-weight agents of 48B and 120B on held-out benchmarks. Beyond AutoResearch, WMRL also transfers to post-training embodied VLA policies, which demonstrates the generalizability of our method.

</details>


### [191] [Prof-K: Probabilistic One-Pass Filtering for Efficient Top-k Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12573)
*Tadeusz Dziarmaga,Witold Sikora,Łukasz Struski,Jacek Tabor,Marcin Mazur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Prof-K is a probabilistic, distribution-agnostic top-k algorithm that uses a single-pass sampling-based threshold estimation and a buffer to recover the true top-k with high probability, achieving speedups and robust guarantees across inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing exact top-k methods are memory- and compute-intensive on large data; approximate methods rely on brittle heuristics. There is a need for robust, distribution-agnostic top-k with probabilistic correctness guarantees.

Method: A one-pass filtering approach: draw a small random sample to estimate an adaptive threshold; stream N elements into a compact buffer; run an exact top-k on the buffer to recover the true top-k with probability at least 1−ε. The paper derives high-probability correctness and buffer-size guarantees, optimizes the sample size S as a function of N and k, and allows controlled relaxation of recall (e.g., 95%).

Result: Prof-K yields 1.5×–10× speedups over optimized PyTorch topk and RadiK, especially in large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regimes. It remains robust to adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs due to distribution-agnostic guarantees. The method enables a principled accuracy-speed trade-off and reduces training costs in applications like BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders.

Conclusion: Prof-K delivers a scalable, distribution-agnostic top-k with probabilistic correctness guarantees and tunable accuracy-speed trade-offs, suitable for demanding ML workloads and large-scale data processing.

Abstract: Top-k selection is a fundamental computational primitive with applications spanning databases, information retrieval, signal processing, and modern machine learning workloads, including sparse activations and attention pruning. As data sizes grow, existing approaches become inefficient: exact methods incur high memory and compute overhead, while approximate methods often rely on brittle heuristics that degrade under adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs. In this paper, we introduce Prof-K, a fast, scalable, and distribution-agnostic top-k algorithm with probabilistic correctness guarantees. Prof-K performs a single-pass filtering procedure: a small random sample estimates an adaptive threshold, the N input elements are streamed once into a compact buffer, and an exact top-k routine on this buffer recovers the true top-k elements with probability at least 1 - $ε$, where $ε$ > 0 is user specified. We derive high-probability guarantees for correctness and buffer size, together with an approximately optimal sample size that minimizes overhead as a function of N and k. Empirically, Prof-K achieves 1.5x-10x speedups over the highly optimized PyTorch topk and recent RadiK implementations, with the largest gains in the large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regime where prior methods struggle most. Unlike previous approaches, these guarantees hold independently of the input distribution, ensuring robustness to adversarial settings. By relaxing the recall target (e.g., recovering 95% of the true top-k values), Prof-K additionally provides a principled accuracy-speed trade-off. We further demonstrate its impact on training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), where top-k selection constitutes a significant portion of the training cost.

</details>


### [192] [Represent, Then Generate: Multimodal-Conditioned Time-Series Generation under Irregular Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12592)
*Haochen Zhang,Jiaheng Guo,Yu-Chao Huang,Nicholas Knoz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-stage conditional generative framework (ReCoGen) decouples conditioning from generation for multimodal time-series, yielding superior surrogate signals across three benchmarks and many settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous physiological signals are often invasive, expensive, or missing. Existing generators struggle with heterogeneous, irregular missingness across time-varying modalities and static covariates. A modular approach separating conditioning from generation can improve robustness and utility.

Method: Stage I: train one masked autoencoder per modality to distill time-varying conditions into compact, missingness-tolerant token sequences. Stage II: train a flow-matching generator that fuses modality tokens with static conditions to synthesize the target signal. Key design choices include learnable cross-attention over frozen per-modality encoders and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route for static conditions.

Result: ReCoGen achieves best downstream utility across all 16 dataset-task-metric settings among six baselines on three benchmarks (AI-READI glucose; MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV arterial pressure). In 13 of 16 settings, its utility matches or exceeds that of the real signal. Ablations attribute gains to the conditioning path.

Conclusion: Decoupling condition representation from generation enables reliable surrogate signal synthesis from routinely collected data, moving toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring.

Abstract: Continuous physiological time series underpin modern clinical monitoring, yet many of the most informative signals are invasive, expensive, or simply unavailable for a given patient. Conditional generation offers a remedy: an absent signal can be synthesized from co-recorded signals and routine clinical variables. Existing generators, however, are built around a single conditioning modality and degrade when forced to handle the heterogeneous, irregularly missing mix of time-variant signals and static covariates seen in practice. We propose ReCoGen (Represent Conditions, then Generate), a two-stage framework that decouples multimodal condition representation from target generation. Stage I trains one masked autoencoder per modality, distilling each time-variant condition into a compact and missingness-tolerant token sequence. Stage II trains a flow-matching generator that fuses these tokens with static conditions to synthesize the target signal. Across three physiological benchmarks, including continuous glucose monitoring on AI-READI and arterial blood pressure generation on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, ReCoGen attains the best downstream utility on all sixteen (dataset, task, metric) settings, surpassing six representative conditional generators; on thirteen of them its utility also reaches or exceeds the utility measured on the real signal, a reference we read as an approximate anchor rather than a ceiling. Ablations trace the gains to the conditioning path: learnable cross-attention over the frozen per-modality encoders, and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route for the static conditions. ReCoGen thus turns routinely collected signals into informative surrogates for invasive or unavailable ones, a step toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring.

</details>


### [193] [Predicting When Random Low-Dimensional Reparameterizations Train Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12597)
*Andrew Cheng,Ali Eslamian,Jie Cheng,Mehdi Zargham,Qiang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes random low-dimensional parameterizations (latent-space mappings) for neural network training, derives an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula predicting when a small latent update can reach a low-loss region, introduces Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN) with memory-efficient structured maps, and validates predictions with controlled and end-to-end experiments showing sharp transitions and improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how large a latent search space must be to access low-loss regions under random mapping; to connect geometric properties (statistical dimension, polar cone) with practical mapping strategies; to enable memory-efficient training via structured mappings.

Method: Recast accessibility transition as a conic problem centered at the statistical dimension of the polar cone; derive orientation-resolved quadratic master formula capturing random-slice residual from curvature spectrum and displacement profile; obtain isotropic predictor and Gaussian-width bound as special case; construct RaMaN using Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps; provide matrix-free curvature approximations and dimension selection; run controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments; perform end-to-end training on image and language models.

Result: Orientation-resolved predictor matches measured transition locations; outperforms orientation-agnostic bounds when displacement matters; RaMaN achieves memory efficiency, reduces optimizer-state from O(P) to O(d); experiments show sharp, protocol-dependent transitions.

Conclusion: Orientation-aware analysis enables accurate latent-dimension predictions and practical, memory-efficient random mappings; results generalize across modalities and protocols, offering a scalable approach to latent-space optimization in neural nets.

Abstract: Neural networks can often be trained or fine-tuned through random low-dimensional reparameterization, where a small latent vector is mapped into a full parameter update by a frozen random map. This raises a practical question: how large must the latent search space be to reach a low-loss region? We first express the known accessibility transition in an equivalent conic form, centered for compact convex targets at the statistical dimension of the polar cone. Our main theoretical contribution is an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula that predicts the random-slice residual from both the curvature spectrum and the reference-to-solution displacement profile. It yields a self-consistent isotropic-orientation predictor and, in a conservative radius-only specialization, recovers the earlier Gaussian-width quadratic bound. Building on this analysis, we introduce Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN), which instantiate the predicted latent dimension using structured Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps. These constructions avoid the O(dP) storage of dense random maps and reduce optimizer-state memory from O(P) to O(d). We also develop matrix-free curvature approximations and sweep-free dimension selection. Across controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments, the orientation-resolved predictor closely tracks measured transition locations and outperforms orientation-agnostic approximations when displacement direction matters. End-to-end experiments further show sharp, protocol-dependent training transitions across image and language models.

</details>


### [194] [The Boolean Power of ReLU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12617)
*Pablo Barceló,Floris Geerts,Matthias Lanzinger,Klara Pakhomenko,Jan Van den Bussche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReLU-MPLang strictly more expressive than Σ-MPLang for Boolean queries on graphs with a single Boolean feature, solving an open question about ReLU-MPLang vs trReLU-MPLang; implies ReLU-GNNs exceed {TrReLU,id}-GNNs for Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine the comparative expressive power of MPLang variants in graph neural networks and its implications for the design of GNN architectures handling Boolean features.

Method: Prove a strict inclusion: Boolean queries expressible in Σ-MPLang (with Σ of eventually constant activation functions and arbitrary real coefficients) form a proper subset of those expressible in ReLU-MPLang; derive as a corollary that ReLU-MNNs are strictly more expressive than TrReLU-id variants for Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

Result: Strict inclusion: Σ-MPLang Boolean queries are a proper subset of ReLU-MPLang Boolean queries.
