This resolves the open problem of whether ReLU-MPLang is more expressive than trReLU-MPLang for Boolean queries. Consequently, ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs with respect to Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

Conclusion: The work clarifies the hierarchy among MPLang variants and confirms a strict expressiveness gap in favor of ReLU-based formulations for Boolean-query tasks on graphs, with direct implications for GNN design.

Abstract: We prove that, on finite simple undirected graphs equipped with a single Boolean node feature, the Boolean queries expressible in $Σ$-MPLang, for any collection $Σ$ of eventually constant activation functions and with arbitrary real coefficients, form a strict subclass of the Boolean queries expressible in ReLU-MPLang. We thereby settle a recently posed open problem: whether ReLU-MPLang is more powerful than trReLU-MPLang when it comes to Boolean queries. In particular, this implies that ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs with respect to Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

</details>


### [195] [Structure-preserving uncertainty quantification for GENERIC dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12624)
*Zequn He,Celia Reina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: S-PENNs provide uncertainty quantification for structure-preserving ML by attaching lightweight epinets to constrained components, yielding thermodynamically consistent predictions with calibrated intervals and large cost savings, demonstrated on GENERIC dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for UQ in hard-constrained, structure-preserving models without violating admissibility or paying high computational costs; instantiate for GENERIC dynamics.

Method: Attach lightweight epinets to constrained components of a pretrained structure-preserving model to keep realizations physically admissible; apply to GENERIC dynamics to ensure first/second laws; use split conformal prediction for post-hoc calibration to obtain finite-sample coverage.

Result: Thermodynamically consistent rollouts; well-calibrated prediction intervals; validated on three numerical tests (ODEs and PDE) with reduced computational cost by 1–3 orders of magnitude compared to deep ensembles.

Conclusion: S-PENNs offer a general UQ framework for hard-constrained scientific ML and can be extended to other models with hard or soft constraints beyond GENERIC.

Abstract: Structure-preserving machine learning embeds physical structure directly into model architectures, yet uncertainty quantification (UQ) for such hard-constrained models remains limited because standard UQ methods may violate the encoded admissibility conditions, require architectural modifications, or impose substantial computational costs. In this work, we propose Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks (S-PENNs), a general framework for UQ in scientific machine learning models with hard architectural constraints, and instantiate it for GENERIC (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible-Irreversible Coupling) dynamics. S-PENNs preserve the structural constraints of a pretrained model by attaching lightweight epinets to its constrained components, ensuring that every sampled realization remains physically admissible by construction. When applied to GENERIC dynamics, such a proposed framework yields thermodynamically consistent rollouts that preserve the first and second laws. Furthermore, we combine S-PENNs with split conformal prediction as a post-hoc calibration method to produce prediction intervals with finite-sample marginal coverage guarantees. We validate S-PENNs on three numerical examples: a harmonic oscillator coupled to a heat bath and an idealized chemical motor, both governed by ODEs, and a one-dimensional viscoplastic model governed by PDEs. Across all three examples, S-PENNs produce thermodynamically consistent stochastic realizations and well-calibrated prediction intervals while reducing the computational cost by about one to three orders of magnitude compared to deep ensembles. Although the present study focuses on GENERIC dynamics, S-PENNs can be extended more broadly to scientific machine learning models in computational mechanics with either hard or soft constraints.

</details>


### [196] [CAKE: Compiler-Agent Co-Design for Frontier Kernel Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12629)
*Zihao Ye,Yingyi Huang,Hongyi Jin,Bohan Hou,Junru Shao,Zhongming Yu,Jinqi Chen,Meghan Cowan,Shiyi Cao,Shanli Xing,Hanfeng Chen,Vinod Grover,Tianqi Chen,Luis Ceze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAKE enables agent-driven, hardware-explicit GPU scheduling via CAKE IR; shows speedups vs Flash/KDA baselines; supports verification, cost modeling, and upstream kernel changes across Ampere–Blackwell GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproduceable, expert-level GPU kernels are hard to reproduce because agents treat the compiler as a black box and DSLs hide or complicate scheduling. A typed, hardware-explicit IR allows agents to specify and verify schedules, while the harness learns from failures.

Method: Introduce CAKE IR, a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation exposing warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines. Agents author IR, supported by verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics. The harness evolves via verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics. The evaluation includes matched implementations (Flash-KMeans, Kimi Delta Attention, KNN, KMeans) across NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere to Blackwell, with budgeting and dispatch. Four kernel changes are provided upstream.

Result: On an 80-million-token budget, the best CAKE IR candidate runs at 1.144x the tuned FlashML baseline and 0.928x for direct CUDA/PTX. Kimi Delta Attention achieves a 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA and passes end-to-end serving validation. Dispatcher-backed KNN and KMeans improve performance by 1.42x to 2.12x across more than 400 shapes, with four kernel changes released as upstream PRs.

Conclusion: CAKE demonstrates that compiler-agent co-design with a typed, hardware-explicit IR can bridge the gap between expert kernels and agent-driven optimization, enabling reproducible performance gains, upstream contributions, and broad hardware support from Ampere through Blackwell; the harness evolves with failures into rules, primitives, and optimizations.

Abstract: GPU kernel agents and GPU programming languages have advanced separately, leaving expert kernels difficult to reproduce. Agents usually treat the compiler as a fixed black box and receive only errors, correctness outcomes, and timing, while existing DSLs either hide critical scheduling decisions or expose them through difficult layout abstractions. We present CAKE, a compiler-agent co-design in which agents author CAKE IR, a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation. CAKE exposes warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines while supporting verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics. The harness itself evolves: recurring failures become verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics. In matched implementation-hidden Flash-KMeans clean starts on B200, the best CAKE IR candidate at an 80-million-token budget runs at 1.144x the tuned FlashML baseline, compared with 0.928x for direct CUDA/PTX. Beyond this benchmark, agent-generated Kimi Delta Attention achieves a 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA and passes end-to-end serving validation. Dispatcher-backed KNN and KMeans improve performance by 1.42x to 2.12x across more than 400 shapes, and four kernel changes are available as upstream PRs. CAKE targets NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere through Blackwell and separates single-shape evolution from library generalization and dispatch.

</details>


### [197] [Interpretable Causal Discovery via Causal-Effect Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12640)
*Cixuan Zhang,Guy Van den Broeck,Benjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a Bayesian rare-event inference method for conditional causal discovery that estimates posteriors conditioned on causal-effect constraints, using particle-based rare-event techniques; validated on synthetic graphs and a Sachs dataset case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable interpretable conditional causal reasoning (e.g., enforcing causal-effect constraints) in Bayesian causal discovery, especially when the event of interest has small posterior mass.

Method: Adapt rare-event estimation to the joint graph-parameter space, guiding a population of particles toward the constrained region while preserving samples that approximate the conditional posterior.

Result: Demonstrates accurate inference for both small- and large-scale graphs on synthetic data and shows pathway-level summaries in the Sachs protein dataset.

Conclusion: The proposed rare-event Bayesian approach facilitates efficient conditional causal discovery, yielding interpretable, pathway-level insights even for rare constraints.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to uncover the underlying causal relationships given data generated from a system. The goal, however, is not merely to predict causal edges given data, but also to be able to interpret and explain either observed or hypothesized phenomena, such as a particularly large causal effect. We consider this task of conditional causal discovery and cast it as a Bayesian inference problem, in which we target the posterior over causal graphs and parameters conditional on an event such as a causal-effect constraint. Unfortunately, this poses a computational challenge: existing approaches to Bayesian causal discovery struggle when the event has small posterior mass. To address this, we adapt rare-event estimation techniques to perform inference the joint graph-parameter space. Our method gradually drives a particle population toward the constrained region while maintaining samples that approximate the conditional posterior. Empirical evaluation on synthetic graphs validates the accuracy of our approach at small and large scales, and we show in a case study on the Sachs protein dataset how our method can be used to aid scientific exploration by providing pathway-level summaries.

</details>


### [198] [Training Under Challenge: Executable Certificates and Challenge-Closed Optimality for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12655)
*Farhang Yeganegi,Arian Eamaz,Mojtaba Soltanalian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Training Under Challenge (TUC), an executable-certificate framework that builds complete alternatives in the same certified class to bound the empirical global-optimality gap and diagnose causes of non-global optima.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A flat training curve does not reveal whether a network has reached a global optimum, is locally trapped, is representation-limited, or is mismatched to its trainer; a formal framework with verifiable certificates and coverage is needed to quantify and diagnose global-optimality gaps.

Method: Define predeclared, architecture-valid procedures to construct complete alternatives within the same certified class and re-evaluate the same objective. Introduce a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus to bound the gap; for squared loss use block-decrease operators to obtain coverage-checkable, uniform and realized-residual bounds. Prove that without coverage a first-order ReLU trainer can reach many exact local optima yet fail globally. Demonstrate on a channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation with known optimum: eight internal challenges cover 240 output directions; realized-residual bounds within factors 1.74–3.02 of the true gap. Use paired predictive certificates to separate decoder under-use from representation insufficiency; show quantized-denoising studies for diagnosis, repair, and current-state recertification.

Result: Provided concrete bounds on global-optimality gaps (realized-residual bounds within 1.74–3.02× of the true gap), demonstrated coverage through eight challenges over 240 directions, and established diagnostic tools to distinguish representation issues from under-utilization, with practical recertification via quantized-denoising.

Conclusion: Global-gap conclusions require a justified coverage mechanism; passing a finite suite is suite-relative. The Training Under Challenge framework offers verifiable certificates, diagnostics, and recertification for neural-network training, enabling principled identification of whether optimization, representation, or trainer mismatch limits global optimality.

Abstract: A flat training curve does not reveal whether a neural network has reached a global optimum, is locally trapped, is representation-limited, or is mismatched to its trainer. We introduce Training Under Challenge, an executable-certificate framework in which predeclared, architecture-valid procedures construct complete alternatives in the same certified class and reevaluate the same objective. Any lower-valued candidate is a replayable witness that lower-bounds the checkpoint's empirical global-optimality gap. Passing a finite suite is only suite-relative; global-gap conclusions require a separately justified coverage mechanism. We define a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus that characterizes the largest gap compatible with passage. For squared loss, current block-decrease operators make coverage checkable and yield uniform and realized-residual bounds. We prove the converse frontier: without coverage, a first-order ReLU trainer can reach infinitely many exact conditional head optima while converging to a non-global point. On a channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation problem with known optimum, eight internal challenges cover all 240 audited output directions, and realized-residual bounds lie within factors of 1.74--3.02 of the true gap. Paired predictive certificates separate decoder under-use from representation insufficiency, while quantized-denoising studies demonstrate diagnosis, repair, and current-state recertification.

</details>


### [199] [Demand Transfer Estimation at Scale via Restricted Logit Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12680)
*Lakshya Garg,Deep Narayan Mishra,Swapnil Yadav,Haoan Wang,Sujal Alugubelli,Karthik Kumaran,Anupriya Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable DT-based framework estimates how demand for a target item transfers to other items if the item is removed, allowing independent item forecasts to be adjusted for substitution; validated on large item universes and multi-location data, showing improved forecasting under plausible substitution assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the inefficiency of forecasting demand for every possible assortment in very large item universes. The goal is to combine fast, independent item demand forecasts with cross-item substitution adjustments to enable accurate assortment-driven demand estimates at scale (1M+ items).

Method: Define Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients as the share of a target item's demand redirected to other items when the target is removed. Develop an estimation procedure that computes DTs for extremely large item sets, enabling adjustments to independent item forecasts to reflect inter-item substitutions. Validate the approach on real transaction data across multiple locations and categories, under reasonable substitution assumptions.

Result: Under plausible substitution assumptions, the proposed DT estimation procedure accurately learns the transfer coefficients and yields improvements in demand forecasting compared with baseline independent forecasts.

Conclusion: DT-based adjustments offer a scalable, accurate way to forecast demand for large assortments by decoupling item-level forecasting from substitution effects; effective for store assortment optimization provided the substitution assumptions hold and sufficient transaction data is available.

Abstract: Item demand forecasting is an integral component of store assortment optimization. Existing literature focuses on learning a suitable customer choice model and using this model to determine the value of an objective function (i.e. expected demand) with respect to an assortment proposal. However, for large item universe with many categories, this approach can prove inefficient, needing a separate demand forecast for every possible item assortment. An alternate approach exists whereby we combine the efficiency of forecasting item demand independently, while at the same time applying adjustments to the independent forecasts that account for the relations between item demand and the availability of other similar items on the shelf.
  Central to this approach is the estimation of Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients. These DT coefficients represent the percent of a particular target item's (item that the customer walked in the store to buy) demand that is redirected to each other item in the universe should the target item be removed from the shelf. We introduce an approach that allows us to compute these DT coefficients on large item universes (assortments having 1 million+ items). Experiments on data as well as historical transaction data for multiple locations within categories demonstrate that when certain reasonable assumptions about substitution behavior are satisfied, our procedure is able to accurately estimate underlying DT coefficients and lead to improvements in demand forecasting.

</details>


### [200] [Finding the Needle in a Haystack: Test-Time Analog Circuit Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12687)
*Fin Amin,Sounak Dutta,Paul D. Franzon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTARO adapts circuit representations online during Bayesian optimization to align embeddings with the figure of merit, improving search efficiency over fixed embeddings and DKL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Representation learning for BO often fixes embeddings after pretraining, causing misalignment with FoM; online adaptation is needed to reflect the optimization objective during search.

Method: Online deep-kernel BO: start with pretrained circuit embeddings, jointly learn a nonlinear feature transform and a Gaussian-process surrogate using observed FoM; update representation and GP after each evaluated circuit; compare to fixed-embedding BO and DKL across 40 settings.

Result: TTARO reduces regret AUC by 15.2% vs fixed BO and 20.7% vs DKL on average across 40 settings; improvements up to 46.7% in some cases.

Conclusion: Online representation adaptation during BO improves alignment with the optimization objective and enhances sample efficiency in analog circuit topology search; TTARO outperforms conventional baselines in most configurations.

Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient framework for analog circuit topology search, where evaluating each candidate topology can require costly simulation. However, representation-based BO methods typically treat circuit embeddings as fixed after encoder training. This creates a mismatch between representation learning and optimization: embeddings learned to encode or reconstruct circuit structure are not necessarily organized according to the figure of merit (FoM) being optimized. This paper introduces Test-Time Analog Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization (TTARO), an online deep-kernel BO framework that adapts circuit representations throughout the search process. Starting from pretrained circuit embeddings, TTARO jointly learns a nonlinear feature transformation and a Gaussian-process surrogate using the FoM labels of the circuits evaluated so far. Following each new evaluation, TTARO updates the representation and surrogate before selecting the next candidate. We compare TTARO with conventional Gaussian Process-based BO over fixed embeddings and with Deep Kernel Learning (DKL), which learns the representation only from the initial evaluated designs and keeps it fixed throughout the remainder of the search. By continually incorporating newly observed FoM labels into representation learning, TTARO aligns the search space with the optimization objective as BO progresses. In our experiments, TTARO reduces regret AUC by 15.2% on average relative to BO and by 20.7% relative to DKL across 40 encoder/kernel/acquisition settings, outperforming prior art in most settings with reductions as large as 46.7%.

</details>


### [201] [The Impact of Temporal Context Length and Encoding Strategies on Self-Supervised ECG Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12695)
*Ahmed Sameh,Ramzi Al-Sharawi,Yogatheesan Varatharajah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Longer temporal context (5–10 minutes) improves transfer and cross-session stability in self-supervised ECG models; continuous (patch) encoders outperform discretized token representations, suggesting quantization loses clinically relevant waveform detail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unclear whether short input windows and discretized token representations preserve information needed for rhythm inference and longitudinal consistency in ambulatory ECG data; need systematic evaluation of input horizon and representation.

Method: Controlled study on the Icentia11k single-lead ECG dataset varying (i) input horizon (16 s, 1 min, 5 min, 10 min) and (ii) front-end representation (continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs fixed vector-quantized tokens), with Transformer backbone and training protocol kept constant; evaluation via downstream abnormal rhythm detection and patient-level retrieval across sessions.

Result: Context length beyond 16 s yields stronger transfer and higher retrieval accuracy; strongest performance for 5- and 10-minute horizons; continuous patch embeddings outperform discretized tokens across horizons; quantization may discard clinically relevant waveform detail.

Conclusion: ECG foundation models should emphasize extended temporal context and continuous encoders to improve clinical prediction and similarity-based tasks; code and pretrained models are publicly available.

Abstract: Self-supervised electrocardiogram (ECG) models are often trained on a few seconds of ECG signal and, increasingly, on discretized token sequences. It remains unclear whether these choices sacrifice information needed for rhythm inference and longitudinal consistency in real-world ambulatory recordings. We present a controlled study on the Icentia11k single-lead dataset that varies (i) the input horizon (16 seconds, 1 minute, 5 minutes, and 10 minutes) and (ii) the front-end representation (continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs. fixed vector-quantized tokens), while holding the Transformer backbone and training protocol constant. Representations are assessed by downstream abnormal rhythm detection and by patient-level retrieval that probes cross-session stability. Our results show that increasing temporal context beyond 16-second snapshots yields stronger transfer and higher retrieval accuracy, with the strongest performance achieved by the 5- and 10-minute models, indicating improved capture of slow-varying rhythm dynamics and individual-specific structure. Across all evaluated horizons, continuous patch embeddings outperform discretized tokens, suggesting that quantization can discard clinically relevant waveform detail. These findings motivate ECG foundation models that emphasize extended context and continuous encoders for clinical prediction and similarity-based applications. Our code and pretrained models are publicly available at https://github.com/muha-0/ecg-ssl-representation-learning.

</details>


### [202] [Towards Socially Compliant Navigation in Deep Reinforcement Learning via Proxemics-Based Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12917)
*Takieddine Soualhi,Jacques Saraydaryan,Laetitia Matignon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a proxemics-based dense reward for DRL social navigation that uses a radial Gaussian-mixture field to model personal space and computes a robot-centric local cost over the robot's field of view, improving social metrics while maintaining navigation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between task-centric DRL navigation and social compliance by providing an interpretable, dense social learning signal based on Hall's proxemics.

Method: Model each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field; derive a robot-centric local cost in the robot's field of view; integrate this reward into established DRL navigation methods; evaluate in simulation across crowd scenarios, baselines, and densities using navigation and social metrics.

Result: The proposed reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation and maintains competitive navigation performance relative to other reward models.

Conclusion: A proxemics-based reward provides a dense, interpretable signal that enhances social compliance in DRL-based navigation without sacrificing efficiency.

Abstract: Developing effective robot navigation methods in crowded environments is essential for real-world applications. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) methods have improved navigation performance in crowded environments, they often focus primarily on task-centric objectives and underrepresent social compliance objectives. In this paper, we introduce a novel proxemics-based reward formulation for DRL social navigation that provides a dense, interpretable social learning signal while maintaining navigation efficiency. Our approach models each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field derived from Hall's proxemics theory and computes a robot-centric local cost over the robot's field of view. We integrate the proposed reward into established DRL navigation methods and evaluate it in simulation across multiple crowd scenarios, reward baselines, and crowd densities using both navigation metrics and social metrics. Results show that the proposed reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation while maintaining competitive navigation performance relative to the compared reward models.

</details>


### [203] [A Contract-Grade Verifier for LLM-Generated GPU Kernels, and a Native Blackwell Backward for the Gated-Linear-Recurrence Family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12700)
*Rishi Shah,Rishav Shrestha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Contract-grade verifier reveals that many ML-generated GPU kernels fail under tolerance-free, adversarial checks; progress in kernel generation is overstated when judged by standard simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fix latent, silent correctness bugs in machine-generated GPU kernels that pass loose, shape- and tolerance-based tests.

Method: Introduce a 12-adversarial-gates, tolerance-free verifier. Audit 2,638 kernels from a public harness; corroborate findings with threshold calibration, a positive control, and stratified hand audits; additionally verify an original kernel (GDN family) against a double-precision oracle and train five family members.

Result: 12 gates reveal 39.5% broken beyond any tolerance; 62.1% carry at least one violation. The public system’s test accepts 1,487 kernels that the verifier rejects, and only 14 the reverse. Independent controls (positive control, calibration sweep, 98.5% agreement with the reference correctness code, stratified audits) support the finding; the authors’ own kernel (GDN backward pass) passes only under their own checks, highlighting weaker real progress signals.

Conclusion: The field’s reported progress in kernel generation is overstated; tolerance-free contracts could close most of the gap, underscoring the need for stronger, contract-based verification.

Abstract: Systems that generate GPU kernels with language models report high correctness rates. Those rates come from a single loose test: run the kernel on a few random inputs at one fixed shape and accept it if the output is close to a reference. A kernel can pass that test and still be silently wrong. It can return an ordinary number where the true answer is a NaN or an infinity, differ from run to run, break when the shape changes, or accumulate in fp16 where the reference keeps an fp32 total. We build the instrument that checks correctness properly: a contract-grade verifier of twelve adversarial gates, each a property a correct kernel must satisfy, several of them tolerance-free, so no choice of threshold can explain a failure away. Aimed outward, the verifier audits 2,638 machine-generated kernels that a public system's own harness had already accepted as correct. It finds 39.5% broken beyond any tolerance argument and 62.1% carrying at least one violation. The field's standard test accepts 1,487 kernels the verifier rejects, against only 14 the other way. We defend the finding four independent ways: a 7/7 positive control, a threshold-calibration sweep, 98.5% agreement with the reference benchmark's own correctness code, and a stratified hand-audit. Aimed inward, the verifier judges a kernel of our own: the first native Blackwell tcgen05 training backward for the gated-linear-recurrence (GDN) family, including the reverse-state stage the field still runs on a fallback. We establish its correctness independently, against a double-precision oracle, and train five family members through it. The correctness signal behind reported progress in kernel generation is far weaker than the numbers suggest, and a set of tolerance-free contracts would close most of the gap.

</details>


### [204] [Federated Compositional Muon Optimizer for Matrix-Wise Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12710)
*Wang Yan,Feihu Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCoMuon introduces federated compositional optimization for matrix-wise problems; adds FedCoMuon-VR with variance reduction; proves convergence in non-i.i.d. non-convex setting; achieves O(ε^{-3}) sample complexity; empirically competitive and achieves best reported accuracy in several tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for efficient federated optimization in hierarchical matrix-wise compositional problems; existing Muon variants are not tailored for distributed structured problems; aim to improve convergence and sample efficiency.

Method: Develop FedCoMuon using compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum; extend to FedCoMuon-VR with momentum-based variance reduction; analyze convergence under non-i.i.d. and non-convex assumptions.

Result: Theoretical convergence guarantees established; FedCoMuon-VR attains O(ε^{-3}) sample complexity for ε-stationary points; empirical results show competitiveness with compositional baselines and sometimes state-of-the-art accuracy in robust FL and task-distributed risk-sensitive meta-learning.

Conclusion: FedCoMuon and FedCoMuon-VR extend Muon to federated matrix-wise compositional optimization; variance-reduction improves sample efficiency; demonstrated practical effectiveness in relevant federated learning settings.

Abstract: Muon, a more recently developed optimizer, is useful for matrix-wise models in AI areas. Although many works have studied Muon and its variants, these methods are still not particularly well-suited for hierarchical structured problems. To fill this gap, we propose an effective federated compositional Muon (FedCoMuon) optimizer to solve distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization problems. Specifically, our FedCoMuon optimizer builds on compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum. Moreover, we propose a variance reduced variant of FedCoMuon (FedCoMuon-VR) based on a momentum-based variance reduced technique. In theory, we analyze the convergence properties of our algorithms under the non-i.i.d. and non-convex settings. In particular, we prove that our FedCoMuon-VR obtains a lower sample complexity of $O(ε^{-3})$ for finding an $ε$-stationary solution than the existing FedMuon algorithms. Extensive numerical experiments on robust federated learning and task-distributed risk-sensitive meta learning show that our proposed methods are competitive with existing compositional baselines and achieve the best reported accuracy in several settings.

</details>


### [205] [Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping (PRISM): naming-error dissociations in language models and post-stroke aphasia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12717)
*Xiang Guan,Roger D. Newman-Norlund,Yong Yang,Saeed Ahmadi,Regan Willis,Nadra Salman,Kalil Warren,Srihari Nelakuditi,Chris Rorden,Leonardo Bonilha,Julius Fridriksson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM adapts neuroimaging subtraction analysis to perturbation-based LLM interpretability, yielding a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional specialization by paralleling brain lesion mapping with layer-wise LLM perturbations; results show robust phonemic dissociation and spatial clusters, with semantic effects inconclusive; ROI-level causal tests reserved for future work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a biologically grounded, falsifiable framework to test whether internal components of large language models are specialized for distinct cognitive operations, leveraging paradigms from lesion-deficit mapping and neuroimaging.

Method: Map seven Philadelphia Naming Test categories to LLM perturbations (seven seeds), perform pairwise error-class subtraction; treat each seed as a subject in a group analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along the transformer layer axis; structurally match with 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients using correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping; replicate findings on held-out splits; compare within-subject error-proportion differences in the LLM to between-subject correlation differences in cortex; assess spatial clustering and directionality (phonemic vs semantic).

Result: Recovered a robust phonemic-favoring dissociation, a deep-layer cluster, and a frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster across datasets, both replicated; semantic-favoring direction is consistently signed but not statistically significant on both substrates.

Conclusion: PRISM provides a falsifiable, spatially resolved approach to test functional specialization in transformer language models; stronger causal claims require future Stage-3 ROI-level interventions.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability of large language models lacks spatially resolved, falsifiable tools for testing whether internal components are specialized for distinct cognitive operations. We adapt subtraction analysis, the standard framework of human neuroimaging, from biological brains to perturbed transformers, and apply the same logic to both substrates in parallel. Building on the Brain-LLM Unified Model (BLUM), which showed that layer-perturbed LLaVA-1.6-Vicuna-13B error profiles match the lesion patterns of aphasic patients, we develop PRISM (Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping). PRISM maps the seven clinical Philadelphia Naming Test categories, subtracts error classes pairwise, and treats each perturbation seed as a subject in a group analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along the layer axis. We run a structurally matched analysis on 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients using correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping, and replicate both sides on held-out splits. The designs match in subject dimension (seeds, patients), spatial dimension (layers, atlas-parcellated cortex) and thresholding, but the contrast operator differs: a within-subject error-proportion difference for the LLM, a between-subject correlation difference for the cortex. Both substrates recover a robust phonemic-favoring dissociation, a deep layer cluster and a frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster, both replicating; the semantic-favoring direction is a consistently signed but non-significant trend on both. PRISM thus gives a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional-specialization claims in transformer language models. A confirmatory ROI-level intervention (PRISM Stage 3) licensing the strongest causal-mechanism claim is left to subsequent work.

</details>


### [206] [MAG: MAnifold Guided Semi-Supervised Multi-modal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12724)
*Zirui Cheng,Xun Xu,Tiankai Chen,Fady Rezk,Bowen Zheng,Xiaodong Shi,Shijie Li,Kangkang Lu,Bharadwaj Veeravalli,Nancy F. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAG uses manifold-guided semi-supervised learning to select in-context demonstrations for multi-modal ICL, leveraging unlabeled data via a two-stage propagation-and-selection process to reduce inference costs and boost performance in label-scarce settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the sensitivity of few-shot in-context learning to chosen demonstrations and underutilization of unlabeled multi-modal data; enable cost-efficient, high-quality demonstration selection without parameter updates.

Method: Construct a multi-modal graph; propagate relevance scores to unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling (stage 1); use multi-modal relevance to curate final demonstrations (stage 2). Textual features drive propagation; both text and visual features are important for final selection. Evaluate on eight multi-modal benchmarks in label-scarce regimes.

Result: MAG consistently surpasses strong baselines in low-label scenarios, delivering significant gains with a limited pseudo-labeling budget across eight benchmarks.

Conclusion: A semi-supervised, manifold-guided framework effectively enhances ICL for multi-modal models by leveraging unlabeled data; propagation in text space is particularly effective for relevance while combining modalities yields high-quality demonstrations and cost savings.

Abstract: Few-shot in-context learning (ICL) with multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) enables task adaptation without parameter updates, but its performance is highly sensitive to the quality and coverage of the selected demonstrations. While unlabeled multi-modal data is abundant, it remains elusive how to exploit them for ICL. We propose MAG (MAnifold-Guided semi-supervised in-context demonstra- tion selection), an efficient framework that leverages unlabeled data to improve multi-modal ICL. MAG formulates demonstration selection as a semi-supervised propagation problem on a multi-modal graph and adopts a two-stage strategy: (i) relevance score propagation identifies a compact set of high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling, reducing MLLM inference cost; (ii) multi-modal relevance is used to select the final demonstrations. We show that textual represen- tations are more effective for relevance propagation, while both visual and textual modalities are crucial for high-quality demonstration selection. Experiments on eight multi-modal benchmarks demonstrate that MAG consistently outperforms strong baselines in label-scarce regimes, achieving significant gains with a limited pseudo-labeling budget.

</details>


### [207] [A Cloud-Edge System for Multimodal Clinical Screening in Resource-Constrained Rural Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12745)
*Hei Ting,Chan,Chenwei Wu,Xueshen Liu,Zesen Zhao,Boyuan Zheng,Luis Filipe Nakayama,Michael G. Morley,Liyue Shen,Jiasi Chen,Z. Morley Mao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cloud–edge collaborative system enabling multimodal medical AI in bandwidth-constrained settings, achieving high diagnostic tool recall and precision with stable latency and lower token costs, matching/exceeding cloud-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resource-constrained rural healthcare where bandwidth, compute, and integration of modalities hinder access to specialist-level AI. Need for coordination across edge and cloud to enable comprehensive, efficient diagnostics.

Method: Lightweight domain-specific models on the edge convert raw medical data into compact, structured outputs; a cloud LLM synthesizes outputs into clinical summaries; an LLM-based orchestrator selects diagnostic tools based on patient context to ensure broad modality coverage while ignoring irrelevant inputs. Evaluation on 20 multimodal cases across cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening under three network profiles (500 kbps–5 Mbps).

Result: Achieves 98–99% diagnostic tool recall with 92–96% precision; matches or exceeds cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy; bandwidth-invariant latency of 25–35 seconds; 4–15x reduction in token cost.

Conclusion: Architectural design is key for efficient multimodal integration and improves factual grounding under deployment constraints, enabling robust performance in resource-constrained settings.

Abstract: Medical AI has demonstrated specialist-level diagnostic accuracy, yet these capabilities remain largely inaccessible in resource-constrained rural settings where bandwidth is scarce, compute is limited, and clinical decision-making requires integrating heterogeneous modalities. We introduce a cloud--edge collaborative architecture that addresses these constraints: lightweight, domain-specific models on the edge transform raw medical data into compact structured outputs, while a cloud LLM synthesizes these outputs into clinical summaries. An LLM-based orchestrator dynamically selects diagnostic tools based on patient context, promoting comprehensive modality coverage without processing irrelevant inputs. We evaluate on 20 multimodal clinical cases spanning cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening scenarios under three simulated network profiles (500,kbps--5,Mbps). The hybrid system achieves 98--99% diagnostic tool recall with 92--96% precision, matches or exceeds cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy, and maintains bandwidth-invariant latency (25--35,s) at 4--15x lower token cost. These results highlight the role of architectural design in enabling efficient multimodal integration and improving factual grounding compared to cloud-only approaches under deployment constraints.

</details>


### [208] [Decentralized Multi-Player Q-Learning in Episodic Markov Decision Processes with Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12753)
*Larissa Xu,King Bi,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized multi-player episodic tabular MDPs with three information-asymmetry settings; introduces mQ-learning, mQ-learning-intervals (Problems A,B) and mEXC, mEXC-Bellman (Problem C); achieves regret close to single-agent Q-learning rates up to logs; performance scales with the joint action space A_joint, becoming challenging as M grows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address learning in decentralized multi-agent RL under limited communication and information asymmetry, quantify regret, and compare to centralized baselines; understand how action-space growth across agents affects learnability.

Method: Problem A: unobserved actions with common rewards -> mQ-learning; Problem B: observed actions with independent rewards -> mQ-learning-intervals; Problem C: unobserved actions with independent rewards -> two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms mEXC and mEXC-Bellman. In all cases, agents follow a pre-agreed protocol and cannot communicate during learning. Regret analyzed over episodic MDPs with horizon H, states S, and total steps T=KH; joint action space A_joint=∏|A_i|.

Result: Regret bounds: A,B -> tilde O(sqrt(H^4 S A_joint T)); C -> tilde O(H (S A_joint)^{1/3} T^{2/3}). These match centralized joint-action benchmarks up to polylog factors and align with the single-agent Q-learning rate (Jin et al. 2018) when A_joint is small; bounds deteriorate as A_joint grows exponentially in M.

Conclusion: Decentralized learning under information asymmetry can achieve near-optimal rates similar to single-agent Q-learning for small numbers of players or small action spaces, though the joint-action explosion limits scalability. The two-phase approach yields favorable 2/3 exponent for Problem C. Open directions include tightening bounds, exploring lower bounds, extending to larger M or non-tabular settings, and characterizing communication-assisted enhancements.

Abstract: We study decentralized multi-player reinforcement learning in episodic tabular Markov decision processes (MDPs) under three forms of information asymmetry: (A) unobserved actions with common rewards, (B) observed actions with independent rewards, and (C) unobserved actions with independent rewards. Players cannot communicate during learning but may agree on a protocol a priori. For Problems A and B we propose \texttt{mQ-learning} and \texttt{mQ-learning-intervals}, achieving $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{H^4 S A_{\text{joint}}\, T})$ regret, where $H$ is the horizon, $S$ the state count, $T = KH$ the total steps, and $A_{\text{joint}} = \prod_{i=1}^M |\mathcal{A}_i|$ the joint action space across $M$ players. For Problem C we give \texttt{mEXC} and \texttt{mEXC-Bellman}, two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms with regret $\tilde{O}(H (S A_{\text{joint}})^{1/3} T^{2/3})$. Against the centralized joint-action benchmark, decentralized learning under information asymmetry matches the single-agent Q-learning rate of \cite{jin2018q} up to logarithmic factors. Because $A_{\text{joint}}$ grows exponentially in $M$, the bounds are most meaningful for small $M$ or small per-player action sets.

</details>


### [209] [Beyond Outcome Rewards: Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12764)
*Haoze Wu,Chuqiao Kuang,Tianyi Zhuang,Xiaoguang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Evidence Anchors and Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO) to improve credit assignment in long-horizon search agents by using privileged, concise step-level evidence and step-level weighting of updates for incorrect trajectories, yielding improved GRPO performance with minimal overhead on Qwen3-8B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep search agents face sparse per-trajectory rewards, making credit assignment difficult. On-policy distillation can leak privileged information from the teacher into the student, biasing exploration. A method is needed to leverage teacher guidance without corrupting the student’s search strategy.

Method: Evidence Anchors: concise step-level evidence snippets (privileged information) extracted from web to guide reasoning without revealing full answer paths. SSPO: converts teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied only to incorrect trajectories; outcome rewards determine the update direction while the teacher modulates the magnitude at each step, leaving correct trajectories untouched.

Result: Empirically, SSPO improves Qwen3-8B performance across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES, outperforming GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps, while incurring only ~5% additional per-step overhead (due to one extra forward pass).

Conclusion: Evidence Anchors plus SSPO provide a robust mechanism for step-wise credit assignment in long-horizon search, decoupling update direction from magnitude and preserving diversity of correct trajectories, with practical efficiency gains.

Abstract: Deep search agents operate over trajectories spanning dozens of steps, yet standard reinforcement learning provides only a single outcome reward per trajectory, which is far too sparse for effective credit assignment. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) addresses this by using the model's own logits as dense token-level teachers, but extending it to search agents introduces a fundamental tension: the teacher, having access to privileged information such as the correct answer, produces a distribution that differs systematically from the student's exploration-based reasoning, and naive distillation causes the student to inherit this information asymmetry rather than learn better search strategies. We resolve this tension through two contributions. First, we construct Evidence Anchors, which are concise, step-level evidence snippets extracted from the web, as privileged information that captures key reasoning steps without revealing the entire answer path. Second, we propose Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO), which converts teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied exclusively to incorrect trajectories. This design decouples what to update from how much to update: the outcome reward determines the direction of policy change, while the teacher modulates its magnitude at each step. Correct trajectories are left untouched, preserving their diversity. On Qwen3-8B, SSPO consistently outperforms GRPO across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES, surpassing or matching GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps while adding only about 5 percent overhead per step from a single additional forward pass.

</details>


### [210] [CoMedBench: A Multi-Source Benchmark of Synthetic Medical Data Fidelity and Downstream Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12805)
*Akanta Das,Al Amin Farhad,Mrinmoy Sarkar Anto,David Rehkopf,Ayin Vala,Tanmoy Sarkar Pias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoMedBench provides a reproducible benchmark to evaluate multiple synthetic-data generators for clinical data across static and temporal tasks, showing substantial retention of downstream signal in many settings, with CTGAN and TVAE variants performing differently by modality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy regulations and data-use restrictions hinder direct use of electronic health records for ML; synthetic data offers a practical alternative to preserve statistical and clinical structure while reducing exposure of sensitive data. A broad, reproducible benchmark is needed to assess when synthetic data supports model development across diverse tasks and datasets.

Method: Introduce CoMedBench, a shared training and evaluation engine that spans 37 dataset-task pairs across two modalities (20 static tabular tasks and 17 temporal ICU time-series tasks) drawn from seven public sources (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, eICU, UCI, BRFSS diabetes cohort, NHANES, and pycox datasets GBSG/METABRIC). Evaluate statistical fidelity and task utility by training models on synthetic data and testing on real data, comparing synthetic-to-real performance. Focus on two generators, CoMed-CTGAN and CoMed-TVAE, to assess how well synthetic data preserves signal.

Result: On tabular tasks, synthetic training data preserves most downstream signal: AUROC synthetic-to-real ratios are 90.6% for CoMed-CTGAN and 97.3% for CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are more challenging and generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN retains 81.6% AUROC and 64.0% AUPRC under imbalance sensitivity; CoMed-TVAE retains ~95% AUROC. Overall, synthetic data can preserve substantial downstream signal across diverse datasets, with performance varying by task and generator.

Conclusion: CoMedBench demonstrates that synthetic data can support a wide range of clinical ML tasks while highlighting generator- and task-specific limitations, particularly for temporal data and imbalance-sensitive metrics. The benchmark provides a reproducible framework to guide generator selection and evaluation for privacy-preserving ML in healthcare.

Abstract: Access to clinical data is essential for developing reliable healthcare machine learning systems, but direct use of electronic health records is constrained by privacy regulation, institutional review, data-use agreements, and the risk of re-identification. Synthetic data promises a practical alternative: it can preserve useful statistical and clinical structure while reducing exposure of sensitive patient records. Prior studies often evaluate a single generator, one dataset, or a narrow downstream task, making it difficult to know when synthetic data can support model development and when it fails to preserve task-critical signal. We introduce CoMedBench, a reproducible benchmark that evaluates a family of generators under a common clinical-validity framework and one shared training and evaluation engine, spanning static tabular and temporal downstream tasks on established critical-care datasets. In total the benchmark spans 37 dataset-task pairs across two modalities consists of 20 static tabular and 17 temporal ICU time-series-drawn from seven public data sources: three intensive-care databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU) together with the UCI Machine Learning Repository, the CDC BRFSS diabetes cohort (2015), NHANES (1999-2014), and the pycox survival datasets (GBSG and METABRIC). The benchmark evaluates both statistical fidelity and task utility by comparing models trained and tested across real and synthetic data. In these settings, synthetic training data preserves most of the downstream signal: on tabular tasks the reference generator CoMed-CTGAN retains a mean AUROC utility (the synthetic-to-real performance ratio) of 90.6%, rising to 97.3% for the strongest generator, CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are harder and more generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN retains 81.6% (AUROC) and only 64.0% under the imbalance-sensitive AUPRC, whereas CoMed-TVAE still retains ~95% (AUROC).

</details>


### [211] [HiRoute: Hierarchical Routed Prompt Tuning for Safety Alignment of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12821)
*Fangzhou Chen,Shiji Zhao,Mengyang Wang,Qihui Zhu,Ranjie Duan,Maoxun Yuan,Xingxing Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiRoute introduces an input-adaptive, hierarchical prompt-tuning framework for safety in LLMs. It separates category-agnostic safety control from category-specific guidance using a lightweight router and a mixture of prompt experts, learned via preference optimization and applied at inference based on risk scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static safety prompts struggle to maintain cross-category safety boundaries while remaining helpful for benign inputs. An adaptive, multi-level prompting strategy can tailor responses to varying risk profiles without excessive refusals.

Method: 1) Train a lightweight hierarchical router on frozen LLM representations to detect harmful intent and output multi-label risk scores. 2) Freeze backbone and router; use alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse-grained prompt and multiple fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings. 3) At inference, benign inputs bypass the safety branch; risky inputs are processed with the shared prompt plus a router-weighted mixture of risk-specific prompt experts.

Result: HiRoute achieves high safety rates across multiple safety benchmarks while preserving helpfulness, reducing over-refusal, and maintaining competitive performance on general-purpose tasks. Experiments span three instruction-tuned models.

Conclusion: An input-adaptive hierarchical prompting strategy effectively separates safety control from content guidance, enabling robust, category-aware risk handling without sacrificing general utility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to harmful requests and jailbreak attacks. Parameter-efficient safety alignment methods based on prompt tuning typically rely on a single global prompt or externally selected prompt modules. Such static designs struggle to maintain a cross-category safety boundary while generating constructive responses tailored to specific risks and avoiding over-refusal of benign inputs. To address these limitations, we propose HiRoute, an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that separates category-agnostic safety control from category-specific response guidance. HiRoute first trains a lightweight hierarchical router on representations extracted from a frozen LLM to jointly detect harmful intent and predict multi-label risk scores. It then freezes both the backbone model and the router and uses preference optimization with alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse-grained prompt and a set of fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings. At inference time, benign inputs bypass the safety branch, whereas risky inputs are processed using the shared prompt together with a router-weighted mixture of risk-specific prompt experts. Experiments across three instruction-tuned models show that HiRoute achieves high safety rates across multiple safety benchmarks while preserving safe-response helpfulness, reducing over-refusal, and maintaining competitive performance on general-purpose tasks.

</details>


### [212] [Fast A/B/n Testing: Exact Multi-Policy Comparison via Tree-Coupled Feedback Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12831)
*Yuxiao Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (TCAB) shares rewards across history-dependent policies via a maximally coupled tree, reducing reward queries while preserving each policy's finite-horizon distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce experimental cost when evaluating many adaptive decision policies by enabling exact feedback-sharing without altering individual policy laws.

Method: Construct a predictable tree over policy histories; apply maximal coupling to parent–child context–action laws; share one reward per matched tree edge; derive a pathwise identity N(T)=T+sum D_{e,t}; optimize over trees (min-spanning tree for current round), achieving edge-local optimality.

Result: For fixed J, each policy achieves sublinear pseudo-regret and E[N(T)]=T+o(T) (vs JT for independent runs); finite-sample variance bounds for pairwise policy contrasts; empirical results show substantial improvements in cost–precision frontier across reward-model, language-model evaluation, and adaptive search tasks.

Conclusion: TCAB provides an exact feedback-sharing framework with near-optimal reward-query costs and provable guarantees, delivering efficient and reliable evaluation of multiple adaptive policies.

Abstract: Online platforms increasingly compare many adaptive decision policies---ranking systems, recommendation algorithms, pricing rules, and language-model agents---while each reward-bearing interaction can be costly or risky. A direct A/B/n design gives each of $J$ policies its own horizon-$T$ trajectory and therefore uses $JT$ outcomes. We introduce Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (\TCAB), an exact feedback-sharing design for arbitrary history-dependent contextual-bandit policies. At each round, a predictable tree connects the current policy histories; every parent--child context--action law is maximally coupled, and one reward is shared within each component of matched tree edges. Every policy retains exactly its standalone finite-horizon trajectory law, even though the policies are deliberately dependent. If $D_{e,t}$ records a mismatch on tree edge $e$ at round $t$, the number of reward queries satisfies the pathwise identity $N(T)=T+\sum_{t,e}D_{e,t}$ and hence equals $T$ plus cumulative tree-edge total variation in expectation. This cost is conditionally optimal among exact edge-local designs on the selected tree, and a current-round minimum-spanning tree is myopically optimal among tree designs. For fixed $J$, sublinear pseudo-regret of every policy and almost-sure uniqueness of the oracle action imply $\mathbb{E}[N(T)]=T+o(T)$, versus $JT$ for independent runs. We also obtain finite-sample variance bounds for pairwise policy contrasts. Experiments on reward-model evaluation, multiple-choice language-model evaluation, and adaptive search policies demonstrate substantial improvements in the cost--precision frontier.

</details>


### [213] [A Compositional Theory of Curvature in Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12869)
*Hrithik Suresh,Sahil Sidheekh,Shelar Parth Vijay,Yasir Z,Sriraam Natarajan,Narayanan Chatapuram Krishnan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Global sharpness regularization for probabilistic circuits is mis-specified due to their compositional curvature; a per-sum-node decomposition shows each node's Hessian trace factors into a circuit flow and a local sharpness term. An adaptive, locality-aware regularizer preserves EM updates and recovers generalization without sacrificing robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ProbabilisticCircuits admit exact Hessian-based curvature measures, but global sharpness regularization can bias learning and cause underfitting due to the compositional structure of PCs. A more faithful regularizer should account for local curvature and node usage.

Method: Derive exact factorization of each sum node's contribution to the Hessian trace into a product of circuit flow (node usage) and a local sharpness term (output distribution). Propose an adaptive sharpness-aware regularizer that penalizes nodes based on intrinsic local curvature while preserving closed-form EM updates.

Result: The Hessian-trace factorization explains why global regularization is depth-biased and prone to underfitting. Empirically, the proposed adaptive, local regularizer recovers the generalization lost under global regularization and retains the robustness benefits of sharpness-aware learning, without breaking EM updates.

Conclusion: Local, composition-aware regularization for probabilistic circuits yields better generalization and preserves optimization properties, highlighting the inadequacy of global sharpness regularization for PC architectures.

Abstract: Probabilistic Circuits (PCs) are generative models that support exact inference and, unlike deep neural networks, admit an exact and tractable measure of loss-surface curvature: the trace of the Hessian of the log-likelihood. Recent work regularizes this trace globally to bias learning toward flatter, better generalizing optima. We show that treating sharpness as a global regularizer can be misspecified for PCs, whose curvature is inherently compositional. We prove that each sum node's contribution to the Hessian trace factorizes exactly into its circuit flow, which measures how heavily the node is used, and a local sharpness term determined by its output distribution. This decomposition provides insights into why global sharpness regularization is depth biased and can lead to underfitting. Building on it, we introduce an adaptive sharpness aware regularizer that penalizes nodes based on intrinsic local curvature and preserves closed form EM updates. We also show that empirically, this targeted regularization recovers the generalization that global regularization sacrifices while retaining the robustness and benefits of sharpness aware learning.

</details>


### [214] [Sustaining Plasticity via Learnable Wavelet Activations in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12874)
*Zeyang Zhang,Tieliang Gong,Junyan Lu,Weizhan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learnable wavelet activation with dynamic injection counters spectral bias and plasticity loss in continual learning, backed by L2-approximation theory and decoupled learning rates, achieving state-of-the-art results on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-form activations exhibit low-frequency spectral bias; unconstrained learnable activations risk catastrophic forgetting. A hybrid, learnable wavelet activation together with dynamic injection aims to restore plasticity while preserving past knowledge.

Method: Decompose activations into low- and high-frequency components via a learnable wavelet; use dynamic wavelet injection guided by a loss-driven trigger; apply regularization to stabilize older knowledge; adopt a decoupled learning-rate mechanism to restore high-frequency plasticity; provide formal derivations for loss-driven injection and L2-approximation guarantees.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: necessity of the hybrid wavelet architecture for efficient L2 approximation; decoupled learning rate restores high-frequency plasticity; formal derivation of injection mechanism. Empirical results show improved trainability and generalization; state-of-the-art performance across diverse continual learning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Hybrid wavelet activation is essential for efficient L2 approximation and flexible plasticity in continual learning; the framework achieves strong empirical performance while maintaining stability across tasks.

Abstract: Plasticity loss has emerged as a critical challenge in continual learning that significantly hinders the acquisition of sequential tasks. While optimizing activation designs offers a potential solution, current fixed-form functions suffer from an inherent spectral bias towards low-frequency variations, whereas learnable variants permit unconstrained updates that induce catastrophic forgetting. To address these limitations, we propose a novel learnable wavelet activation that decomposes the activation function into low-frequency and high-frequency components to explicitly counter spectral bias. Furthermore, we employ dynamic wavelet injection to adaptively enhance plasticity for new tasks, alongside a regularization strategy to ensure the stability of previous learned knowledge. Theoretically, we provide rigorous mathematical guarantees for the proposed framework, proving the structural necessity of the hybrid wavelet architecture for efficient $L^2$ approximation and demonstrating that the decoupled learning rate mechanism successfully restores network plasticity for high-frequency information. Additionally, we provide a formal derivation of the loss-driven injection trigger mechanism to precisely guide the injection. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that our approach maintains superior trainability and generalization throughout the learning process and achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse continual learning benchmarks.

</details>


### [215] [Robust data-driven discovery of fractional differential equations via weak formulations and Pareto-based subset selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12879)
*Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weak-Pareto combines an adjoint-consistent weak formulation for fractional terms with Pareto-based model selection over discrete term types and continuous fractional orders, enabling robust, parsimonious discovery of fractional PDEs from noisy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discover fractional PDE operators from noisy measurements where fractional derivatives amplify noise and derivative orders are unknown; require noise-robust, parsimonious identification.

Method: Use an adjoint-consistent weak formulation to move fractional operators onto smooth test functions, replacing pointwise differentiation with smoothing integration. For linear RHS terms, derive noise-suppressing features; for nonlinear terms, partial suppression. Fit coefficients with ridge regression within a branch-aware differential-evolution search over fractional orders; select support size via validation-error vs complexity elbow; use a Pareto-based selection over term types and orders.

Result: Weak-Pareto recovers parsimonious operators across fractional advection–diffusion, reaction–diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks, even under multiplicative and additive noise. The weak library enhances noise robustness; continuous-order Pareto search avoids fixed-dictionary failures; outperforms unregularised strong-form baselines and a neural baseline on advection–diffusion with lower runtime and more consistent recovery.

Conclusion: Weak-Pareto provides a robust, scalable framework for data-driven discovery of fractional PDEs from noisy data, delivering stable operator recovery and improved runtime versus neural baselines, with ablations confirming the value of the weak formulation and Pareto-based order selection.

Abstract: Fractional partial differential equations describe nonlocal dynamics, but discovering them from noisy data is difficult because fractional differentiation amplifies high-frequency measurement noise and the derivative orders are unknown. We propose Weak-Pareto, which combines an adjoint-consistent weak formulation of fractional terms with Pareto-based subset selection over discrete term types and continuous fractional orders. For linear right-hand-side terms, the adjoint transfers fractional operators from measured fields to smooth test functions, replacing noise-sensitive pointwise differentiation with smoothing integration; for nonlinear terms, the noise-suppression effect is partial yet useful. Coefficients are fitted by ridge regression within a branch-aware differential-evolution search over the orders. The support size is then selected at the validation-error-complexity elbow. We show that the variance of fixed linear right-hand-side weak features vanishes under grid refinement, whereas noise amplification in pointwise fractional features increases with derivative order. Across fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks, Weak-Pareto recovers parsimonious structures from clean and noisy measurements. In controlled advection-diffusion and Burgers comparisons, it retains the correct support at every tested multiplicative-noise level, whereas the unregularised strong-form counterpart largely fails once noise is introduced; this advantage persists under additive Gaussian noise. Ablations show that the weak library drives noise robustness and that continuous-order Pareto search avoids the support-selection failure of a dense fixed dictionary. On the advection-diffusion benchmark, Weak-Pareto yields more consistent operator recovery and substantially lower measured runtime than a contemporary neural baseline.

</details>


### [216] [Adaptive $k$ Nearest Neighbors Classifier via Granular Ball Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12903)
*Xiaoyu Lian,Shuyin Xia,Hongxuan He,Lifeng Shen,Guoyin Wang,Xinbo Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive and efficient KNN via granular-ball computing; dynamic k selection and robust local neighborhoods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KNN performance hinges on choosing k; conventional methods struggle with noise and data distributions; propose a coarse-to-fine granular-ball framework with Fisher criterion to create multi-granularity representations and adaptively determine k.

Method: Two-stage approach: training builds coarse granular balls; Fisher criterion guides splitting/stopping to obtain multi-granularity representation. Prediction locates nearest granule with weighted distance, constructs an adaptive neighborhood around the test point, and sets k equal to the number of samples in that neighborhood.

Result: Empirical results show improved accuracy and efficiency over several KNN variants across multiple datasets; code available at GitHub.

Conclusion: Granular-ball based KNN provides stable local information, robustness to noise, and automatic, data-driven k selection through neighborhood size; enhances both accuracy and speed.

Abstract: The $k$-Nearest Neighbor~(KNN) algorithm is widely used across various tasks. The selection of the $k$ value is a key issue because it significantly impacts performance. In this paper, an adaptive and efficient KNN approach via granular-ball computing is proposed. The method consists of two stages. \textcolor{black}{In the training stage, the dataset is first coarsely partitioned to reduce the complexity of data distributions within a granular ball, and then the Fisher criterion is introduced to control ball splitting and stopping, yielding a multi-granularity granular ball representation. In the prediction stage, the nearest granular ball is first located through a weighted distance mechanism, and an adaptive neighborhood is then constructed around the test sample. The effective $k$ value is dynamically determined by the actual number of samples contained in this neighborhood. The neighborhood induced by the nearest granular ball provides more stable local group information, thereby improving robustness against noise and local perturbations.} Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing KNN variants across multiple datasets in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. The code has been open-sourced for reproducibility: https://github.com/lianxiaoyu724/Adaptive-GBKNN.

</details>


### [217] [EGRL: Edge generation-guided relation-aware learning for RNA-protein interaction prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12906)
*Danyu Li,Ling Zhou,Rubing Huang,Xian Zhong,Bin Zou,Kui Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGRL for RPIP combines implicit meta-path learning, multi-relation attention, and a graph-edge generator for cold-start molecules, achieving competitive overall performance and strong cold-start generalization (AUROC 0.867, AUPR 0.861; +8.6% AUROC, +5.0% AUPR vs SOTA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle data sparsity and cold-start generalization in RNA–protein interaction prediction by moving beyond homogeneous graphs and handcrafted meta-paths.

Method: Implicit meta-path learning; multi-relation-aware attention; graph generator predicting soft edges for unseen molecules; multi-feature fusion predictor; joint optimization with primary task and auxiliary generator loss.

Result: Competitive results on four benchmarks; notable improvement in cold-start scenarios with AUROC 0.867 and AUPR 0.861 for unknown molecules, outperforming previous methods by 8.6% and 5.0%, respectively.

Conclusion: EGRL enhances generalization to unseen molecules in RPIP and offers a scalable framework for relation-aware, edge-augmented GNNs; code will be released.

Abstract: RNA-Protein Interactions (RPIs) are critical for regulating cellular functions. While traditional wet-lab experiments for RPI detection are costly and time-consuming, Deep Learning (DL) methods provide an efficient computational alternative for RPI Prediction (RPIP). In particular, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are promising, as they naturally model RPI networks. However, existing GNN-based methods often rely on homogeneous graphs or predefined meta-paths, which limit their ability to handle data sparsity and to generalize to cold-start scenarios involving unknown molecules. To address these limitations, we propose Edge Generation-guided Relation-aware Learning (EGRL), a novel framework with several key components: implicit meta-path learning to capture relational semantics without handcrafted paths; a multi-relation-aware attention mechanism for adaptive fusion of interaction patterns; a graph generator that predicts potential ("soft") edges to support cold-start nodes; and a multi-feature fusion predictor for final interaction scoring. EGRL is jointly trained with a primary task loss and an auxiliary generator loss. Comprehensive evaluations on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that EGRL achieves competitive overall performance. More importantly, it exhibits superior generalization in cold-start settings, achieving an Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.867 and an Area Under the Precision-Recall curve (AUPR) of 0.861 on unknown molecules, corresponding to improvements of 8.6% in AUROC and 5.0% in AUPR over prior state-of-the-art methods. The code will be released soon.

</details>


### [218] [Revisiting Overestimation Bias Problem of Q-learning: Settling Large Discrete Action Space via Action Intersection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12912)
*Pu Li,Tao Tan,Hong Xie,Xiaoyu Shi,Mingsheng Shang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Action intersection introduces semi-decoupled Q-learning to mitigate overestimation bias in large action spaces by sharing data between two Q-functions, enabling controllable bias from underestimation to overestimation and fine-grained control; shows strong empirical gains in tabular and deep RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overestimation bias worsened in large action spaces; existing coupling (positive bias) and decoupling (negative bias) paradigms suffer bottlenecks; need a mechanism to control bias while leveraging benefits of both paradigms.

Method: Propose action intersection: two Q-functions share a fraction of trajectory data; if a sample is shared, update via coupling; otherwise update via decoupling; adjust shared data fraction to control bias; fine-grained control via adjustable intersection size.

Result: Deep RL experiments show drastic improvements over SOTA baselines; tabular experiments reveal rationale for performance gains.

Conclusion: Action intersection is a simple, effective strategy to relieve overestimation bias in large action spaces, offering a tunable bias range and fine granularity.

Abstract: This paper considers the overestimation bias problem of Q-learning in the setting of a large action space, for the purpose of relieving the bottleneck of existing methods. We find that the large action space increases the randomness in Q-value estimation. The randomness makes two paradigms that drive the major literature on the overestimation problem have their own bottlenecks: the coupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with the same Q-function, always has a positive bias. This is because randomness leads to some actions having abnormally high estimated values than their true values, and the coupling methods prefer these actions. The decoupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with two independent Q-functions, always has a negative bias. This is because randomness increases the estimation gap between the two independent Q-tables for the same action. This paper shows that action intersection can be a simple yet powerful strategy to relieve these bottlenecks. The action intersection strategy enables semi-decoupling via two designs: (1) it allows two Q-functions to share a certain fraction of trajectory data; (2) if a data sample is shared, each Q-function is updated using the coupling paradigm; otherwise, using the decoupling paradigm. Two properties make the action intersection strategy powerful: (1) attaining a large bias range, i.e., varying the data sharing fraction, the estimation bias varies from underestimating to overestimating; (2) fine granularity: the action intersection size can be made arbitrarily finer to enable finer control. We consider two experiment settings, i.e., tabular and deep RL, deep RL experiments show that our method outperforms several SOTA baselines drastically; tabular experiments reveal why our method can achieve superior performance.

</details>


### [219] [Momentum as Residual-Driven Multiplier Correction for Deep Learning Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12925)
*Zhixin Ren,Yau Lyu,Congrong Li,Liping Zhang,Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces AIM, an ADMM-inspired momentum framework that decouples update geometry from acceleration via residual-splitting, and extends it with RADAR, a Relativistic Adaptive Gradient Descent with Accelerated Residuals. Proves stochastic convergence and reports empirical gains across vision, language modeling, and reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify the relationship between momentum recursion, update geometry, and acceleration in deep learning optimizers; provide a principled framework to separate geometry and subproblem approximation for improved optimization dynamics.

Method: AIM uses residual-penalty variable splitting to reinterpret momentum as a multiplier-like correction; recovers EMA of gradients from an ADMM-style update; separates geometry (residual penalty) from acceleration (objective subproblem approximation).
