RADAR builds on AIM with relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering to improve update direction and momentum estimation; analyzed via a variance-perturbed Lyapunov drift to establish stochastic convergence.

Result: The approach yields convergence guarantees under stochastic settings and empirical improvements over strong adaptive baselines across supervised vision tasks, language modeling, and reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: A unified framework to disentangle geometry and acceleration in momentum-based optimizers; provides theoretical convergence and practical performance gains, with RADAR offering a robust, adaptive mechanism for momentum estimation and update direction across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Momentum-based optimizers are widely used in modern deep learning, yet the relations among momentum recursion, update geometry, and acceleration remain only partially understood. We develop an $\textbf{A}$DMM-$\textbf{I}$nspired $\textbf{M}$omentum (AIM) framework based on residual-penalty variable splitting, which interprets momentum as a multiplier-like correction driven by the splitting residual. AIM recovers the exponential moving average of gradients from an ADMM-style multiplier update and separates two mechanisms that are usually intertwined in practical optimizers: the residual penalty determines the update geometry, whereas the approximation of the objective-related subproblem determines the acceleration form. Building on AIM, we propose $\textbf{R}$elativistic $\textbf{A}$daptive gradient $\textbf{D}$escent with $\textbf{A}$ccelerated $\textbf{R}$esidual (RADAR), which combines relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering to improve the update direction and momentum estimation. We establish stochastic convergence through a variance-perturbed Lyapunov drift analysis. Experiments on supervised vision learning, language modeling, and reinforcement learning show that RADAR achieves consistent improvements over strong adaptive optimizer baselines.

</details>


### [220] [H-VAEP and H-xT: Valuing Offensive On-the-Ball Actions in Handball by Estimating Probabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12926)
*Julius Broermann,Oliver Müller,Michael Döring,Jochen Baumeister*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First adaptation of xT and VAEP to handball (H-xT, H-VAEP) using five seasons of tracking data; shows robust, discriminative player ratings and emphasis on build-up; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in handball analytics for action valuation and multi-player sequences; adapt football xT/VAEP frameworks to handball.

Method: Develop handball-native court zoning for xT; compare with standard rectangular grids via simulations; tailor VAEP features and context length to limit team-identity leakage; evaluate on five Bundesliga seasons; release code.

Result: H-xT demonstrates robustness with court-based zoning; H-VAEP yields stable, discriminative player ratings that emphasize build-up play; complete code repository released for deployment.

Conclusion: Presents the first comprehensive handball adaptation of xT/VAEP, enabling robust, interpretable player valuation and practical deployment by clubs.

Abstract: Traditional player evaluation in professional handball relies on basic box-score metrics or heuristic indices, which fail to credit the multi-player build-up chain. While football (soccer) analytics has adopted Expected Threat (xT) and Valuing Actions by Estimating Probabilities (VAEP), these event-based action valuation frameworks have not yet been adapted to handball. In this paper, we present the first comprehensive adaptation and evaluation of xT and VAEP for handball, utilizing five seasons of tracking-derived event data from the Handball Bundesliga. We develop Handball-xT (H-xT) using a handball-native court zoning layout, demonstrating via simulations that it is systematically more robust than standard rectangular grids. We optimize Handball-VAEP (H-VAEP) by tailoring its feature space and selecting the context length to limit team-identity leakage. Our evaluation shows that H-VAEP yields exceptionally stable, discriminative, and intuitive player ratings that highlight build-up play. Finally, we release our complete code repository to help professional clubs deploy these models.

</details>


### [221] [Multi-perspective Imbalance-Conscious 6G Beamforming Optimization and Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12929)
*Chukwunonso Henry Nwokoye,Blessing Oluchi Iloka,Chikwue V. Umeugoji,Christopher Anene Egemba,Nnenna D. Duroha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic ML study on 6G-IoT beamforming optimization (6GBO) comparing predictive power of feature groups and exploring unsupervised clustering; network features prevail; environment/device type drive clustering; bandwidth/IoT sensors/mobility are globally important; future work includes deep RL for throughput/latency optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the predictive value of network, environmental, device, and vision features for 6GBO and to explore unsupervised clustering perspectives to guide beamforming decisions.

Method: Supervised and unsupervised ML; evaluation across feature groups; imbalance-aware experiments; clustering with K-means, DBSCAN, hierarchical; explainability analysis of feature importance; metrics include recall, F1-score, ROC-AUC, Silhouette, Elbow, Davies-Bouldin; future DL/RL directions.

Result: Network features show stronger prediction power (higher recall, F1, ROC-AUC) than other groups; clustering primarily influenced by deployment environment and device type rather than mobility; global feature importance highest for bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility.

Conclusion: Suggests extending with deep and reinforcement learning to predict throughput/latency and optimize performance rewards such as SNR; highlights the pivotal role of network-related features and deployment context in 6GBO.

Abstract: The study presents a systematic machine learning (ML) study of 6G-IoT beamforming optimization (6GBO) using supervised and unsupervised approaches. We compared the predictive power of network, environmental, device, and vision feature groups for 6GBO. Additionally, it addressed other unsupervised perspectives that can enhance 6GBO, including clustering network scenarios using methods such as K-means, DBSCAN, and hierarchical clustering. Several imbalance-aware experiments revealed that network features possess better prediction power than device, environmental, and vision feature groups, as evidenced by their recall, F1-score and ROC-AUC values. For unsupervised ML exploration (assessed using Elbow, Silhouette score, and Davies-Bouldin Index methods), the results indicate that the deployment environment and type of device primarily influence clustering, rather than mobility-based attributes. Furthermore, the explainability analysis showed that bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility possess higher global feature importance across the feature groups. In the future, we would apply deep and reinforcement learning techniques to predict throughput/latency or to optimize rewards determined by performance indicators like SNR enhancement

</details>


### [222] [Diagnosing JEPA World Models with Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12939)
*Guo An,Zijing Wu,Honghua Dong,Yuhao Yan,Zixuan Gui,Haochong Chen,Shanzhao Ruan,Xiang Wang,Yurong Ling,Qi Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACPC is a diagnostic for evaluating robustness of joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) under visual perturbations, using pairwise rollout divergence to define Invariance Radius (IR) and Separation Rate (SR); demonstrates predictive/cost correlations and transferability across tasks and architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual perturbations can change latent representations in JEPAs, undermining their invariance to nuisance appearance. Grounded in a bisimulation idea, ACPC quantifies when two observations should be treated as the same state based on action-conditioned futures.

Method: Compute divergence between clean and perturbed histories rolled forward under identical action sequences (ACPC). Prove that this divergence upper-bounds perturbation-induced changes in multi-step prediction error and planner cost. From pairwise ACPC derive two metrics: Invariance Radius (IR) capturing rollout spread for clean vs perturbed histories, and Separation Rate (SR) assessing whether distinct states remain distinguishable after rollout. Evaluate on four visual control tasks; examine cross-task transfer (LeWM) of IR-SR; test robustness to blur and resize; report similar trends for PLDM with a different architecture.

Result: Pairwise ACPC forecasts perturbation-induced changes in prediction and cost. IR-SR transfers across tasks on LeWM, and the diagnostic remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM shows parallel diagnostic trends with another architecture.

Conclusion: ACPC provides a principled, practical diagnostic for robustness of JEPAs to visual perturbations. IR captures the spread of clean vs perturbed rollouts, while SR checks state distinguishability after rollout. The measures generalize across tasks and architectures and correlate with prediction error and planner cost, offering a usable tool to diagnose and guide robust representation learning.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models that predict in a compact latent space rather than in pixels, reducing the pressure to model nuisance appearance. Yet this provides no guarantee against visual perturbations: they can still alter the encoded representation and affect subsequent action-conditioned predictions. Bisimulation captures this requirement precisely: two observations should be treated as the same state only when their action-conditioned consequences agree. Guided by this criterion, we introduce Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC), a diagnostic that measures how far a clean history and a visually perturbed view of it diverge after being rolled forward under the same action sequence. We prove that this divergence bounds the perturbation-induced change in multi-step prediction error and planner cost. Building on pairwise ACPC, we define two complementary measures: the Invariance Radius (IR) summarizes clean-perturbed rollout spread, while the Separation Rate (SR) checks whether different states remain distinguishable after rollout. Experiments on four visual control tasks show that pairwise ACPC predicts perturbation-induced prediction and cost changes. On LeWM, the IR-SR screen transfers across tasks, and the joint diagnostic remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM exhibits similar diagnostic trends under a different architecture.

</details>


### [223] [CardioState-JEPA: Delay-Aware Cross-Modal Learning of a Shared Cardiac Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12944)
*Hamza Shafiq,Hung Manh Pham,Bin Zhu,Pan Zhou,Jun Hu,Aaqib Saeed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multimodal cardiac foundation model (CardioState-JEPA) learns a shared physiological representation across ECG, PPG, and PCG using a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture with a cross-modal delay aligner; pretrained on unimodal data and aligned with paired data, achieving strong downstream performance across 25 tasks, and surpassing self-supervised baselines while rivaling supervised/privileged models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current cardiac foundation models are modality-specific and fail to exploit shared physiology across ECG, PPG, and PCG. Scarcity of synchronized multi-sensor data and temporal misalignment between electrical, mechanical, and hemodynamic signals motivate a cross-modal, time-aware, shared representation learning approach.

Method: Heterogeneous waveforms are mapped into a common token space and processed by a single shared Transformer encoder. The pretraining objective masks latent cardiac states and predicts them, emphasizing shared physiology rather than sensor appearance. A learned delay aligner compensates for cardiac-time offsets to align modalities. Training proceeds in two stages: first, learn intra-modality structure from abundant unimodal data; second, use paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time. Evaluation is performed with a frozen encoder across 25 downstream tasks spanning ECG, PPG, and PCG.

Result: Across 25 benchmarks, CardioState-JEPA improves average AUROC: PPG classification by 8.2 points, PCG murmur detection by 18.8 points, and ECG classification by 15.5 points over the best self-supervised baseline. The encoder matches or exceeds cardiac models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous cardiac signals can mutually supervise a single physiology-focused foundation model, enabling a unified representation that transfers across modalities and tasks, and potentially reducing reliance on labeled data or privileged information.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG), photoplethysmography (PPG), and phonocardiography (PCG) provide complementary views of the same cardiac cycle, yet existing cardiac foundation models are trained for a single sensing modality, leaving the shared physiology across sensors unexploited. We introduce CardioState-JEPA, a cardiac foundation model to learn a single shared representation jointly across ECG, PPG, and PCG, built on a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture. The model maps heterogeneous waveforms into a common token space, processes them with a single shared Transformer encoder, and learns by predicting masked latent cardiac states, placing the pretraining target on shared physiology rather than sensor-specific waveform appearance. To handle the temporal offsets between electrical, mechanical, and hemodynamic events, cross-modal prediction uses a learned delay aligner that matches signals at the corresponding cardiac time. Because synchronized multi-sensor recordings are scarce, CardioState-JEPA first learns within-modality structure from abundant unimodal data and then uses paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time. Evaluated as a frozen encoder across 25 downstream tasks spanning ECG, PPG, and PCG, our encoder improves average PPG classification by 8.2 AUROC points, PCG murmur detection by 18.8 AUROC points, and ECG classification by 15.5 AUROC points over the best self-supervised signal baseline and matches or exceeds cardiac models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks. These results establish that heterogeneous cardiac signals can mutually supervise a single foundation model of cardiac physiology.

</details>


### [224] [I-SDPO: Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12957)
*Yubo Zhang,Xinhong Ma,Zezhong Tan,Ziqiang Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive per-instance self-distillation gating improves GRPO by using teacher influence only when group-relative rewards are uninformative, yielding strong gains on SciKnowEval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO struggles when all sampled responses are incorrect due to uninformative relative signals; privileged self-distillation can help but risks biasing updates if relied on throughout training.

Method: Introduce I-SDPO: for each input instance, decide whether to apply privileged self-distillation or keep GRPO based on whether the instance's rollout group is all-incorrect or any-success. The routing decision is shared across the group. The distillation weight is automatically reduced as success probability increases, removing the need for a hand-designed schedule. A local analysis characterizes alignment between teacher and reward directions and reveals a bias floor if the distillation term remains non-zero.

Result: I-SDPO achieves state-of-the-art performance on SciKnowEval, with best results across all four scientific domains. Average mean@16 accuracy improves from 56.67% (GRPO) to 70.31% with I-SDPO, with a maximum domain gain of 18.24 points.

Conclusion: Instance-level adaptive routing of self-distillation effectively mitigates teacher-induced bias and leverages imitation only when group-relative rewards are uninformative, yielding substantial performance gains.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within a rollout group, but receives no useful relative signal when every sampled response is incorrect. Privileged self-distillation can fill this gap with dense token supervision, yet applying it throughout training creates a different failure mode: the teacher is a biased, low-variance surrogate for the reward objective, so persistent imitation can oppose reward-improving updates after the policy becomes capable of producing successful trajectories. We introduce I-SDPO (Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization), which treats teacher reliance as capability-dependent. I-SDPO makes one routing decision per input instance and shares it across that instance's rollout group: all-incorrect groups use a privileged self-distillation objective, whereas any-success groups remain intact for GRPO. This design uses imitation only where group-relative rewards are uninformative. A local analysis characterizes when teacher and reward directions align and shows that a non-vanishing biased distillation weight induces an optimization bias floor. The routing rule automatically reduces the expected distillation rate as success probability rises, withdrawing teacher influence without a hand-designed schedule. On SciKnowEval, I-SDPO obtains the best result in all four scientific domains and improves average mean@16 accuracy from 56.67% with GRPO to 70.31%, with a maximum domain gain of 18.24 points.

</details>


### [225] [The Objective Is the Bottleneck: Latent World Models Encode What Their Planners Cannot Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12959)
*Joyjeet Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Long-horizon failures in latent world models are due to the planner’s objective, not the predictor. Replacing the objective (without retraining) dramatically improves long-horizon success, indicating planning failures stem from cost design rather than predictive quality; the predictor remains reasonably accurate while planners learn reachability rather than proximity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether predictor accuracy limits planning in latent world models at long horizons and reproduce LeWorldModel on TwoRoom to identify bottlenecks. Examine the relationship between latent prediction error, planning horizon, and planning objective.

Method: Reproduce LeWorldModel on TwoRoom. Use cross-entropy method (CEM) planning that minimizes squared latent distance. Assess predictor horizon (up to ~75 steps ahead) vs planner imagination (~25 steps). Evaluate information in frozen embeddings via a ridge probe for position (R^2 ~0.9922). Replace only the objective (no retraining, no GPU) to see impact on long-horizon success. Compare budgets and offsets (e.g., offset 100 vs 25). Also compare a head learned from frame-separation vs a position probe in terms of distance accuracy and planning success.

Result: The predictor’s long-horizon error is not the bottleneck; imagined horizon is limited to ~25 steps and is less critical than the objective. Cross-entropy planning with squared latent distance tracks true distance at r=0.426, saturates around ~80 units, and decreases beyond 120 units, implying suboptimal costs can drive detours. A ridge probe on frozen embeddings recovers position with high R^2 (0.9922). Replacing only the objective, with no retraining, raises success at offset 100 from 26.0% to 98.0% (matching 98.0% at offset 25), and achieves 92.0% with a third of the budget; planning stops depend on horizon. The best cost is not the most accurate: a head trained from frame separation may have worse distance accuracy (r=0.819) yet plans better and incurs higher cost to cross a dividing wall, indicating learned reachability rather than proximity.

Conclusion: The pathology lies in the planning method, not the predictor. The long-horizon failure arises from an objective that encourages proximity in latent space rather than reachability or feasibility. Modifying the objective alone—without retraining—substantially improves long-horizon planning, suggesting that better planning formulations (prioritizing reachability/feasibility) can unlock latent-world-model potential.

Abstract: Latent world models are judged by how well they predict, so when planning fails at long horizons the natural reading is that the predictor degrades. On a reproduction of LeWorldModel on TwoRoom we show the binding constraint is the planner's objective instead.
  The predictor is not the limit: its imagined state seventy-five environment steps ahead is still only 0.189 as wrong as assuming the world froze, while the planner never imagines beyond twenty-five. The objective is. Cross-entropy-method planning minimises squared latent distance, which tracks true distance at r = 0.426, saturates by about eighty arena units and decreases beyond a hundred and twenty, so moving away from the goal can lower the cost. The information is present throughout: a ridge probe recovers position from the frozen embedding at R^2 0.9922.
  The pathology is the method's, not one reimplementation's. It is present in the authors' released weights, and across four checkpoints long-horizon success rank-orders exactly with metric quality and inversely with prediction accuracy.
  Replacing only the objective, with nothing retrained and no GPU, lifts goals reached at offset 100 from 26.0% to 98.0%, equals the 98.0% at offset 25, and reaches 92.0% under a third of the budget: planning stops depending on the horizon. The best cost is not the most accurate. A head learned from frame separation alone predicts spatial distance worse than a position probe (r = 0.819 against 0.9897) yet plans better, charging 24% more to cross the environment's dividing wall where squared latent distance charges 4% less. It has learned reachability, not proximity.

</details>


### [226] [Understanding Backdoor Vulnerabilities in Vertical Federated Learning: The Gap Between Research and Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12962)
*Ziqi Zhao,Jialin Lu,Junjie Shan,Junyuan Zhang,Shuya Yang,Ka-Ho Chow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VFL backdoor vulnerabilities are fragile under realistic constraints; the paper redefines threat models and introduces BVBench for fair, practical evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the gap between theoretical backdoor attacks in vertical federated learning and practical feasibility; assess evaluation practices and promote robust defenses.

Method: Systematic, practice-oriented study that redefines threat models with realistic constraints, develops practical backdoor workflows, and introduces BVBench—a backdoor-centric benchmark with state-of-the-art baselines.

Result: Finds that prior backdoor claims overstate attack success under real conditions; demonstrates fragility of current understanding; BVBench enables fair, comprehensive evaluation and benchmarking.

Conclusion: Advocates for realistic vulnerability assessment in VFL, and positions BVBench as a foundation for advancing practical vulnerabilities research and defenses.

Abstract: Vertical Federated Learning (VFL) enables organizations holding complementary features of shared entities to collaborate and train models. In this setting, the initiator can withhold information about the learning task, while other contributors participate without exposing their local datasets, creating an asymmetric information structure aligned with growing privacy demands. However, this asymmetry is a double-edged sword. Among various threats, backdoor attacks are particularly concerning because VFL not only enables malicious contributors to poison the model during training, but also allows them to activate the backdoor at inference time to manipulate predictions. Although prior work has reported near-perfect attack success rates and proposed effective defenses, we find that most findings fail to hold under realistic conditions, exposing a fundamental gap between research and practice. In this paper, we present a systematic, practice-oriented study of backdoor vulnerabilities in VFL, revealing this gap in both methodological design and evaluation practices. We show that existing approaches overlook key practical constraints and therefore rely on unrealistic prior knowledge. Furthermore, these limitations have remained hidden due to poorly designed evaluation practices in the literature. To bridge this gap, we redefine threat models under realistic constraints, propose practical backdoor workflows, and introduce BVBench, a backdoor-centric benchmark that enables fair, practical, and comprehensive evaluation, preloaded with state-of-the-art baselines. BVBench provides strong evidence of the fragility of the current understanding of VFL backdoor risks and establishes a foundation for steering research toward uncovering practical vulnerabilities and developing more meaningful defenses.

</details>


### [227] [Comment on "Modeling rapid language learning by distilling Bayesian priors into artificial neural networks"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12974)
*Orr Well,Idan Tarshish,Nur Lan,Roni Katzir*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: M&G's claim that a Bayesian prior can be distilled into ANNs via MAML is contested. Under standard Bayesian interpretation it does not embed a prior, merely initializes weights; even with a broader interpretation, there are conceptual challenges; empirically, MAML-based models tend to overfit and generalize poorly compared to genuine Bayesian learners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critically assess whether meta-learning with MAML actually implements a Bayesian prior in neural networks; evaluate both standard and permissive interpretations, and compare empirical performance with a true Bayesian learner.

Method: Conceptual critique of the prior interpretation; analysis of MAML as weight initialization vs. prior; evaluation of a system-level Bayesian view; empirical comparison to Yang & Piantadosi (2023) and standard ANNs.

Result: Under the standard interpretation, the procedure does not instantiate a prior, only favorable initialization; a permissive system-level interpretation faces nontrivial challenges; MAML-based models overfit and generalize poorly relative to genuine Bayesian learners.

Conclusion: Claims of prior distillation via MAML are not warranted under standard Bayesian theory; any Bayesian-like behavior from meta-learned networks is at best incidental; more work is needed to realize true priors and robust Bayesian-like generalization in neural networks.

Abstract: McCoy & Griffiths (2025, henceforth M&G) suggest that a Bayesian prior can be distilled into Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) through Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML, Finn et al., 2017). They support this empirically by showing that meta-trained networks demonstrate formal language learning abilities comparable to Yang & Piantadosi (2023)'s Bayesian learner, significantly outperforming standard ANNs. We point out that under the standard interpretation of a prior, M&G's procedure does not actually instill one; it merely initializes network weights favorably, leaving the objective function unchanged. We then consider a more permissive interpretation, where the system as a whole can be seen as implementing a Bayesian learner even without an explicit prior in the objective. We show that this interpretation faces nontrivial challenges. Finally, we assess how well MAML approximates the empirical results of Bayesian learning, showing that unlike genuine Bayesian learners, M&G's model overfits and generalizes poorly to unseen data.

</details>


### [228] [Learning the Mathematical Property for Designing Low Mutual Coherence Binary Sensing Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12982)
*Rekha,Santosh Singh,S. K. Neogy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A neural network framework is proposed to construct a binary sensing matrix with low mutual coherence for compressive sensing without using datasets or application-specific data, reducing computational cost and storage by leveraging coherence as the loss function.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The design of sensing matrices with low coherence is essential for reliable sparse recovery in compressive sensing. Classical properties like RIP/NSP/Spark are NP-hard to enforce, making matrix construction computationally expensive. A dataset-free, property-driven approach aims to simplify and generalize matrix design while reducing resources.

Method: A neural network architecture generates a binary sensing matrix using a shared rule. The loss function is defined by a mathematical property—mutual coherence—rather than empirical data. The approach avoids large-scale training data and emphasizes simplicity, generality, and reduced storage.

Result: The framework yields a binary sensing matrix with low mutual coherence, with reported advantages including drastic computational cost reduction, robustness, generality, and reduced storage requirements.

Conclusion: The work presents a novel, low-resource neural network-based method for designing sensing matrices in compressive sensing, leveraging mutual coherence as a principled loss to enable effective sparse recovery without datasets.

Abstract: In this research work, we are constructing the sensing matrix, which is essential for the success of the compressive sensing technique. We have chosen a learning-based technique for the construction of the sensing matrix. The novelty and uniqueness of the proposed technique is that it does not use any data set and also does not use a specific application. It uses the mathematical property/constraint for the construction of the sensing matrix for the perfect recovery of the signal. The perfect recovery of signals is an old and still very challenging problem in real-world applications. In late 2000, compressive sensing became a popular mathematical tool for the perfect recovery of sparse signals. The core of the compressive technique is the construction of the sensing matrix, which satisfies certain special properties such as restricted isometry property (RIP), null space property (NSP), and spark property (SP). All these properties are NP-hard problems and hence computationally challenging to solve. For all practical purposes, the construction of the sensing matrix needs to achieve low mutual coherence to achieve the perfect recovery of the signals. We have used a neural network for the construction of the sensing matrix, and this framework constructs a binary sensing matrix with low mutual coherence. The entries in the matrix are generated through a shared underlying rule. The proposed architecture is simple and does not use large-scale training data sets. Such uniqueness and novelty bring a drastic reduction in computational cost, and also, for the first time in literature, the use of a mathematical property for defining the loss function. In this proposed research work, the mutual coherence property has been used in the neural network framework. Such a neural network framework brings generality, robustness, and reduces storage requirements.

</details>


### [229] [Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection for Scalable TabPFN Inference on Large-Scale Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12989)
*Mahboobe Jadid,Melika Rezaye Garkani,Ali Mousavi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BAPS constructs a compact prototype-based context to enable scalable TabPFN inference without retraining, achieving strong performance and calibration with ~1953x context compression using 512 prototypes on million-row datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable pretrained tabular foundation models to process million-scale datasets by reducing the inference context while preserving predictive quality and calibration.

Method: Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS) jointly preserves representative structure, informative decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity to select a small set of prototypes (512) used as the inference context for TabPFN, with no model modification or retraining and CPU-only operation.

Result: On million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets, 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, achieving approximately 1,953-fold context compression; experiments run on an Intel Core i7 CPU with 16 GB RAM and no GPU acceleration.

Conclusion: Context construction via BAPS offers a practical mechanism to scale pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets without retraining, maintaining performance and calibration while greatly reducing context size.

Abstract: Pretrained tabular foundation models have demonstrated strong predictive capability; however, their application to large-scale datasets remains constrained by the limited inference context. This paper introduces Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS), a framework for constructing compact, information-preserving contexts for scalable TabPFN inference. Without modifying or retraining the pretrained model, BAPS jointly preserves representative structure, informative decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity. Experiments on the million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets show that 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, corresponding to an approximately 1,953-fold context compression. All experiments were conducted on an Intel Core i7 CPU with 16 GB RAM and no GPU acceleration. These findings establish effective context construction as a practical mechanism for extending pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets.

</details>


### [230] [Incremental Evaluation and Training in Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13023)
*Jakub Peleška,Gustav Šír*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Incremental, multi-episode evaluation for Relational Deep Learning on temporal graphs; introduces near-future temporal metric and transfer learning regimes that outperform from-scratch baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, single-episode RDL benchmarks fail to capture data evolution and temporal robustness; many tasks exhibit concept drift as data accumulates.

Method: Proposes an incremental training/evaluation paradigm across multiple episodes; analyzes data evolution and training dynamics on large-scale datasets; introduces several incremental fine-tuning regimes; demonstrates transfer learning viability in RDL; defines a temporal metric prioritizing near-future accuracy.

Result: Temporal concept drift is prevalent across most predictive tasks; incrementally fine-tuned models consistently surpass standard from-scratch baselines; transfer learning is feasible and effective in RDL; temporal evaluation highlights improved near-future performance.

Conclusion: An incrementally trained RDL framework with a dedicated temporal metric improves robustness and practicality in evolving data settings, enabling better performance with less training cost than scratch training.

Abstract: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) models multi-tabular databases as temporal heterogeneous graphs to enable end-to-end representation learning. However, prevailing RDL evaluation practices rely on static, single-episode dataset snapshots, overlooking the continuous, time-evolving nature of real-world databases. Consequently, current RDL benchmarks fail to capture how model performance changes as new data accumulates over time. To address this limitation, we introduce an incremental, multi-episode evaluation and training paradigm to assess and improve the temporal robustness and adaptability of state-of-the-art RDL models. Using established large-scale datasets, we examine data evolution and model training dynamics, demonstrating that temporal concept drifts occur in the majority of predictive tasks. We present multiple incremental training regimes for fine-tuning the models and demonstrate that transfer learning is both feasible and highly effective in the RDL setting. Alongside a new temporal evaluation metric that prioritizes near-future accuracy, we show that our incrementally fine-tuned models consistently outperform the standard, expensive, from-scratch trained baselines.

</details>


### [231] [On the global feature importance for interpretable and trustworthy heat demand forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13039)
*Milan Zdravković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework combining intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting with post-hoc global XAI methods (PDP, ALE, SHAP) to evaluate global feature importance in heat-demand forecasting for district heating, aiming to improve interpretability and trust while avoiding permutation-based biases; results discuss complementarities in domain-specific interpretations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance interpretability, trust, and regulatory/compliance alignment in ML-based heat-demand forecasting for district heating, addressing communal standards, customer satisfaction, and liability concerns.

Method: Four approaches are used: (i) intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting, and (ii) post-hoc methods Partial Dependence Plots (PDP), Accumulated Local Effects (ALE), and SHAP. The framework explicitly avoids feature permutation/perturbation to prevent bias from unrealistic data combinations and analyzes complementarities among methods within the district heating context.

Result: Results are discussed with an emphasis on complementarities among methods and context-specific interpretations relevant to district heating processes.

Conclusion: The proposed ante-hoc XAI methodology provides a robust framework for global feature importance in ML-based heat-demand forecasting, enhancing interpretability and trust. Further empirical validation and careful treatment of feature correlations and model biases are recommended to solidify practical applicability.

Abstract: The paper introduces the ante-hoc Explainable AI methodology to assess the global feature importance of the Machine Learning models used for heat demand forecasting in intelligent control of District Heating Systems, with motivation to facilitate their interpretability and trustworthiness, hence addressing the challenges related to adherence to communal standards, customer satisfaction and liability risks. Methodology includes use of four different approaches, namely intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting method and selected post-hoc methods, namely Partial Dependence, Accumulated Local Effects and SHAP. None of the selected methods assume feature permutation or perturbations which can introduce bias due to introduction of random unrealistic values of data instances. Discussion of results is provided, including the assessment of complementarities where applicable, with specific interpretations in context of the district heating processes.

</details>


### [232] [Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13040)
*Guibin Zhang,Jiayang Lyu,Ran Sun,Xinlei Yu,Haoyu Zhao,Qibing Ren,Shuicheng Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LOPD enables learning privileged teacher context end-to-end by composing relevant experiences into latent tokens that condition a self-teacher, while the student learns from dense, token-level supervision on its own trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address reliance on hand-crafted privileged artifacts in on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) and improve end-to-end learnability and scalability for continual agent self-improvement.

Method: Retrieve and assemble relevant experiences into continuous latent tokens that condition a self-teacher; the student generates trajectories from task history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix; introduce a privileged-margin objective to stabilize latent-context learning.

Result: LOPD achieves strong performance, surpassing RLVR and OPSD variants (OPSD, SDPO, Skill-SD) on agentic tool use and code generation; exhibits higher learning efficiency with less than 30% rollout budget of comparators; ablations indicate learnable privileged context is essential for gains.

Conclusion: Learning the privileged context end-to-end enables scalable, self-directed advancement of agents, marking a step toward more autonomous continual self-improvement.

Abstract: Enabling agents to learn from experience and internalize it into their policy has become a central problem in self-evolving AI. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers an effective pathway by using a privileged self-teacher to provide dense supervision on the student's own trajectories; however, existing methods still rely heavily on designer-specified privileged artifacts (e.g., answers, feedback, skills, or trajectories), limiting the end-to-end learnability and scalability required for continual self-improvement. In this work, we introduce Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation (LOPD), which, rather than proposing another hand-crafted OPSD variant with a newly prescribed form of privileged context, makes the teacher's privileged context itself learnable end-to-end from experience. Technically, LOPD retrieves relevant experiences and composes them into continuous latent tokens that condition a self-teacher, while the student generates trajectories from the task and interaction history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix. We further introduce a privileged-margin objective to stabilize and regulate the learning of latent context. Empirically, LOPD demonstrates (I) strong performance, outperforming RLVR and representative OPSD methods including OPSD, SDPO, and Skill-SD across both agentic tool use and code generation; and (II) high learning efficiency, surpassing GRPO and Skill-SD with less than 30% of their rollout budget. Ablation studies further provide direct evidence that making privileged context learnable is necessary for realizing these gains. Together, these results position LOPD as a step toward a more scalable and self-directed paradigm for agent evolution.

</details>


### [233] [A Multispectral Framework for the Detection of Calcium Carbide-Induced Ripening and Shelf-Life Estimation in Climacteric Fruits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13073)
*Gurbhit Chaurakoti,Harshit Kumar,Hani Kumar,Anurag Singh,Ram Asrey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A non-invasive, spectroscopic framework using a 18-wavelength visible-NIR sensor (AS7265x) plus PCA and XGBoost to classify calcium carbide-ripened fruits versus naturally/ethephon-ripened fruits, estimate ripening progression, and predict remaining shelf life in mango and banana.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Calcium carbide ripening poses health risks due to arsenic/phosphorus residues. There is a need for a rapid, non-destructive method to distinguish carbide-ripened produce from safe ripening methods and to quantify ripening status and shelf life.

Method: Collect spectral profiles of mango and banana at 18 wavelengths (410–940 nm) with the AS7265x spectral triad sensor. Extract features via inter-method spectral variance, wavelength-intensity ratios, and environmental data (temperature, humidity). Reduce dimensionality with PCA (retaining >90% variance in the first 5–7 components). Train three independent XGBoost models for (i) ripening-method classification, (ii) remaining shelf-life estimation, and (iii) ripening progression (percentage).

Result: For mango: classification accuracy 95%, carbide-class recall 0.67. For banana: accuracy 81%, carbide-class recall 0.74. PCA retained >90% variance in 5–7 components; models demonstrate effective non-invasive discrimination and ripening metrics.

Conclusion: The proposed non-invasive spectroscopic approach effectively differentiates carbide-ripened from naturally/ethephon-ripened fruits and provides quantitative estimates of ripening progression and shelf life, though carbide-specific recall is moderate and may benefit from sensor/data enhancements.

Abstract: Significant health risks are associated with the illegal, yet commonly practiced use of industrial-grade Calcium Carbide (CaC2) for ripening climacteric fruits like mango and banana, which leaves behind trace residues of arsenic and phosphorus. To address this, the proposed study explores a novel, non-invasive multispectral framework for distinguishing safely ripened fruits (naturally ripened and ethephon-induced) from calcium carbide-ripened samples, while also estimating their ripening progression (in percentage) and remaining shelf life (in days). The spectral profiles of mango (Mangifera indica) and banana (Musa acuminata) at 18 discrete wavelengths in the visible-near infrared (NIR) range (410 nm - 940 nm) are studied using the AS7265x spectral triad sensor. CaC2-treated samples exhibit sharper spectral intensity drops in the visible region, consistent with accelerated chlorophyll degradation and carotenoid development. To characterize these physiological changes, the feature engineering strategy integrates inter-method spectral variance, intensity ratios at distinct wavelengths, and environmental parameters including temperature and humidity. Dimensionality reduction using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retains >90% of spectral variance within the first 5-7 components. The resulting feature set is used to train three independent eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost)-based learning algorithms for ripening method classification, along with quantitative estimation of remaining shelf life and ripening progression. A classification accuracy of 95% along with carbide class recall of 0.67 is observed for mango samples, while the model achieves an accuracy of 81% and carbide class recall of 0.74 for banana. This instrumentation and data-driven approach demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed non-invasive framework.

</details>


### [234] [Learning Discrete Decisions for MIPs with Constraint-Aware Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13079)
*Vincenzo Di Vito,Mehdi Taghizadeh,Deepjyoti Deka,Kaarthik Sundar,Ferdinando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based, graph-conditioned generative model (CGD) learning the discrete decisions of MINLPs, with a training-free feasibility projection in the reverse diffusion path; after discrete decisions are generated, the continuous subproblem is solved efficiently, yielding large speedups and better feasibility across problems like ACOPF-based transmission switching and discrete portfolio optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed-integer optimization (MINLP) is computationally hard due to the need to jointly optimize discrete and continuous variables under complex combinatorial constraints. A scalable, problem-agnostic approach that can produce feasible, high-quality solutions quickly is highly desirable.

Method: A graph-based generative diffusion model learns the discrete component of MINLPs. A training-free feasibility projection operator is embedded in the reverse diffusion process to steer samples toward the feasible set during generation. Once discrete decisions are produced, the remaining continuous problem is solved with conventional optimization methods. The framework CGD is designed to be problem-agnostic, requiring only a suitable projection operator for the target problem.

Result: CGD achieves substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality compared with learning-based baselines and delivers speedups up to 425× over state-of-the-art MINLP solvers. Evaluations include applications to optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization.

Conclusion: CGD provides a flexible, problem-agnostic framework for solving MINLPs by decoupling discrete and continuous components and enforcing feasibility during diffusion-based generation via a projection operator. Its success relies on effective discrete-learning and suitable feasibility projections; it offers significant speedups and improved feasibility, with broad applicability to MINLPs through appropriate projections.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel learning-based approach to approximately solve instances of mixed-integer optimization problems. These problems are computationally challenging, as they require jointly determining discrete and continuous decisions while satisfying complex combinatorial constraints. The proposed method relies on a graph-based generative diffusion model that learns the discrete component of mixed-integer optimization problems while integrating a training-free feasibility projection operator directly into the reverse diffusion process to steer intermediate samples toward the feasible set throughout generation. Once the discrete decisions are generated, the remaining optimization reduces to a continuous problem that can be solved efficiently (relative to the original problem) using existing numerical methods. The resulting framework named Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD), is problem-agnostic and can accommodate a broad class of mixed-integer optimization problems through suitable projection operators. We evaluate CGD on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization, demonstrating substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality over learning-based baselines while achieving speedups of up to $425\times$ over state-of-the-art numerical solvers for MINLPs.

</details>


### [235] [Sampling Luck Masquerades as Allocation Gain: Auditing Test-Time Budget Allocation for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13087)
*Jinhyung Bae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-uniform sampling budget allocation in neural combinatorial optimization can inflate in-sample gains but yields no robust out-of-sample benefit; after correcting for measurement bias, real gains emerge under distribution shift (≈11-12%), with additional exploratory approaches showing smaller gains; a correction procedure, preregistration, and data/code release are provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether reallocating a fixed total sampling budget across instances improves solver performance and determine whether observed gains are real or artifacts of measurement bias; audit the measurement process across in-distribution and shifted distributions.

Method: Evaluate three solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100 with both in-sample oracle allocation and out-of-sample evaluation; calibrate against an instance-wise null to quantify phantom gains; conduct pre-registered confirmatory experiments under a distribution-shift workload (mixture of uniform and clustered instances) using held-out sample statistics for allocation; compare to a negative control; test an exploratory policy with a 20-sample probe; propose a correction procedure and a reporting checklist; release data, code, and preregistration.

Result: In-distribution, the oracle-based gain is 2.2–2.6% (in-sample) but the same gain is indistinguishable from zero out-of-sample; standard in-sample reporting would support ~2% gain erroneously. Under distribution shift, held-out-sample-statistics allocation improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM) and 12.0% (SymNCO) with equal budget; negative control (POMO) shows no improvement (-0.3%). The improvement exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points. An exploratory 20-sample-probe policy yields 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO).

Conclusion: Most observed in-sample gains are phantom artifacts; a correction yields robust gains under distribution shift; the authors provide a correction procedure, a preregistration protocol, and release the data/code to enable auditing and replication.

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) solvers report the best of many sampled solutions per instance, and the sample count is, by convention, identical for every instance. Whether a non-uniform allocation of a fixed total budget would buy anything has not been measured. We measure it, and we audit the measurement itself.
  First, on in-distribution workloads the allocation headroom is not detectable. Across three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100, an oracle allocation computed and evaluated on the same stored samples reports a 2.2-2.6% gain with intervals excluding zero; measured out of sample the same gain is indistinguishable from zero (0.457, 0.015, -0.512 percent). Following the customary in-sample procedure, all three solvers would have supported a published 2%-level gain that does not exist. We calibrate this bias against an instance-wise null in which the true gain is zero by construction; over the ranges we test it does not shrink with more samples or more instances.
  Second, the same correction that removes the phantom gains preserves a real one. Under distribution shift (a workload mixing uniform and clustered instances), a pre-registered confirmatory experiment finds that allocation guided by held-out sample statistics improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM, primary endpoint; 95% CI [7.4, 19.7]) and 12.0% (SymNCO, replication) at equal evaluation budget, with the signal-acquisition cost not charged; a pre-registered negative control (POMO, an order of magnitude more robust to shift) shows -0.3% [-0.7, 0.24]. The gain exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points [1.9, 7.7]. An exploratory policy charging a 20-sample probe against the same budget retains 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO).
  We give a correction procedure and a reporting checklist, and release all data, code, and the pre-registration record.

</details>


### [236] [FlowLOB: Efficient and Controllable Limit Order Book Generation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13096)
*Zhuohan Wang,Andreea Bacalum,Ollie Olby,Carmine Ventre,Namid Stillman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowLOB is a conditional flow-matching generator for LOB trajectories trained on HKEX data that generalizes to unseen instruments; it achieves higher realism at lower computational cost (best with 10 ODE steps) and supports controllable scenario generation, outperforming baselines in distributional metrics at multiple sampling frequencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need efficient, realistic LOB simulators that generalize across instruments and enable controllable scenario generation; existing agent-based and deep generative models trade off realism, speed, or generalization.

Method: Train conditional flow-matching model (FlowLOB) on HKEX symbols at 0.1s, 1s, 10s sampling frequencies, in tick-relative representation; compare with diffusion models trained identically; sample with fixed-step ODE solvers; assess realism via distributional metrics; test zero-shot transfer to unseen instrument; perform ablations on architecture and learning rate; evaluate counterfactual controllability via distributional tests that check movement toward real tails.

Result: FlowLOB achieves best quality with only 10 ODE steps; diffusion requires more steps to reach similar fidelity. At efficient point FlowLOB improves realism over two learned and two agent-based baselines across most distributional metrics at 0.1s and 1s frequencies; FlowLOB shows zero-shot transfer to unseen instruments and satisfies controllability criterion in most tested settings; ablation studies show robustness to architecture and learning rate choices.

Conclusion: FlowLOB offers an efficient, transferable LOB trajectory generator with controllable scenario generation, outperforming baselines on realism and generalization; flow-matching can be more sample-efficient than diffusion for this task.

Abstract: Limit order book (LOB) simulators are most useful to practitioners when they combine realistic market dynamics, computationally efficient sampling, controllable scenario generation, and the ability to generalize beyond the instruments seen during training---properties that existing agent-based and deep generative simulators provide only partially. We present \textbf{FlowLOB}, a conditional \textbf{flow}-matching generator of \textbf{LOB} trajectories, trained on multiple Hong Kong Exchange (HKEX) symbols at three sampling frequencies ($0.1$s, $1$s, $10$s) in tick-relative representation that transfers to unseen instruments. Because flow and diffusion models admit a common formulation, we train both with identical data, architecture, and budget, and sample both through the same fixed-step ODE solvers, yielding a controlled comparison of sampling efficiency and fidelity. Flow matching attains its best quality with only $10$ ODE-solver steps, whereas diffusion needs many more function evaluations to approach the same fidelity. At this efficient operating point, FlowLOB improves realism over baselines, two learned and two agent-based models, in most distributional metrics at the two finer sampling frequencies. We evaluate counterfactual controllability with a distributional test that asks whether changing a scenario condition moves the generated statistic toward the corresponding real tail regime; FlowLOB satisfies this criterion in most tested settings. Both realism and control effects transfer zero-shot on a held-out symbol. We additionally conduct ablation studies on the network architecture and the learning rate.

</details>


### [237] [Branch and Bound for Relational Verification of Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13118)
*Kota Fukuda,Zhenya Zhang,Guanqin Zhang,Jianjun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A branch-and-bound verification framework (SaBRe) for neural networks against relational specifications, using relational neuron splitting guided by a dual-based selection strategy to maximize refinement; evaluated on 817 problems across multiple datasets, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational specifications (e.g., global robustness) require reasoning about relationships between multiple inferences, which existing convex/over-approximation methods struggle with due to incompleteness and false alarms. A refinement loop can improve verification results for safety-critical CPS with neural components.

Method: A branch-and-bound framework that splits relational neurons (not individual neurons). A relational neuron selection strategy driven by the dual formulation of the verification problem identifies the most impactful split to tighten abstractions and improve verification efficiency.

Result: SaBRe solves more instances and is more efficient than baselines across 817 verification problems on ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR, and GTSRB datasets.

Conclusion: Relational neuron-focused splitting guided by a dual-based selection strategy effectively refines abstractions, improving the performance of BaB-based verification for relational properties in neural networks.

Abstract: Verification of neural networks against relational specifications, such as global robustness, is crucial for safety-critical applications of cyber-physical systems (CPS), given their increasing adoption of AI components. Compared to simple trace properties (e.g., local robustness), verifying relational specifications requires reasoning about the relationship between multiple network inferences, which brings significant technical challenges. Existing research has explored abstraction techniques based on sound and convex over-approximation of neural network outputs; however, since these approaches are inherently incomplete and may raise false alarms, they further underscore the need of effective abstraction refinement.
  In this paper, we propose a branch-and-bound (BaB) framework to mitigate the issue, which iteratively splits the problem until all sub-problems are verified. Specifically, our BaB framework features splitting of relational neurons rather than individual neurons as prior works do, and as the core of our technique, we devise a relational neuron selection strategy based on the dual formulation of the verification problem, which allows us to efficiently select the (most likely) optimal relational neuron that maximizes the refinement brought by problem splitting. We evaluate SaBRe on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR and GTSRB. The results show that SaBRe outperforms different baseline approaches, in terms of the number of solved instances and verification efficiency, which demonstrates the effectiveness of our proposed techniques.

</details>


### [238] [ProME: Prototype-Margin Environments with Repair-Aware Selection for Group-Robust Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13190)
*Qianqian Wang,Yunshan Li,Dawei Huang,Wenwu Gong,Lili Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProME aligns environment discovery with the deployed predictor for group robustness without training-group labels, using prototype-margin–based endogenous environments and repair-aware selection; achieves state-of-the-art worst-group accuracy under same group-label access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address group-robust learning when training-group labels are unavailable and ensure alignment between environment inference and the deployed predictor; mitigate reliance on separate reference models.

Method: Construct environments by splitting prototype margins at the median along the training trajectory to create approximately balanced environments; fit a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank predictors by worst-group validation accuracy; provide a theoretical bound on worst-risk across inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, with transfer to oracle groups under an alignment condition.

Result: Prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples; repair-based classifier evaluation reshapes candidate ranking; ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among methods with the same group-label access in extensive experiments.

Conclusion: ProME offers an end-to-end, alignment-friendly approach to group robustness without training-group labels, backed by a theoretical bound and strong empirical performance; it highlights the importance of jointly considering environment construction and predictor repair.

Abstract: Group-robust learning is crucial for maintaining accuracy on rare subpopulations when training-group labels are unavailable. However, existing methods often infer environments from a separate reference model and select representations before fitting the classifier used at deployment, leaving both decisions misaligned with the deployed predictor. In this work, we formulate group robustness without training-group labels as the endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) problem, and propose ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) to align both decisions with the deployed predictor. ProME splits prototype margins at their median to construct approximately balanced environments along the training trajectory, and fits a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank the resulting predictors by validation worst-group accuracy. We theoretically bound the worst risk across the inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, showing that this bound transfers to the oracle groups under an explicit alignment condition. Extensive experiments show that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples, classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation, and ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among the compared methods with the same group-label access.

</details>


### [239] [Beyond Simulated Benchmarks: Evaluating Motion Representations for Fall Detection Under Real-World Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13197)
*Timilehin B. Aderinola,Ilaria D'Ascanio,Luca Palmerini,Lorenzo Chiari,Jochen Klenk,Clemens Becker,Brian Caulfield,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Representation choice crucial for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity; highly parameterised models excel on simulated data but falter under scarcity and domain shift, while interval-based and symbolics with impact descriptors offer robustness and maintain detection under extreme scarcity, albeit with trade-offs in precision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world falls are rare, making labelled data scarce; simulated datasets may not generalize; there is a need to assess how different representations handle data scarcity and domain shift.

Method: Compare four motion representations (interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation-model) using accelerometer data on FallAllD (simulated) and FARSEEING (real-world). Evaluate via cross-validation, controlled data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer to assess robustness under deployment-like conditions.

Result: Kernel and foundation models attain strongest performance on simulated data but degrade markedly with limited data and domain shift. Interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance. A lightweight symbolic representation augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors exhibits the least degradation under domain shift and retains detection under extreme scarcity, though with reduced precision.

Conclusion: Evaluating beyond simulated benchmarks is essential; representation choice critically affects deployability of fall detection systems in scarce real-world data settings; combining symbolic representations with impact descriptors offers robust performance under realistic deployment constraints.

Abstract: Falls are a major health concern for older adults, and wearable sensors have been widely explored for detecting falls and enabling timely intervention. However, real-world falls are extremely rare: collecting 100 of them requires an estimated 100,000 days of monitoring, resulting in severely limited labelled data for training machine learning models. Consequently, many approaches rely on simulated datasets, often reporting high laboratory performance but limited real-world generalisation. We present a systematic evaluation of motion representations for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity. Using accelerometer signals, we compare interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation model representations. As an interpretable baseline, we additionally investigate a lightweight symbolic representation that converts short motion segments into symbolic sentences augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors. Experiments use FallAllD, a simulated falls dataset, and FARSEEING, a clinically verified real-world falls dataset. Through cross-validation, controlled data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer, we examine how representation choices affect robustness under realistic deployment. Our results reveal that highly parameterised kernel and foundation models excel on simulated data but degrade severely under both data scarcity and domain shift. Although the interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance, augmenting a symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors yields the smallest degradation under domain shift and retains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity, albeit at lower precision. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating beyond simulated benchmarks and show that representation choice is critical for deployable fall detection given the scarcity of real-world data.

</details>


### [240] [TANGCO: Learning Topology-Aware Capacity Allocation for Overload-driven Cascading Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13212)
*Orkun Irsoy,Leman Akoglu,Osman Yagan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Topology-aware neural policy (TANGCO) allocates fixed capacity budgets to resist cascading failures in networked loads with local redistribution; trained with cascade simulations using policy-gradient and graph neural networks; outperforms hand-designed heuristics on synthetic and real networks, with strong transferability and scalable training; provides insights and a new closed-form heuristic; pre-trained variant reduces deployment cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cascading failures arise when node loads exceed capacity and redistribute locally, risking system-wide cascades. The capacity allocation problem is hard due to non-differentiable, piecewise outcomes and unknown optimal allocations.

Method: Introduce TANGCO, a graph neural network policy that outputs node capacities given the network topology and load; train with a cascade simulator using policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor; evaluate on five synthetic graph families and five real networks (power, road, air, Internet); test transferability across unseen graphs and topologies; explore a pre-trained variant TANGCO^pre; compare against four hand-designed heuristics; analyze the learned allocations to derive improved closed-form heuristics.

Result: The learned policy consistently beats the best of four heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains from 1.6% to 246%. Policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies; TANGCO^pre matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size. Free-vector variants (no GNN) perform close to heuristics, highlighting the value of graph representations. The study also derives insights that motivate a stronger closed-form heuristic and identifies regimes where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

Conclusion: TANGCO delivers scalable, transferable, topology-aware capacity allocation that outperforms heuristics, with practical deployment advantages via pre-training. Graph representations contribute beyond numerical search, enabling both performance gains and actionable insights for improved heuristics.

Abstract: Networked systems, from power grids to traffic networks and cloud clusters, carry loads across nodes with limited capacity. A node whose load exceeds its capacity fails and sheds its load onto its neighbors, which can trigger a system-wide cascade. We study how to allocate a fixed capacity budget across nodes to resist these cascades under local load redistribution. The problem is difficult because no optimal allocation is known, and the fail-or-survive objective is non-differentiable and piecewise constant, so exact and gradient-based optimization methods do not directly apply. We introduce TANGCO (Topology-Aware Neural Graph-Guided Capacity Optimization), which uses a graph neural network policy trained through the cascade simulator with policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor. We evaluate TANGCO on five synthetic graph families and five real networks spanning power, road, air, and Internet topologies. The learned policy improves on the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and in 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains ranging from 1.6% to 246%. The learned policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies, and TANGCO$^{pre}$, pre-trained on synthetic graphs, matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size, and TANGCO$^{pre}$ allocates on a new network with no per-target training, matching the deployment cost of a hand-designed heuristic. Free-vector variants without the GNN, stay close to the heuristics, so the graph representation carries the gain beyond numerical search. Finally, analysis of the learned allocations identifies when local risk is sufficient, leads to an improved closed-form heuristic, and reveals the regimes where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

</details>


### [241] [History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13215)
*Tianshuo Zhang,Xianglei Xing,Wenzhe Zhai,Jia Gao,He Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiLNN learns from history to reconstruct unobserved velocity and adapt Lagrangian parameters from position histories, enabling accurate long-horizon forecasts from partial observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting long-horizon dynamics from position-only data is challenging due to hidden velocities and changing system parameters; physics-guided models like Lagrangian Neural Networks require full state inputs and struggle with parameter variability.

Method: A recurrent encoder processes history of position sequences to produce a latent context that (i) reconstructs the initial velocity and (ii) adaptively modulates the mass matrix M(q), potential energy V(q), and damping terms in a structured Lagrangian system. Training uses a differentiable RK4 rollout with end-to-end optimization over multi-step trajectories and energy-consistency regularization.

Result: HiLNN achieves superior long-term prediction accuracy and tighter energy profiles compared with state-of-the-art baselines across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems; code is publicly available.

Conclusion: Leveraging history to infer hidden velocities and parameter variation enables robust, physically plausible long-horizon forecasting from partial observations, broadening the applicability of Lagrangian-based models.

Abstract: Forecasting the long-horizon evolution of mechanical systems from position-only observations is a pivotal yet difficult task, as hidden velocities and trajectory-specific physical properties must be inferred simultaneously. Although physics-guided neural networks like Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) guarantee physical plausibility, they generally require complete state inputs and lack adaptability to changing system parameters. To break these limitations, we introduce History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks (HiLNN). Grounded in the insight that temporal position sequences implicitly encode underlying dynamics, HiLNN employs a recurrent encoder to extract a latent context from history. This context not only reconstructs the unobserved initial velocity but also adaptively modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. By leveraging a differentiable RK4 rollout scheme, the entire pipeline is optimized end-to-end under multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization. Empirical evaluations across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems show that HiLNN delivers superior long-term prediction accuracy and maintains precise energy profiles compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/yingtian22/History-informed-LNN.

</details>


### [242] [Knowledge-guided Pattern Discovery via Coupled Tensor Factorizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13234)
*Gaute Johannessen,Geert Roelof van der Ploeg,Evrim Acar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Knowledge-guided, coupled tensor factorization of real and simulated data improves pattern discovery in multiway biological data and detects data-model mismatches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many biomedical datasets are multiway (e.g., subjects × metabolites × time). Traditional tensor factorizations are data-driven and may miss informative priors. Integrating computational-model simulations provides prior information to guide discovery and diagnose discrepancies.

Method: Coupled tensor factorization with linear coupling between a real-data tensor and a simulated-data tensor generated by a computational model, enabling joint analysis and shared latent structure.

Result: Applied to metabolomics data, guiding analysis with simulated data enhances pattern discovery in noisy measurements and reveals potential mismatches between data and the computational model.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided, linear-coupled tensor factorization effectively integrates data and models to improve interpretability, pattern discovery, and model validation in complex multiway systems.

Abstract: In order to understand complex systems such as the human metabolome or human brain, different sensing technologies are used, generating complex data. These datasets are often multiway, i.e., with more than two axes of variation such as a subjects by metabolites by time array. While tensor factorizations have successfully revealed interpretable patterns from such complex data, they have so far been mainly data-driven. On the other hand, there is more to data -- there are computational models (of these systems), which are rich sources of prior information. In this paper, we introduce a knowledge-guided approach that brings together data and computational models by jointly analyzing real data and simulated data (generated using a computational model) using coupled tensor factorizations with linear coupling. Our experiments on real metabolomics measurements demonstrate that guiding the analysis of such noisy data with simulated data improves the pattern discovery performance while also revealing potential discrepancies between data and computational models.

</details>


### [243] [Novel Knowledge-Guided Generative Methods for Synthetic Transcriptomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13256)
*Francesca Pia Panaccione,Sofia Mongardi,Marco Masseroli,Pietro Pinoli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MK-TGAN, a multi-kernel GNN-based GAN leveraging gene knowledge graphs, delivers the most realistic and biologically plausible synthetic transcriptomic data among three GAN variants; integrating prior biological knowledge via graph neural networks improves data quality and downstream utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical datasets are often limited by imbalances, biases, and access constraints due to ethical/legal issues. Synthetic data generation aims to augment datasets while preserving usefulness for downstream tasks; incorporating prior biological knowledge through gene graphs can steer generation toward realistic gene expression patterns.

Method: Comparative analysis of three GAN variants for transcriptomic data; development of MK-TGAN, a multi-kernel Graph Neural Network-based GAN that uses prior knowledge graphs; evaluation of realism and utility on downstream tasks.

Result: Incorporating prior knowledge via graphs improves performance; MK-TGAN achieves superior realism and biological plausibility across metrics; MK-TGAN outperforms other variants in both realism and utility.

Conclusion: Knowledge-informed generative modeling with graph neural networks is advantageous for synthetic transcriptomic data; MK-TGAN is a promising approach for generating high-quality, biologically plausible data that preserve downstream task performance.

Abstract: As biomedical research increasingly relies on data-intensive tools, the quality and utility of datasets are critical. Challenges such as imbalances, biases, and ethical or legal constraints often limit access to high-quality data. Synthetic data generation can help overcome these limitations.
