Diffusion-based drafters further reduce proposal latency by predicting an entire token block in parallel, but their position-wise distributions are marginal rather than conditioned on tokens selected along each draft path. Existing recurrent correction incorporates causal information along a single draft chain, whereas diffusion-based tree construction broadens candidate coverage without carrying this correction along individual branches. We introduce DARTree, a training-free speculative decoding method that extends a pretrained AR correction head from chains to trees. DARTree first constructs a fixed-width candidate tree by expanding and scoring all nodes at each depth in a single batch, and then only applies best-first pruning to select the verification tree, decoupling AR-head inference from sequential heap operations. Across seven math, code, and chat benchmarks, DARTree achieves the highest average acceptance length and speedup in all four model--temperature configurations, accepting up to 12.97 tokens per verification round, 98.6\% more than DFlash and 27.9\% more than Domino in the same setting, and reaching up to 9.73$\times$ lossless speedup over locally measured autoregressive decoding.

</details>


### [267] [Exponential Convex Calibration Dimension for the Multi-Label Jaccard Measure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13549)
*Mingyuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Exposes that exact calibration of Jaccard-based surrogates for multi-label tasks demands exponential prediction coordinates; provides polynomial approximations via MinHash/Möbius inversion with explicit regret guarantees, linking F1-to-Jaccard and MinHash surrogates to dimension and regret trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand the computational and statistical limits of calibrating convex surrogates for the per-instance Jaccard loss in multi-label and binary segmentation settings, and propose practical surrogate constructions with provable regret bounds.

Method: Combine a finite MinHash Gram representation with Boolean Möbius inversion to analyze the Jaccard loss matrix; derive nonsingularity and affine dimension results; establish Bayes-optimal distributions to prove lower bounds on dimension (CCdim) for exact calibration; propose two polynomial-dimensional surrogate constructions (F1-to-Jaccard transfer and MinHash square-loss surrogate) with explicit regret transfer guarantees.

Result: - The Jac loss matrix is nonsingular with 2^s outcomes; loss columns lie in an affine subspace of dimension 2^s-1. 2^{s-1} ≤ CCdim(L^{Jac}) ≤ 2^s-1. Exponential dimension is necessary for exact convex calibration. Two polynomial-dimension surrogates with regret guarantees: (i) an F1-to-Jaccard transfer yielding asymptotic Jaccard regret ≤ 3-2√2 from an (s^2+1)-dimensional F1 surrogate; (ii) a MinHash square-loss surrogate achieving a floor of α for Jaccard-regret with dimension O((s^2+s log(1/ρ))/α^2) or O((s+log(1/ρ))/α^2) depending on the variant. Zero-regret calibration requires exponential dimension; for any fixed additive regret tolerance, polynomial prediction dimension is achievable.

Conclusion: Exact calibration of Jaccard-based surrogates is infeasible in high dimensions due to exponential dimensionality, but practical, polynomial-dimension surrogates with provable regret guarantees are possible for fixed additive tolerances; the results link combinatorial structures (MinHash, Möbius inversion) to statistical calibration in multi-label problems.

Abstract: The per-instance Jaccard score, or intersection over union (IoU), is standard in multi-label classification and binary segmentation. With $s$ labels, its loss matrix has $2^s$ outcomes and reports. Under the convention $\mathrm{Jac}(\varnothing,\varnothing)=1$, we prove that the Jaccard score, shifted-loss, and ordinary loss matrices are nonsingular and that the loss columns have affine dimension $2^s-1$. The proof combines a finite MinHash Gram representation with Boolean Möbius inversion. For exact calibration, we prove $2^{s-1} \leq \mathrm{CCdim}(L^{\mathrm{Jac}}) \leq 2^s-1$. The lower bound uses a factorially weighted distribution with $2^{s-1}+1$ supported outcomes and Bayes-optimal reports. Consequently, every exactly calibrated convex surrogate requires exponentially many prediction coordinates. We also give two polynomial-dimensional approximation guarantees with explicit regret transfers. A new $F_1$-to-Jaccard transfer turns an existing $(s^2+1)$-dimensional $F_1$ surrogate into a polynomial-time rule with asymptotic Jaccard regret at most $3-2\sqrt{2}$. For any $α>0$ and $0<ρ<1$, a MinHash square-loss surrogate attains Jaccard-regret floor $α$ uniformly over arbitrary conditional label distributions. With probability at least $1-ρ$, the direct construction has dimension $O((s^2+s\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$, while a signed variant has dimension $O((s+\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$. Thus zero-regret calibration requires exponential dimension, whereas every fixed additive regret tolerance admits polynomial prediction dimension.

</details>


### [268] [Defensive Boosting for Online Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13554)
*Georgy Noarov,Aaron Roth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Defensive Booster unifies two complementary online boosting guarantees with a single weak learner: competitive Brier score against the span(H) and, under a smooth weak-learning condition, performance matching online classification boosting; plus a strongly adaptive variant and strong empirical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses online probabilistic forecasting of binary outcomes under adaptive adversaries. Existing online boosting yields either Brier-score competitiveness (gradient boosting) or classification-error guarantees (weak-to-strong boosting) but not both. The work seeks a single algorithm delivering dual guarantees.

Method: Introduce the Defensive Booster, a defensive forecasting algorithm. It implements a dual view of boosting: when randomized error is high, its weight updates form a smooth reweighting leading to low edge for all weak hypotheses, producing an ex-post hard-core certificate of failure of the weak-learning condition. It achieves two guarantees simultaneously and includes a strongly adaptive variant that holds on every time interval. It uses just one weak learner, improving efficiency over ensembles.

Result: The algorithm is competitively close to the best predictor in span(H) in Brier score at the same rate as online gradient boosting on every adaptive sequence. When the realized transcript satisfies the smooth weak-learning condition, its Brier score and randomized classification error achieve the same rate as online classification boosting. A strongly adaptive variant maintains both guarantees on every time interval. Empirically, Defensive Booster shows strong predictive performance and orders-of-magnitude faster runtimes than prior baselines on synthetic and real data streams.

Conclusion: The Defensive Booster provides a unifying, efficient online boosting approach that delivers dual theoretical guarantees regardless of whether the span(H) contains an accurate predictor. It matches the best gradient-boosting benchmarks while retaining strong, condition-dependent classification guarantees and superior practical efficiency.

Abstract: We study online probabilistic forecasting of binary outcomes chosen by an adaptive adversary. Given an online learning algorithm for a weak hypothesis class $H$, we would like to efficiently obtain two incomparable guarantees that existing online boosting techniques provide separately. Online gradient boosting competes in Brier score with the best predictor induced by the span of $H$ on every sequence, but promises nothing when the span does not contain an accurate predictor. Online weak-to-strong boosting drives classification error to zero under a weak-learning condition, but promises little when that condition fails.
  We give a simple defensive forecasting algorithm, the Defensive Booster, that obtains both guarantees. On every adaptive sequence, its Brier score is competitive with the best prediction induced by the span of $H$ at the same rate as online gradient boosting; simultaneously, whenever the realized transcript satisfies the smooth weak-learning condition, its Brier score and randomized classification error satisfy the same rate guarantee as online classification boosting. This is achieved by operationalizing the "dual view" of boosting: When the algorithm's randomized classification error is persistently high, its mistake weights form a smooth reweighting on which every weak hypothesis has low edge, yielding an ex-post hard-core certificate that the weak-learning condition fails. We also develop a strongly adaptive variant, which satisfies both guarantees on every time interval. The Defensive Booster is very efficient: it accesses just one weak-class learner, whereas the prior online boosting methods we compare against maintain large weak-learner ensembles. Experiments on synthetic and real data streams demonstrate its strong predictive performance (sometimes substantially improving over all prior baselines) coupled with orders-of-magnitude faster runtime.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [269] [RoboSynChallenge: Mastering Real-World Dexterity via Generalizing Synthesized Manipulation Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12416)
*Runyi Zhao,Ruixin Wu,Chengkun Li,Hongrui Zhang,Ang Li,Ruixing Jin,Yueci Deng,Yingying Guo,Lihe Ding,Shaocong Dong,Tianfan Xue,Yanjun Gao,Yudong Luo,Pascal Poupart,Simo Wu,Kui Jia,Wei-shi Zheng,Guiliang Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboSynChallenge proposes a unified benchmark for generalizable robotic manipulation, pairing large-scale synthetic data with unseen real-world evaluation, and providing baseline policies to enable reproducible, data-efficient learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the bottleneck of scarce and narrow real-world data, which limits generalizable manipulation policies. A scalable, standardized framework is needed to train in simulation yet validate in the real world.

Method: A unified benchmark spanning tasks, environments, and difficulty levels. Synthetic state-action data generation is used to train policies, while final evaluation is conducted exclusively on unseen real-world manipulation environments. Baselines include Transformer-, Diffusion-, Vision-Language-Action, and World-Action-Model based policies to ensure reproducibility.

Result: A framework and protocol that enables training on synthesized data and evaluation on real-world tasks, with baseline implementations provided to ensure comparability and reproducibility across participants.

Conclusion: Coupling scalable simulation-based training with rigorous real-world validation advances the development of broadly capable, data-efficient, and adaptable manipulation systems, contributing toward general robotic intelligence.

Abstract: Achieving generalizable robotic manipulation remains a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Despite rapid advances in model architectures and learning algorithms, progress is often limited by the scarcity and narrow diversity of real-world data. The RoboSynChallenge competition introduces a unified benchmark to evaluate and advance the generalizability of manipulation policies across a spectrum of tasks, environments, and difficulty levels. To alleviate the shortage of realistic data, the challenge integrates large-scale synthetic data generation with standardized real-world robotic evaluation. Participants are encouraged to leverage synthesized state-action trials to improve general-purpose policy learning, while final assessments are conducted exclusively on unseen real-world manipulation environments. Baseline implementations, including Transformer-, Diffusion-, Vision-Language-Action, and World-Action-Model-based policies, are provided to ensure reproducibility and comparability. By coupling scalable simulation-based training with rigorous real-world validation, RoboSynChallenge aims to foster the development of broadly capable, data-efficient, and adaptable manipulation systems, thereby paving the way toward truly general robotic intelligence.

</details>


### [270] [Attune: A Self-Annotation Tool for Understanding Robot Operator Attention Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12650)
*Puqi Zhou,Sungsoo Ray Hong,David Porfirio*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Attune is a pre-deployment tool that uses AI-assisted gaze analysis to identify and annotate operator eye gaze during multi-robot supervision, producing summaries to guide behavior design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designers of multi-robot supervision interfaces lack empirical guidance on how robot behavior influences operator attention; there is variation in operator gaze across individuals, and a tool is needed to elicit and summarize attention patterns.

Method: Developed Attune to automatically detect meaningful gaze shifts, provide AI-assisted annotations explaining why shifts occurred, and output summaries of gaze patterns; evaluated via a user study where participants annotated visual triggers for attention.

Result: Gaze patterns vary across operators; Attune effectively characterizes operator attention and aids in understanding how gaze relates to robot behavior in supervision tasks.

Conclusion: Attune provides empirical guidance for calibrating robot behavior to different operator attention profiles, supporting attention-aware design of multi-robot supervision interfaces.

Abstract: Deploying robot fleets in complex, real-world environments requires human operators to supervise multiple robots simultaneously. Managing operator attention is a fundamental challenge of designing multi-robot supervision interfaces, encompassing both feed layout and feed content (i.e., robot behavior design). Thus far, designers lack empirical guidance on the latter-how to change a robot's behavior to capture, sustain, or relinquish operator attention during multi-robot supervision. In our vision of the future, designers should be able to use this guidance to calibrate robot behavior to different operator attention profiles. Treating operator eye gaze as a robot behavior design clue, we created a pre-deployment elicitation tool called Attune. Attune automatically identifies when meaningful gaze shifts occur, provides AI assistance for annotating why shifts occurred, and outputs a summary of operator gaze patterns for operator review. We evaluated Attune through a user study in which participants annotated the visual triggers that drew their attention. Our findings unveil variation in observed gaze patterns and reveal how Attune helps characterize operator attention.

</details>


### [271] [FUSE: Active Functional Affordance Grounding through Adaptive Semantic-Geometric Evidence Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12683)
*Zhou Chen,Sathyanarayanan N. Aakur*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FUSE enables active, view-planning-driven functional grounding for embodied agents, achieving efficient, high-quality grounding and introducing a Habitat-based benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Objects must be identified by function rather than identity; existing grounding methods use fixed viewpoints and struggle when functional cues are occluded or incomplete, necessitating active observation planning.

Method: Propose FUSE: adaptive semantic-geometric evidence acquisition that combines explicit uncertainty-driven exploration with a learned amortized planner to selectively view informative viewpoints; sequential exploration; introduce a Habitat-based benchmark for evaluation.

Result: FUSE achieves the highest observed non-oracle grounding performance and reduces computation by 1.33x relative to fully explicit exploration, maintaining effectiveness across multiple affordance knowledge sources.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility and efficiency of active functional grounding; the adaptive framework generalizes across knowledge sources and environments; the benchmark provides a standard evaluation platform.

Abstract: Embodied agents must often identify and interact with objects based on their function rather than their identity, requiring them to actively acquire observations that reveal discriminative functional evidence. Existing affordance grounding methods operate from fixed viewpoints and lack mechanisms for deciding where to look when functional cues are occluded or incomplete. We introduce Active Functional Affordance Grounding, a new task in which an agent sequentially explores a scene to identify and spatially ground an object satisfying a functional query. To address this problem, we propose FUSE, an adaptive semantic-geometric evidence acquisition framework that combines explicit uncertainty-driven exploration with a learned amortized planner to efficiently select informative viewpoints. We further introduce a Habitat-based benchmark for evaluating active functional grounding. Experiments show that FUSE achieves the highest observed non-oracle grounding performance while reducing computation by 1.33x relative to fully explicit exploration, and remains effective across multiple affordance knowledge sources.

</details>


### [272] [SAP-Nav: Spatial Semantic Representation Meets Active Perception for Hierarchical Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12707)
*Xuetong Pei,Jian Liu,Vidura Munasinghe,Bo Miao,U-Xuan Tan,Wenrui Ding,Na Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAP-Nav is a fully online, zero-shot framework for hierarchical open-vocabulary object navigation that uses active perception to build a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from explored views and actively verifies viewpoints before evaluating candidates, achieving state-of-the-art performance on LangMap and HM3D-OVON without task-specific training or precomputed maps; demonstrated on real robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcoming partial observations in hierarchical OVON, where targets may be described with scene-, room-, region-, or instance-level cues, requires persistent environment-level evidence and discriminative candidate views; existing methods struggle to combine spatial grounding with target verification in an online, zero-shot setting.

Method: Incrementally construct a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from actively acquired room views. Enable spatial semantic queries from explored locations. Use Active Viewpoint Verification to determine if current observation suffices; reposition to more informative viewpoints and re-verify against category and attribute constraints. Works without task-specific training or precomputed scene maps; supports both hierarchical and standard OVON.

Result: Achieves overall best performance on LangMap and HM3D-OVON, including a 12.2% improvement in success rate (SR) over training-based methods for region-level navigation. Real-world robot experiments validate practical feasibility.

Conclusion: SAP-Nav demonstrates that online, zero-shot hierarchical OVON can be effectively solved via active perception through a learnable yet map-free representation and viewpoint-driven verification, with broad applicability to both hierarchical and standard OVON.

Abstract: Hierarchical open-vocabulary object navigation (OVON) requires agents to follow free-form instructions that may specify targets through scene-, room-, region-, and instance-level cues in unseen environments. Although recent work LangMap has formalized this setting, reliably solving it under partial observations remains challenging: spatial grounding requires persistent environment-level evidence, whereas target verification requires clear and discriminative candidate views. We present SAP-Nav, a fully online, zero-shot framework that addresses both requirements through active perception. SAP-Nav incrementally constructs a Queryable Spatial-Semantic Representation from actively acquired room views, enabling spatial semantic queries from any explored location. It further employs Active Viewpoint Verification to assess whether the current observation provides sufficient evidence and, when necessary, reposition the agent to a more informative viewpoint before verifying candidates against category and attribute constraints. Although designed for hierarchical OVON, SAP-Nav supports both hierarchical and standard category-level OVON without task-specific training or precomputed scene maps. Experiments on LangMap and HM3D-OVON show that SAP-Nav achieves the overall best performance, including a 12.2% improvement in SR over training-based methods on region-level navigation. Real-world robot experiments further demonstrate its practical feasibility. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [273] [Genetic Fuzzy System-Based Multi-Robot Coordination for Planetary Missions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12755)
*Daegyun Choi,Donghoon Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GA-optimized fuzzy controllers enable decentralized multi-robot object transport on a traversability map derived from elevation data, aiming to minimize path length and avoid obstacles; validated across varied scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address path-length minimization and obstacle avoidance in unstructured environments by reducing map dimensionality via terrain traversability and using GA-tuned fuzzy controllers for velocity commands.

Method: Convert elevation maps to 2D traversability maps using slope thresholds to identify non-traversable regions. Design fuzzy inference systems (FIS) to generate robot velocities for transporting objects to a target. Optimize FIS parameters with a genetic algorithm across scenarios (local minima, obstacle-adjacent target, clutter). Apply trained FIS to testing traversability maps and validate over multiple scenarios.

Result: Trained FIS models can navigate towards targets while reducing total path length and avoiding obstacles; demonstrated robustness across several challenging scenarios in the testing environment.

Conclusion: GA-optimized fuzzy control for decentralized MRS on traversability maps shows viability for unstructured environments. Further details needed on GA settings, FIS structure, robustness to noise and dynamics, and real-time performance; potential extensions include dynamic obstacles and inter-robot coordination.

Abstract: This paper proposes a decentralized approach for a multi-robot system (MRS) using a genetic fuzzy system to perform a collaborative object transportation task that minimizes the total path length of the MRS in unstructured environment while avoiding obstacles. For an environment given by an elevation map, terrain traversability analysis with respect to the slope is performed to reduce the dimension and identify non-traversable areas that can be considered as obstacles, and the given map is converted into a traversability map in two dimensional space. In the training process, proposed fuzzy inference systems (FISs) to generate the MRS's velocity for transporting an object to a target position are optimized by a genetic algorithm with several scenarios, such as a local minima, a target that is close to an obstacle, and a cluttered environment. The trained FIS models are applied to the testing environment, which is the converted traversability map, and validated using multiple scenarios.

</details>


### [274] [AirForesight: Current-to-Future Spatial Map Imagination with Cross-Space Planning Consistency for UAV-VLN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12835)
*Yutong Liu,Xiaojie Li,Mingzhu Xu,Jianlong Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AirForesight introduces a current-to-future spatial map imagination framework for UAV-VLN, learning a structured current-map from multi-view data and predicting future trajectories, with structured causal attention and cross-space planning consistency, achieving strong results on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UAV-VLN approaches largely map vision-language inputs directly to actions, offering limited explicit grounding of the scene and weak future-oriented reasoning in outdoor settings. A map-like, future-aware representation can enhance navigation reliability and safety.

Method: 1) Learn a structured current-map from multi-view observations, supervised by current-map reconstruction and future-trajectory prediction. 2) Use structured causal attention to propagate current spatial knowledge to future-map reasoning. 3) Aggregate current and future representations to predict the next 3D waypoint. 4) Introduce cross-space planning consistency loss to align the map-space trajectory direction with the expert action direction from the ground-truth waypoint displacement.

Result: Experimental results on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S, plus extensive ablations, demonstrate strong performance and validate the effectiveness and stability of AirForesight.

Conclusion: The proposed AirForesight framework advances UAV-VLN by incorporating current-to-future spatial imagination and grounding, improving navigation guidance through structured map representations and consistency constraints.

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicle Vision-Language Navigation (UAV-VLN) requires agents to follow language instructions, infer spatial structure from sparse multi-view observations, and execute feasible 3D motion in complex outdoor environments. Despite recent progress with large language models, most existing methods still map vision-language inputs directly to actions, providing limited explicit scene grounding and future-aware spatial reasoning. We propose AirForesight, a current-to-future spatial map imagination framework for UAV-VLN. AirForesight first learns a structured current-map representation from multi-view observations. This representation is jointly supervised by current-map reconstruction and future-trajectory prediction, encouraging it to encode both present scene structure and future motion intent. Under structured causal attention, the current spatial knowledge is propagated to future-map reasoning, and the resulting current and future representations are aggregated to predict the next 3D waypoint. To make spatial imagination more relevant to navigation, we introduce a cross-space planning consistency loss that encourages directional agreement between the predicted map-space trajectory and the expert action direction derived from the ground-truth waypoint displacement. Experiments on OpenUAV and AerialVLN-S, together with extensive ablations, demonstrate strong performance and support the effectiveness and stability of the proposed framework.

</details>


### [275] [ASPIRE-VINS: Adaptive Spline-based Visual-inertial Navigation System With Robust 3D Measurement Residuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12840)
*Kwangyik Jung,Eungchang Mason Lee,Taekjun Oh,Hyun Myung*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ASPIRE-VINS introduces motion-adaptive continuous-time trajectory estimation for VINS using AKP, MRS, and 3D-MSR to improve accuracy and flexibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete-time, keyframe-based VINS and uniform-continuous representations each have limitations: residual evaluation at arbitrary timestamps requires flexible timing; uniform knots under- or over-parameterize rapid dynamics or static intervals. A motion-adaptive, continuous-time representation aims to improve accuracy and efficiency across diverse motions.

Method: Integrates adaptive knot placement (AKP) based on local motion variation, multi-resolution splines (MRS) with bounded local tangent-space refinement, and 3D measurement-space residuals (3D-MSR) that align transformed features with calibrated observation rays to enforce bearing consistency.

Result: Experiments show ASPIRE-VINS achieves competitive or lower trajectory errors compared with baselines across diverse motion and sensing conditions.

Conclusion: Motion-adaptive, continuous-time trajectory modeling with AKP+MRS+3D-MSR is effective for robust, accurate VINS under varied dynamics.

Abstract: Visual-inertial navigation systems estimate six-degree-of-freedom motion by fusing visual and inertial data. Modern discrete-time methods with IMU preintegration provide strong accuracy and efficiency, but keyframe-based representations can be less flexible when residuals must be evaluated at arbitrary timestamps or when motion-dependent temporal resolution is needed. Continuous-time splines address this issue by representing the trajectory as a smooth temporal function, but uniformly spaced knots can under-represent rapid dynamics or over-parameterize static intervals. This letter proposes ASPIRE-VINS, a continuous-time VINS framework that combines adaptive knot placement (AKP), multi-resolution splines (MRS), and 3D measurement-space residuals (3D-MSR). AKP allocates knots according to local motion variation, MRS adds bounded local refinement in tangent space, and 3D-MSR provides bearing consistency by aligning transformed features with calibrated observation rays in 3D measurement space. Experiments show that ASPIRE-VINS achieves competitive or lower trajectory errors than the compared baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of motion-adaptive continuous-time trajectory modeling under diverse motion and sensing conditions.

</details>


### [276] [BrainWAM: Action-Space Coordination of Semantic Priors and Predictive Dynamics for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12854)
*Bing Zhan,Shuyao Shang,Jiahao Gu,Shuo Lu,Yuan Xu,Zhao Wang,Yida Wang,Xueyang Zhang,Kun Zhan,Lue Fan,Zhaoxiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BrainWAM introduces a structured, dual-pathway planning framework that separately handles semantic reasoning and predictive world modeling, then aligns them through compact action representations, complemented by asynchronous rectified-flow inference to reduce latency. It achieves state-of-the-art NAVSIM scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous-driving approaches either rely on semantic priors (VLA) or predictive dynamics (WAM). Naively combining them with joint attention causes an attention-allocation mismatch, privileging semantics over dynamics. A coordinated, specialized-action-space design could leverage both strengths.

Method: Two specialized action-oriented pathways encode semantic reasoning (VLM priors) and predictive world modeling (WAMs). These pathways are aligned at compact action representations rather than token-level attention. An asynchronous rectified-flow inference strategy with decoupled video and action denoising reduces latency while maintaining predictive context.

Result: BrainWAM achieves state-of-the-art results on NAVSIM v1 (89.5 PDMS) and NAVSIM v2 (89.6 EPDMS), consistently outperforming VLA-only or WAM-only baselines.

Conclusion: Structured coordination of semantic and predictive modules with efficient inference is a practical and promising direction for autonomous driving systems.

Abstract: Autonomous driving requires planning under both semantic constraints and predictive dynamics. Existing end-to-end driving approaches, however, typically emphasize only one side of this requirement: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models exploit VLM priors for semantic reasoning, while World Action Models (WAMs) provide future-aware prediction through generative world modeling. This naturally motivates a unified planner that can leverage both semantic priors and predictive dynamics. However, we find that a naive combination through joint token-level attention suffers from an attention-allocation mismatch, where semantic shortcuts dominate the shared attention space and suppress predictive dynamics. Inspired by neuroscience evidence that complex behavior arises from coordination among functionally specialized systems, we propose BrainWAM, a structured action-space coordination framework that converts semantic reasoning and predictive world modeling into two specialized action-oriented pathways, and aligns them at the level of compact action representations. We further introduce an asynchronous rectified-flow inference strategy with decoupled video and action denoising, which shortens inference latency while preserving planning-relevant predictive context. BrainWAM reaches state-of-the-art performance on both NAVSIM v1 (89.5 PDMS) and NAVSIM v2 (89.6 EPDMS), consistently outperforming VLA-only or WAM-only methods, highlighting BrainWAM as a practical and promising direction for autonomous driving systems.

</details>


### [277] [HumanoidVLN: A Physics-Grounded Simulator and Benchmark for Vision-Language Navigation Across Diverse Humanoid Embodiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12860)
*Quan-Dung Pham,Anh Dao,The-Anh Nguyen,Minh Nguyen-Dinh,Phuong Nam Dang,Tri Pham,Hung Tran,Bach Dao,Tuyen P. Le,Truong Nguyen,Quan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A physics-grounded humanoid VLN platform and benchmark (HumanoidVLN) enabling cross-embodiment navigation research with sim-to-real evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address VLN gaps for humanoids: physics-based constraints of bipedal locomotion, diverse morphologies, and egocentric vision distortions, enabling fair cross-robot comparisons and sim-to-real study.

Method: Develop HumanoidVLN on NVIDIA Isaac Sim with four humanoids (Unitree G1/H1, Internal-A/B), 10–12 lower-body DoF, heights 1.17–1.80 m; integrate a hierarchical RL locomotion policy with PD/MPC trackers; support external VLN models; create scenes via artist designs and 3D Gaussian Splatting; generate 933 collision-aware episodes with one fine-grained instruction plus three coarse variants via a dual-generator reviewer-paraphraser pipeline with human-in-the-loop verification.

Result: JanusVLN achieves the highest mean success rate (43.55%) and nDTW (48.38) across four models/embodiments; in a 20-episode sim-to-real pilot (DualVLN on Unitree G1), navigation errors correlate highly (r=0.935) with mean absolute error 0.68 m and mean trajectory similarity 0.782 (±0.188) nDTW.

Conclusion: The platform reveals interactions among VLN models, controllers, and humanoid embodiments; open-source code, benchmark, and data will be released, enabling broader evaluation and transfer across humanoid morphologies.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) for humanoid robots poses challenges existing benchmarks fail to address: bipedal locomotion imposes physical constraints absent from wheeled agents, humanoid morphologies vary across platforms, and egocentric observations are distorted by locomotion-induced camera dynamics. We present HumanoidVLN, a physics-grounded simulator and benchmark for VLN across diverse humanoid embodiments. Built on NVIDIA Isaac Sim, our platform supports an extensible set of humanoid configurations, demonstrated on four robots (Unitree G1, Unitree H1, Internal-A, Internal-B) spanning 10-12 lower-body DoF and heights from 1.17m to 1.80m, via a hierarchical control stack combining a reinforcement learning locomotion policy with interchangeable PD or MPC path trackers. New robots and VLN models integrate with minimal effort; we demonstrate compatibility with NaVILA, DualVLN, StreamVLN, and JanusVLN. Environments are drawn from artist-designed scenes and 3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructions, filtered for navigable areas exceeding 100 square meters. Instructions are generated by a dual generator-reviewer plus paraphraser multi-agent pipeline with human-in-the-loop verification, yielding 933 collision-aware reference episodes, each paired with one fine-grained instruction and three coarse-grained stylistic variants (formal, natural, casual). Across four models and four embodiments, JanusVLN achieves the highest mean success rate of 43.55% and nDTW of 48.38. In a 20-episode sim-to-real pilot with DualVLN and the Unitree G1, navigation errors correlate strongly (r=0.935), with a mean absolute difference of 0.68m and mean trajectory similarity of 0.782 (+/-0.188) nDTW. These results highlight the interaction between VLN models, controllers, and humanoid embodiments under physical execution. Code, benchmark, and data will be released upon acceptance at https://humanoid-vln.github.io/.

</details>


### [278] [AMR-Pose: An Active LED Marker-Based Relative Pose Estimation Framework With Probabilistic Switching PnP for Cooperative AUVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12866)
*Zeyu Sha,Xiaorui Wang,Mingyang Yang,Feitian Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AMR-Pose delivers robust active-marker based relative pose estimation for leader–follower AUVs using a PSwPnP estimator that fuses SE(3) pose propagation, probabilistic marker association, and visibility-adaptive measurements, validated in water-tank and real-time experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater vision is severely degraded by turbidity, variable illumination, reflections, and occlusions, making reliable relative localization difficult for cooperative AUV tasks.

Method: Deploys a compact leader-mounted marker module with one red central LED and three blue peripheral LEDs. Observes markers and uses a probabilistic switching PnP (PSwPnP) framework that combines Lie-group SE(3) pose propagation, probabilistic marker association, and visibility-adaptive fusion to estimate full six-DOF relative pose.

Result: Demonstrates accurate, smooth, and robust six-DOF relative pose estimation under challenging underwater conditions in water-tank experiments with motion-capture ground truth. Closed-loop leader–follower experiments confirm real-time viability for relative pose feedback in cooperative underwater robotics.

Conclusion: AMR-Pose provides stable, real-time relative pose estimation for cooperative AUVs by leveraging active LED markers and a visibility-aware probabilistic PSwPnP framework that maintains geometric consistency across partial observations and visibility transitions.

Abstract: Reliable relative pose estimation between autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) is critical for cooperative ocean exploration, sampling, and multi-robot coordination. However, achieving robust vision-based relative localization in underwater environments remains challenging due to severe optical degradation, including turbidity, illumination variations, reflections, and intermittent feature occlusions. This paper presents AMR-Pose, an active LED marker-based relative pose estimation framework for cooperative AUVs. A compact marker module consisting of one red central LED and three blue peripheral LEDs is developed and integrated onto the leader AUV to provide distinctive visual features under complex underwater conditions. Building upon the detected marker observations, a probabilistic switching Perspective-n-Point estimator (PSwPnP) is developed by combining Lie-group pose propagation on $SE(3)$, probabilistic marker association, and visibility-adaptive measurement fusion for robust six-degree-of-freedom relative pose estimation. The proposed framework dynamically adapts the estimation process according to marker visibility, maintaining geometric consistency and temporal stability during partial observations and visibility transitions. Extensive water-tank experiments with motion-capture ground truth validate that AMR-Pose achieves accurate, smooth, and robust relative pose estimation under challenging underwater conditions. Closed-loop leader-follower experiments further demonstrate its feasibility for real-time relative pose feedback in cooperative underwater robotics.

</details>


### [279] [Temporal GRPO: Beyond Trajectory-Level Credit in Vision-Language-Action Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13026)
*Yao Zhou,Hang Gao,Fengge Wu,Changwen Zheng,Wenwen Qiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Temporal GRPO mitigates trajectory-level credit aliasing in outcome-driven RL for VLA tasks by detecting task stages and using stage-specific action intervals with stage-wise advantages; yields improved success and sample efficiency on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO-Long.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In GRPO-based post-training, a rollout-level advantage penalizes earlier actions if later stages fail, causing credit aliasing that hampers learning across long horizons. A stage-aware credit assignment aims to align incentives with progress through the task.

Method: Detect detectable task stages and align rollouts with stage-specific action intervals. Compute and apply stage advantages to the corresponding intervals within a single policy update. Additionally, on LIBERO-Long, use controlled updates that preserve shared prerequisite stages and focus improvement at the first stage where rollout outcomes diverge.

Result: On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO improves task success and sample efficiency with consistent gains across task horizons. On LIBERO-Long, controlled updates preserve shared prerequisites and concentrate improvement at the first divergence stage.

Conclusion: Stage-aligned credit assignment effectively addresses credit aliasing in outcome-driven RL for VLA tasks, offering improved performance and sample efficiency and showing potential for broader applicability in staged or hierarchical tasks.

Abstract: Outcome-driven reinforcement learning offers a scalable way to post-train vision-language-action (VLA) policies from sparse task-success feedback. In common GRPO-based VLA post-training, one rollout-level advantage is applied to every action in the trajectory. A rollout that completes several valid stages but fails later can therefore penalize the actions that produced its earlier progress. We call this trajectory-level credit aliasing. Temporal GRPO addresses this problem by constructing detectable task stages, aligning each rollout with stage-specific action intervals, and comparing only rollouts that have entered the same stage. The resulting stage advantages are applied to their corresponding intervals in a single policy update. On RoboTwin 2.0, Temporal GRPO improves task success and sample efficiency, with consistent gains across task horizons. Controlled updates on LIBERO-Long preserve shared prerequisite stages and concentrate improvement at the first stage where rollout outcomes diverge.

</details>


### [280] [H2R-Bench: Benchmarking Human-to-Robot Manipulation Video Generation in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13049)
*Dingyi Rong,Yue Shi,Chaofan Ma,Jiezhang Cao,Zongrui Wang,Zeyu Zhang,Yao Mu,Guangtao Zhai,Ning Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces H2R-Bench, a benchmark for cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation. It evaluates how well models convert egocentric human demonstrations into robot-centric videos across 11 models, 6 manipulation families, and 2 embodiments, using five evaluation axes; results show current video world models struggle with embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scalable robot manipulation data, leveraging abundant egocentric human videos. Cross-embodiment transfer from human hands to robotic end-effectors is underexplored and challenging; a diagnostic benchmark is needed to assess and drive improvements in video world models for this transfer.

Method: Construct H2R-Bench: a dataset of instances with a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations (task goals, action events, functional contacts, object responses). Evaluation spans five dimensions: goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and video quality. Benchmark 11 state-of-the-art video generation models over 6 manipulation families and 2 robot embodiments; provide systematic diagnostics of cross-embodiment transfer capabilities.

Result: Empirical evaluation reveals current video world models underperform in cross-embodiment transfer, with frequent failures in embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution.

Conclusion: H2R-Bench offers a systematic diagnostic framework to assess and guide the development of video world models toward bridging the human-to-robot embodiment gap, converting human manipulation observations into robot-centric training data.

Abstract: Large-scale manipulation data is essential for robot learning, yet collecting robot demonstrations remains expensive and difficult to scale. Meanwhile, abundant egocentric human manipulation videos provide rich behavioral experiences, but transferring them across embodiments remains challenging due to differences between human hands and robotic end-effectors. Recent advances in video world models offer a promising pathway to synthesize robot-centric manipulation videos from human observations, while their cross-embodiment transfer capability remains largely unexplored. Therefore, we introduce H2R-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating cross-embodiment human-to-robot manipulation video generation, where models transform egocentric human demonstrations into robot manipulation videos under specified embodiments. Each benchmark instance contains a human demonstration video, target embodiment constraints, and source-grounded annotations covering task goals, action events, functional contacts, and object responses. H2R-Bench evaluates generated videos through five dimensions, including goal-state completion, action-event completion, functional contact transfer, embodiment correctness, and general video quality. We benchmark eleven state-of-the-art video generation models across six manipulation families and two robot embodiments. Our evaluation reveals that current video world models remain limited in human-to-robot manipulation transfer: even leading models often fail in embodiment consistency, functional interaction, and task execution. H2R-Bench provides a systematic diagnostic framework for evaluating whether video world models can bridge the human-to-robot embodiment gap and convert human manipulation observations into robot-centric training resources.

</details>


### [281] [Semantic Radiance Fields as Simulators for Spatial Reasoning in Real-World Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13095)
*Nico Heider,Michał Jan Włodarczyk,Katarzyna Wasielewska-Michniewska,Przemysław Hołda,Martin Schieck,Marcin Paprzycki,Maria Ganzha,Bogdan Franczyk*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Semantic Radiance Fields (SRF) unify geometry, appearance, and per-class semantics in a single 3D radiance field reconstructed from real-scene RGB captures, enabling novel-view rendering and semantic/occupancy queries for grounded, diverse environments to train and evaluate spatial reasoning agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current simulators trade realism for ground-truth semantics or vice versa. A representation that is both realistic and semantically queryable is needed to train embodied agents with reliable ground truth.

Method: Lift 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into a 3D radiance field, reconstruct the field from posed RGB captures of real scenes, enabling novel-view synthesis, semantic queries, and free-space/occupancy queries within one grounded representation.

Result: Provides ground-truth semantics, appearance, and occupancy information within a single SRF, enabling efficient generation of diverse real-world environments for training/evaluating spatial reasoning models; demonstrated via an orchard apple-reaching task using SRF as a simulator for rendering, semantics, and physics queries.

Conclusion: SRFs offer a practical bridge between realism and semantics for embodied AI, scalable to diverse real-world environments and tasks by combining geometry, appearance, and semantic identity in a unified, queryable representation.

Abstract: Training and evaluating spatial reasoning in embodied agents requires diverse environments that are both geometrically faithful and semantically queryable. Synthetic simulators offer ground truth semantics but sacrifice realism; simulators based on reconstructions of real-world environments have realistic appearance but lack ground truth semantics by default. We propose using Semantic Radiance Fields (SRF) as simulators for spatial reasoning agents. SRFs are a representation that unifies these requirements by lifting 2D semantic segmentations from pretrained vision models into a 3D radiance field that jointly encodes geometry, appearance, and per-class semantic identity. The resulting fields are reconstructed from posed RGB captures of real scenes and support novel-view synthesis, semantic and free-space queries within a single grounded representation. This enables the efficient generation of diverse real-world environments to train and evaluate spatial reasoning models. As an example application, we outline an SRF-driven simulator for an orchard apple-reaching task, in which the radiance field supplies camera rendering, semantic ground truth, and occupancy queries to a physics engine.

</details>


### [282] [S2-HWM: Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model for Long-Horizon Surgical Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13103)
*Shuzhe Zhang,Xin Zhu,Yinling Qian,Qiong Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: S2-HWM introduces a sparse event-structured hierarchical world model for long-horizon robot manipulation by learning sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker; it uses an Event Transition Model to predict next boundary, duration, and reward, enabling variable-duration planning beyond primitive imagination horizon; achieves 98.7% success on SurRoL PegTransfer, surpassing GAS DreamerV3 by 22.7 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon surgical tasks have sparse, irregular rewards; existing world models operate at primitive-step resolution, lacking structured intermediate signals; manually defined stages are not aligned with state-dependent interaction transitions.

Method: Propose S2-HWM architecture: extract sparse event evidence from latent trajectories; event-level manager updates goals, each latent-goal conditions worker actions until next update; event evidence also forms an Event Transition Model (ETM) to predict next boundary stochastic state, segment duration, and accumulated segment reward; chain event-level predictions to extend horizon; train manager and worker with respective objectives (manager for higher-level planning, worker with actor-critic).

Result: On SurRoL-based PegTransfer task, 98.7% success; 22.7 percentage-point improvement over GAS DreamerV3.

Conclusion: Hierarchical, sparse-event world models with variable-duration planning improve long-horizon robotic manipulation under sparse rewards; the ETM and event-based coordination enable longer-horizon foresight while preserving robust primitive control.

Abstract: Long-horizon surgical robot manipulation is challenging because task rewards are sparse, while meaningful interaction changes occur at irregular intervals. Existing world-model agents typically imagine at primitive-step resolution, leaving variable-duration task progress implicit. Manually specified stages can provide intermediate structure, but their task specific boundaries are difficult to align with state-dependent interaction transitions. We propose S2-HWM, a Sparse Event-Structured Hierarchical World Model that learns sparse event evidence from primitive latent trajectories to coordinate an event-level manager and a primitive-step worker. The event evidence schedules manager goal updates, and each selected latent goal conditions the worker's primitive actions until the next update. The learned event evidence also forms variable-duration segments for an Event Transition Model (ETM), which predicts the next?boundary stochastic state, segment duration, and accumulated segment reward. Chaining these event-level predictions provides a variable-duration continuation beyond the primitive imagination horizon for manager learning, while the worker retains primitive-step actor-critic learning. On a SurRoL-based PegTransfer task, S2-HWM achieves a success rate of 98.7%, outperforming the flat GAS DreamerV3 baseline by 22.7 percentage points.

</details>


### [283] [FAM-DQ: A Dual-Quadrotor-Based Fully Actuated Aerial Manipulator for High-Torque Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13220)
*Xuwei Yang,Ruoyu Ren,Ziqian Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces FAM-DQ: a dual-quadrotor fully actuated aerial manipulator with decoupled 6-DoF control and high torque, validated experimentally (1.019 N·m torque, 0.447 kg; 2.28 N·m/kg).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerial manipulation requires large interaction forces/torques with precise end-effector control; underactuated designs suffer strong position-attitude coupling, while fully actuated designs face structural complexity, limited payload, and insufficient torque.

Method: Two quadrotor propulsion modules mounted at the ends of a central frame via passive joints, with a gear-driven servo to regulate the pointing direction, enabling decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control, omnidirectional manipulation, and enhanced torque output.

Result: Experimental validation through trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving; achieved a maximum torque of 1.019 N·m with a total mass of 0.447 kg, yielding a torque-to-mass ratio of 2.28 N·m/kg.

Conclusion: FAM-DQ provides decoupled 6-DoF end-effector control with enhanced torque output using a dual-quadrotor fully actuated architecture; validated experimentally, demonstrating viability for high-torque aerial manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Aerial physical interaction requires aerial manipulation platforms to generate large interaction forces and torques while maintaining precise end-effector control. However, conventional underactuated aerial manipulators suffer from strong position-attitude coupling, whereas fully actuated platform designs often face structural complexity, limited payload capacity, and insufficient torque output. This paper presents FAM-DQ, a dual-quadrotor based fully actuated aerial manipulator designed for high-torque physical interaction tasks. By mounting two quadrotor propulsion modules at the ends of a central frame through passive joints, while using a gear-driven servo to regulate the pointing direction, FAM-DQ achieves decoupled $6$-DoF end-effector control with omnidirectional manipulation capability and enhanced torque output. Experiments including trajectory tracking, attitude tracking, static torque measurement, and screw driving validate the proposed design. FAM-DQ achieves a maximum torque of $1.019~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m}$ with a total mass of $0.447~\mathrm{kg}$, corresponding to a torque-to-mass ratio of $2.28~\mathrm{N}\cdot\mathrm{m/kg}$.

</details>


### [284] [Manufacturing Complex Airtight Soft Pneumatic Actuators for Soft Robotics: Process Evaluation and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13233)
*Mohammed Abboodi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A comparative manufacturing study shows FDM as the most adaptable route for complex soft pneumatic actuators, with performance limited by material behavior and post-processing across methods; emphasizes airtightness depends on wall architecture and extrusion paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable fabrication of complex soft pneumatic actuators by comparing multiple manufacturing routes and identifying inherent defects vs. correctable process defects.

Method: Process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement applied to heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder-based additive manufacturing, liquid-based additive manufacturing, and fused deposition modeling (FDM) to map capabilities and limitations.

Result: Heat-shrink forming constrained by geometric conformity; casting limited by mold accessibility and bonded interfaces; powder-based methods trap residual material within enclosed passages; DLP (digital light processing) constrained by material properties and post-processing needs; FDM offered the most adaptable route with defects reducible via optimization. Airtightness depends on extrusion-path architecture; unsupported geometries hinder internal post-processing.

Conclusion: A practical design-for-manufacturing framework that guides material and process selection based on actuator architecture and manufacturing constraints, enabling development of complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators for soft robotics.

Abstract: Manufacturing complex soft pneumatic actuators remains challenging because geometric fidelity, compliance, structural integrity, and airtightness must be achieved simultaneously. This study presents a manufacturing-focused evaluation of several fabrication routes for complex pneumatic structures, including heat-shrink forming, silicone casting, powder- and liquid-based additive manufacturing, and fused deposition modeling (FDM). The processes were assessed through process screening, baseline fabrication, failure analysis, and process improvement to distinguish inherent process limitations from correctable manufacturing defects. Heat-shrink forming was limited by geometric conformity, casting by mold accessibility and bonded interfaces, powder-based methods by residual material trapped within enclosed passages, and digital light processing by the material properties and post-processing requirements of the investigated system. FDM provided the most adaptable route because its dominant defects could be progressively reduced through process optimization. The results further showed that airtightness depends not only on nominal wall thickness but also on extrusion-path architecture, while support-free geometry is important when access for internal post-processing is limited. These findings establish a practical design-for-manufacturing approach in which process selection is guided by the compatibility between actuator architecture and manufacturing constraints. The proposed approach provides practical guidance for developing complex, flexible, and airtight soft pneumatic actuators for soft robotic applications

</details>


### [285] [Predictive Relative-Velocity Steering for Safe Robotic Manipulator Teleoperation in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13284)
*Changhao Hu,Zeyi Liu,Songqiao Hu,Shuang Liu,Zihan Meng,Xiao He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A lightweight modular framework for proactive end-effector collision avoidance in teleoperation. It predicts potential collisions using time-to-collision (TTC) with overshoot protection and deflects the relative-velocity direction via Rodrigues' rotation formula, preserving speed to avoid deadlocks common in APF methods. It compensates for perception latency and operates at high frequency. Validated in simulation and on a real robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety risks in teleoperation when obstacles appear suddenly, especially under network latency or limited operator attention. Conventional methods (e.g., APF) can cause deadlocks or require high computation; there is a need for a fast, proactive and lightweight solution that preserves control bandwidth.

Method: Preprocess point cloud data; predict collisions using TTC with overshoot protection; rotate the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula to deflect its direction while preserving magnitude; this avoids deadlock by not altering speed. A prediction module compensates for point-cloud processing latency, enabling high-frequency control and modular, lightweight design suitable for teleoperation contexts.

Result: Simulation results show a higher end-effector collision-avoidance rate compared with baseline methods across diverse scenarios. Physical robot experiments validate the collision-avoidance effectiveness in real-world teleoperation. 

Conclusion: The approach delivers proactive end-effector collision avoidance at the velocity-command level, maintaining nominal speed while steering around obstacles. The Rodrigues-based deflection avoids deadlocks while the latency-compensating predictor and lightweight, modular design support high-frequency teleoperation control.

Abstract: Recent advances in teleoperation have enabled robotic manipulators to perform dexterous, human-arm-like motions. However, human operators may fail to avoid suddenly appearing obstacles promptly and effectively, particularly under network latency or limited attention, thereby creating safety risks. To address this issue, we propose a lightweight and modular framework for proactive collision avoidance, operating directly at the end-effector velocity-command level. After preprocessing the point cloud, the framework first predicts potential collisions based on time-to-collision (TTC) with integrated overshoot protection, and subsequently rotates the relative-velocity vector using Rodrigues' rotation formula. The deflection changes only the direction of the relative velocity while preserving its magnitude, thereby mitigating the deadlock problem commonly encountered by conventional artificial potential field (APF) methods. The prediction module compensates for point-cloud processing latency introduced by complex teleoperation pipelines, while the lightweight design enables the high-frequency control required for teleoperation. Simulations across diverse scenarios show that the proposed method achieves a higher end-effector collision avoidance rate than the baseline methods. Experiments on a physical robotic system further validate its collision-avoidance effectiveness.

</details>


### [286] [NestDex: Nested Policy Learning with Copilot Assisted Teleoperation for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13362)
*James Zhao,Jinhe Tang,Mingyuan Ba,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NestDex introduces a hierarchical imitation-learning framework for dexterous manipulation that reduces expert demonstration burden by using learned hand skills controlled via a single-DoF clutch. An inner hand policy uses proprioceptive history and vision-language cues to select hand skills; an outer visuomotor policy learns to control arm and hand using demonstrations without the inner policies at deployment. A hand-action VAE provides compact hand-action targets while preserving arm commands in joint space. Real-world experiments show improved demonstration reliability/efficiency and effective autonomous policy learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous manipulation requires coordinated finger trajectories and contact-rich behavior, making demonstrations difficult and prone to inconsistency. Reducing operator burden and decoupling hand skills from arm control can facilitate data collection and learning.

Method: A nested policy-learning framework (NestDex) where an operator uses a single-DoF clutch to regulate an inner hand policy; the inner policy adapts from proprioceptive history; a vision-language selector chooses the skill per task stage; an outer visuomotor policy learns to control both arm and hand without inner policies during deployment; a hand-action variational autoencoder compresses hand-actions while keeping arm commands in joint space.

Result: Across real-world dexterous manipulation tasks, NestDex improves demonstration reliability and efficiency, enabling effective autonomous policy learning as evidenced by empirical evaluations and a link to video demonstrations.

Conclusion: NestDex successfully reduces demonstration burden in dexterous manipulation by decomposing control into adjustable inner hand skills and an outer visuomotor policy, enabling reliable data collection and effective autonomous policy learning for real-world tasks.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation promises substantially richer robot interaction with the physical world, but learning these behaviours remains constrained by the difficulty of collecting consistent, complete-task demonstrations. Unlike parallel-jaw manipulation, dexterous tasks require the operator to coordinate arm motion with precise, contact-rich finger behaviour throughout the task. We introduce NestDex, a nested policy-learning framework that reduces this burden by using learned hand skills to assist demonstration collection. The operator controls the arm and regulates the active hand skill through a single-DoF clutch, rather than directly specifying the full finger trajectory. The inner hand policy adapts its motion from the latest proprioceptive history, while a vision-language selector activates the appropriate skill for each task stage. The resulting demonstrations train a separate outer visuomotor policy that controls both the arm and hand without the inner policies at deployment. A hand-action variational autoencoder provides compact hand-action targets while retaining arm commands in joint space. Across real-world dexterous manipulation experiments, NestDex improves demonstration reliability and efficiency, and the resulting empirical evaluations support effective autonomous policy learning. Video Demo are available at project website https://aus.bot/research/nestdex.

</details>


### [287] [FIRE-VLA: Failure-Informed Self-Evolution for Vision-Language-Action Models in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13395)
*Hao Dou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FIRE-VLA introduces a failure-informed self-evolution loop for VLA RL, converting unresolved low-signal rollouts into privileged supervision from a frozen round-start copy, improving robustness by reducing persistent failures without external teachers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO can rank but not fix failures when all sampled trajectories are poor; there is a need to adapt the policy to the failure distribution as the policy evolves, especially to reduce tail failures in autonomous driving tasks.

Method: A failure-informed self-distillation framework where low-reward, low-diversity rollout groups trigger distillation from a frozen round-start teacher with the same parameter scale. The teacher observes hidden future trajectories; supervision guides the student’s next-token outputs while GRPO remains active. The updated policy replaces the teacher for the next round, starting from a Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT checkpoint. The approach is evaluated on 6,019 examples from 150 nuScenes scenes, comparing single-sample planning, mean L2 error (G=4), and persistent failure prevalence.

Result: FIRE-VLA maintains comparable single-sample planning performance, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 m to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%. The main gains arise from mitigating rare severe rollouts rather than uniform improvements across typical trajectories.

Conclusion: Failure-informed self-evolution can adapt the policy distribution to evolving failure modes, achieving tail-error reductions without external teachers; the benefits are strongest for rare, severe failures, suggesting potential applicability to other RL settings with similar failure tails.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves autonomous-driving vision-language-action (VLA) models by evaluating trajectories sampled from the current policy. Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within each rollout group. When all sampled trajectories are poor, this relative signal can rank failures without identifying behavior outside the failed region. We introduce FIRE-VLA, a failure-informed self-evolution framework that converts such unresolved failures into privileged supervision for the next policy. Low-reward, low-diversity groups trigger self-distillation from a frozen round-start copy of the same model. Teacher and student have the same parameter scale, but only the teacher observes the hidden future trajectory. Supervision follows the student's generated prefix and is restricted to answer tokens, while GRPO remains active for every group. The updated policy supplies the teacher for the next round, allowing the routed failure distribution to change with the policy without requiring a larger external teacher. Starting from the same Qwen2.5-VL-3B SFT checkpoint, the comparison matches student rollout and policy-update counts. On 6,019 examples from 150 held-out nuScenes scenes, FIRE-VLA retains comparable single-sample planning, reduces G=4 mean L2 from 1.848 to 1.500 m, and lowers evaluation-persistent failure prevalence from 13.03% to 11.20%. The reduction in mean error arises mainly from rare severe rollouts rather than uniform improvement across ordinary trajectories.

</details>


### [288] [Capstan-driven Continuum Surgical Robot: Design, Modeling, and Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13396)
*Gang Zhang,Yufu Qiu,Junyan Yan,Wenhui Zeng,Wenlong Lu,Shing Shin Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An integrated design-modeling-sensing approach enables real-time shape and force sensing in capstan-driven continuum surgical robots without increasing capstan size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The main bottleneck is obtaining cable tension and shape information inside the confined capstan assembly, which hampers real-time perception and safe manipulation.

Method: Introduce a compliant element in the motor mounting bracket to convert cable reaction forces into measurable micro-deformations; develop a parallel multibody short-thick-beam model to capture shear and multi-cable interactions in unconventional joint configurations; add a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an anchor for stability and accuracy.

Result: Demonstrate real-time tip pose estimation along with contact force and location perception in single- and dual-segment capstan-driven continuum robots, validating the integrated perception framework.

Conclusion: Cable tension feedback can be obtained without enlarging the capstan architecture, making integrated perception feasible for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots.

Abstract: Shape and force sensing have long been critical bottlenecks in the development of compact capstan-driven continuum surgical robots, primarily due to the difficulty of obtaining cable tension information within the confined capstan assembly. To overcome these challenges, this paper presents an integrated design-modeling-sensing approach based on the concept of actuation-perception co-design. A compliant element is introduced into the motor mounting bracket of the drive system, enabling micro-deformation under the cable reaction force and thereby allowing real-time cable tension measurement without occupying the compact capstan space. To address the modeling complexity arising from unconventional joint configurations introduced by the spatial cable routing strategy, a parallel computation framework based on a multibody short-thick-beam model is proposed, which captures shear effects in short beam segments and synergistic multi-cable interactions while achieving real-time performance. Building on this framework, stable shape and force sensing is achieved by incorporating a proximal multi-axis force/torque sensor as an additional measurement anchor. Following this design-modeling-sensing framework, capstan-driven continuum surgical robots with single- and dual-segment configurations are developed. Experimental results validate the proposed framework in both single- and dual-segment continuum robots, demonstrating real-time tip pose estimation together with contact force and location perception. By enabling cable tension feedback without compromising the compact capstan architecture, the proposed framework makes integrated perception feasible for capstan-driven continuum surgical robots.

</details>


### [289] [Deliberate Practice: Learning Robot Skills under a Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13415)
*Shivam Vats,Sudarshan Harithas,Mete Tuluhan Akbulut,Arvind Raghunathan,George Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DP proposes a budget-optimal active skill-learning method for sequential robotic tasks under a fixed practice budget, using a bilinear program to allocate practice time across skills to maximize expected cumulative reward; validated on simulated and real long-horizon manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficiently utilize a limited practice budget to acquire useful policies for long-horizon planning; existing approaches struggle with combinatorial skill plans and budget constraints.

Method: Introduce Deliberate Practice (DP). Estimate skill mastery time and cumulative reward of task plans; formulate budget allocation as a bilinear program; solve with standard solvers; supports exact optimization.

Result: Experiments (simulation and real-world) show DP allocates practice time optimally and improves long-horizon planning performance.

Conclusion: DP achieves provably budget-optimal allocation for skill practice and demonstrates practical gains; the bilinear formulation enables exact optimization within practical budgets.

Abstract: We consider the problem of autonomously learning robot skills under a limited practice budget for sequential tasks. We propose an active skill learning algorithm, \emph{Deliberate Practice (DP)}, that computes a provably \emph{budget-optimal} allocation---practicing skills that maximize expected cumulative reward while being learnable within the budget. DP estimates both the time needed to master skills and the cumulative reward of the task plans that the skills unlock. Computing a budget-optimal allocation is challenging as it requires reasoning about combinatorially many skill plans over a large practice budget. Our key contribution is a bilinear program that can compute this exactly using off-the-shelf solvers. Through simulated and real-world experiments on long-horizon manipulation tasks, we show that our approach allows robots to optimally use limited practice time to acquire useful policies and improve long-horizon planning.

</details>


### [290] [Attention from Action, for Action: Emergent Visual Bottlenecks for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13422)
*Zheyu Zhuang,Ruiyu Wang,Nick Heppert,Johannes Fabian Hahn,Abhinav Valada,Florian T. Pokorny,Danica Kragic*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Seeker learns task- and state-conditioned ROIs from action supervision to produce progression-aware visual crops, improving data-efficient visuomotor learning over label-free and action-derived baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Label-free ROI cropping for visuomotor control often misaligns with when task evidence lies away from the end-effector or changes with task progress; a method that derives ROI from action can be fragile without progress awareness.

Method: Starting from frozen DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query with visual evidence gathered along action trajectories to produce progression-aware ROIs, which are used for RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering.

Result: Seeker yields improved data efficiency and robustness across simulation and real robots, outperforming no-crop, augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines; in real robots, in-domain success rises from 48.3% to 76.7% and lighting/background robustness from 20.0% to 60.0%.

Conclusion: Progression-aware, action-supervised ROI learning can overcome limitations of fixed, end-effector–centered crops, enabling more reliable, data-efficient visuomotor control across varying conditions.

Abstract: Visual bottlenecks that focus policy inputs on regions of interest (ROIs) can improve data-efficient visuomotor learning by separating where to look from how to act. Many ROI interfaces rely on external spatial labels, such as gaze, object classes, or affordance annotations. Label-free alternatives often derive crops from trajectories by detecting gripper or motion events and centering a fixed crop at the projected end-effector. Such action-derived crops are useful spatial priors that require no additional labels, but they encode fixed choices about event timing, proxy points, and crop scale. When the visual evidence needed for control lies away from the end-effector or changes continuously with task progress, these crops can become misaligned. We propose Seeker, a task- and state-conditioned readout that learns attention from action. Starting from frozen DINOv3 features, Seeker iteratively updates a query with gathered visual evidence, producing progression-aware ROIs solely from action supervision. The learned ROI serves as a spatial interface for RGB cropping, mask-guided background augmentation, and point-cloud filtering. In simulation and the real world, Seeker improves data efficiency and robustness over no-crop, augmentation, and action-derived crop baselines. On real robots, Seeker raises average in-domain success from the best baseline's 48.3% to 76.7% and success under lighting/background shifts from 20.0% to 60.0%.

</details>


### [291] [ContactGuard: Pre-Contact Execution Monitoring with Action-Conditioned Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13438)
*Gehan Zheng,Matthew Johnson-Roberson,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ContactGuard is a pre-contact execution monitor for chunked visuomotor policies that predicts short-horizon outcomes in latent visual space and aborts imminent failures before contact, using unlabelled trajectories to train a latent model and a small labelled set for a lightweight failure probe; it transfers to real robots without modifying the policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In contact-rich manipulation, failures often occur at the moment of contact, which is hard to detect in time with wrist-camera setups. Early, pre-contact failure detection is needed to prevent pushing, slipping, or disturbing objects and to enable safer, more reliable behavior.

Method: Train a latent world model from unlabelled robot trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions (avoiding pixel-level video prediction). Train a lightweight failure probe from a small labelled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, anchor predictions before contact, roll the model forward under the policy's actions, and verify the predicted post-contact latent; abort if failure is predicted.

Result: Across real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, ContactGuard predicts failure more accurately than direct and corrupted-action ablations and transfers to live robot as a pre-contact abort signal without modifying the underlying policy.

Conclusion: A pre-contact execution monitor can effectively anticipate and abort imminent contact failures in chunked visuomotor policies, improving safety and reliability in real-world manipulation without requiring changes to the learned policy.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation failures are often detected only after the robot has committed to contact. This is especially limiting in wrist-camera setups: close gripper--object views help observe contact, but a poor approach may already push, miss, slip, or disturb the object before conventional detectors react. We introduce \emph{ContactGuard}, a pre-contact execution monitor for chunked visuomotor policies. Given the policy's planned action chunk, ContactGuard predicts its short-horizon consequence in latent visual space and aborts if the predicted future latent indicates likely failure.
