Its latent world model is trained from unlabelled robot trajectories to predict compact multi-view visual embeddings under planned actions, avoiding pixel-level video prediction. A lightweight failure probe is then trained from a small labelled set of pre-contact clips. At deployment, ContactGuard anchors prediction before an imminent contact event, rolls the model forward under the policy's own actions, and verifies the predicted post-contact latent. Across real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, ContactGuard predicts failure more accurately than direct and corrupted-action ablations, and transfers to live robot as a pre-contact abort signal without modifying the underlying policy.

</details>


### [292] [Mind the Context: Continual Learning of Socially Appropriate Robot Actions via Environmental-Social Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13448)
*Rafal Robert Karpinski,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Nikhil Churamani,Yiming Luo,Maartje M. A. de Graaf,Davide Dell'Anna,Hatice Gunes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) for domain-incremental continual learning in social robotics, disentangling environmental and social cues to learn action appropriateness across indoor contexts, using replay to mitigate forgetting; outperforms baselines; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots operate in diverse, uncontrolled environments where environmental and social cues jointly determine appropriate actions. Domain-incremental learning is required to adapt to unseen contexts while retaining past knowledge, which current methods do not address with explicit cue disentanglement.

Method: EDD introduces a dual-branch architecture that explicitly separates environmental knowledge from social-agent related knowledge. It employs replay-based rehearsal to mitigate catastrophic forgetting during continual learning across domains (e.g., living room, meeting room, office, hallway). The framework is evaluated on learning action appropriateness (e.g., cleaning, serving, starting a conversation) across several indoor domains, with ablations on disentanglement strategies and domain ordering.

Result: EDD outperforms several state-of-the-art continual learning baselines. Ablation studies show the impact of different disentanglement strategies and sensitivity to domain ordering.

Conclusion: Explicitly disentangling environmental and social-agent cues via EDD improves domain-incremental continual learning for socially appropriate robot actions, with robust performance and insights from ablations; code is publicly available.

Abstract: Social robots are expected to operate across diverse environments, where similar arrangements can imply different socially appropriate actions, e.g., starting a conversation may be acceptable in a crowded home but disruptive in an office meeting. Because such norms and environments cannot all be anticipated in advance, robots require continual learning (CL) to adapt from sequential experience while retaining previously acquired knowledge. Prior work has studied CL for generating socially appropriate robot actions, but it has not addressed domain-incremental settings in which the robot incrementally encounters diverse contexts (e.g., living room, meeting room, office, hallway), where both environmental (e.g., whether the space is open or cluttered with furniture) and social cues (e.g., how people or other agents are positioned around the robot) jointly shape the appropriateness of robot actions. We address this gap with the Explicit Disentanglement Dual-Branch (EDD) framework. EDD explicitly separates environmental and social-agent related knowledge and uses replay-based rehearsal to mitigate forgetting while learning the appropriateness of robot actions (e.g., cleaning, serving, starting a conversation) across several indoor domains. Experiments show that EDD outperforms several state-of-the-art baselines, and ablation studies further evaluate different disentanglement strategies and the sensitivity to domain ordering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Cambridge-AFAR/Mind-the-Context.git.

</details>


### [293] [Decoding Task Progress from VLA Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13474)
*Atiksh Bhardwaj,Edward Weiyi Duan,Prithwish Dan,Wei-Chiu Ma,Preston Culbertson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Linear probes can extract task progress from the residual stream of a VLA backbone (π_{0.5}) even before task-specific training; this signal can monitor progress and serve as a label-free OOD detector, but does not enable steering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what VLAs internally represent and to develop lightweight, runtime monitoring tools for deployed visuomotor policies by probing internal representations for semantically meaningful quantities.

Method: Probe the residual stream of π_{0.5} with a linear classifier to read task progress; test generalization to unseen tasks; analyze effect of language prompts via multi-prompt data; evaluate as an unsupervised OOD detector against state-of-the-art methods.

Result: Task progress is linearly readable from activations; the signal exists in the pretrained backbone before task-specific data; a single linear probe generalizes to new tasks and responds to language counterfactuals with multi-prompt data; it does not enable meaningful policy steering; the probe can function as a label-free OOD detector competitive with SOTA methods.

Conclusion: VLAs encode rich, linearly decodable semantic quantities like task progress; simple probes for reading these signals provide an interpretable, low-overhead path for monitoring deployed visuomotor policies.

Abstract: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) are moving rapidly towards deployment as general-purpose manipulation policies, but we currently lack basic tools for understanding what these models represent internally or for monitoring them at runtime. Leveraging ideas from mechanistic interpretability, we probe the residual stream of $π_{0.5}$ and find that task progress, the normalized time remaining in a trajectory, is linearly readable from the activations. We find that this signal is present in the pretrained PaliGemma backbone prior to training on any robot-specific data. A single linear probe generalizes to unseen tasks and varies under language counterfactuals when trained on multi-prompt data, but does not enable meaningful steering of the policy. These properties make the signal directly useful for instrumenting deployed VLAs. We use the probe as a simple label-free OOD detector, which detects stalled task progress, and find it competitive with state-of-the-art methods. Our results suggest that VLAs have rich, linearly readable internal representations of semantic quantities like task progress, and that learning to read these signals offers a lightweight, interpretable path toward monitoring deployed visuomotor policies.

</details>


### [294] [A Browser-Native Digital Test Range for Benchmarking 4D Ocean-Glider Planning Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13511)
*Edward Holmberg,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A browser-native, four-dimensional OSSE test range enables reproducible evaluation of ocean-glider planners without field deployments, using a common plan-to-observation contract and Pyodide-based simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scarcity of in-situ resources and deployable vehicles for repeated planner evaluation; need reproducible, scalable, and dataset-ready OSSEs that unify vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions across manual routes and learned planners.

Method: A browser-based test range converts a region into a four-dimensional experiment: regional domain, bathymetry, time/depth forcing, science objectives, optional task decomposition, route specification, current-advected execution, observation generation, and scoring. It enforces a common contract between plan and observations, supports imported classical or learned planners, and exports artifacts as dataset-ready records. An OSSE evaluates five classical planners across two episodes, three deterministic seeds, and a calibrated 60-hour horizon (54 missions completed). A field deployment provides audit data. GliderFlight 1.2.0 is ported to browser via Pyodide/WebAssembly for native-to-browser parity, enabling high-fidelity multi-tier simulation.

Result: All 54 missions completed and recovered without hard violations. Planner rankings and dive-policy effects exposed operational–scientific tradeoffs. Public deployment supplied field plausibility. GliderFlight achieves browser parity, enabling high-fidelity simulation. Exported artifacts are suitable for dataset-ready analyses and pre-deployment experimentation.

Conclusion: The framework yields a scientifically traceable and component-qualified space for mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation, enabling reproducible OSSEs and safer, more efficient planner evaluation without field deployments.

Abstract: Repeated in-situ evaluation of ocean-glider planners requires scarce vehicles, operators, deployment and recovery resources, and ocean conditions that cannot be reset for competing algorithms. We present a guided, installation-free browser-native digital test range that transforms a selected region into a reproducible four-dimensional experiment. The system leads users from regional domain selection through mission-scoped bathymetry, time/depth forcing, science objectives, optional task decomposition, route specification, current-advected execution, observation generation, and scoring. Its primary contribution is a common plan-to-observation contract unifying vehicle, sensing, and evaluator assumptions across manual routes, transparent built-in algorithms, and imported classical or learned-planner outputs, while exported artifacts form dataset-ready records. A controlled Observing System Simulation Experiment (OSSE) evaluates five classical planners in two episodes, three deterministic seeds, and a calibrated 60-hour horizon. All 54 missions completed and recovered without hard violations, while planner rankings and dive-policy effects revealed operational-scientific tradeoffs. An authentic public deployment supplied a field-referenced audit to scope current kinematic boundaries. Separately, source-locked GliderFlight 1.2.0 achieved native-to-browser parity through Pyodide/WebAssembly, establishing a pathway for high-fidelity multi-tier simulation. The resulting operational space is scientifically traceable and component-qualified for mission-scale pre-deployment experimentation.

</details>


### [295] [HumanTracker: Towards Comprehensive and Human-Aligned Motion Tracking Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13555)
*Dairu Liu,Zekun Qi,Jiayu Zeng,Ruixi Yu,Yu Guan,Yintianrun Zhang,Xuchuan Chen,Sikai Liang,Zekai Li,Chenghuai Lin,Xinqiang Yu,Wenyao Zhang,He Wang,Li Yi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces HumanTracker benchmark and HumanScore metric to evaluate humanoid tracking by perceptual alignment and contact stability; large dataset and pairwise preferences show better correlation with human judgments than traditional kinematic metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-frame kinematic pose errors miss critical perceptual and physical artifacts (unstable support, mis-timed contacts) and existing test suites lack diversity for stress-testing long-horizon, contact-rich behaviors.

Method: Assembles ~153 hours of optical motion trajectories from professional performers, organized into four motion families with textual labels for fine-grained diagnosis. Develops HumanScore, a preference-aligned metric trained on 12K motion pairs (24K motions).

Result: HumanScore better predicts human preferences across representative trackers and uncovers contact and stability failures that kinematic metrics overlook.

Conclusion: Perceptually aligned, scalable evaluation of humanoid tracking is feasible and beneficial, enabling more accurate assessment and diagnosis of tracking systems.

Abstract: Humanoid motion tracking is central to teleoperation and whole-body imitation, yet evaluation often disagrees with what people perceive in videos. Kinematic errors average per-frame pose differences but miss the physical artifacts that matter most, particularly unstable support and incorrect contacts such as foot skating and mistimed touch-downs. Meanwhile, widely used test suites are small and lack the diversity needed to stress contact-rich, long-horizon behaviors. We introduce HumanTracker to make humanoid tracking evaluation both perceptually aligned and scalable. The HumanTracker benchmark contains approximately 153 hours of optical motion trajectories from multiple professional performers, organized into four motion families with text labels for fine-grained diagnosis. We further propose HumanScore, a preference-aligned metric trained on 12K motion pairs containing 24K motions. Across representative state-of-the-art trackers, HumanScore better predicts human preferences and reveals contact and stability failures that kinematic metrics often miss.

</details>
